Public benchmarks guide model selection, post-training rewards and claims of progress in artificial intelligence, yet leaderboards report aggregate scores without verifying whether each task's prompt, environment and grader agree on what counts as success. How often that agreement fails in contemporary evaluations, and in which direction confirmed defects move scores, has remained unmeasured across grading designs. Here we synthesize task-level quality reviews of 749 tasks across eight frontier benchmarks and 767 public model evaluations spanning closed-form question answering, document rubrics and agentic execution to assess whether prompts, environments and graders align. Task validity stratifies sharply by grading architecture. Closed-form benchmarks revised after public error reporting reach 92.5% verified validity, compared with 29.3% across six multi-step suites that nevertheless already supply 161 verified complex tasks. Rubric suites fail through mismatches between prompts and reference checklists, whereas execution harnesses add incomplete state assertions, shared-channel leaks and grading-time collisions. Across confirmed defects, 71.7% withhold credit from compliant solutions while 28.3% permit shortcut passes, indicating that raw leaderboard ceilings understate frontier capability on many practical tasks. Grading committed patches inside a fresh container cuts confirmed runtime bypasses from 14.7% in shared containers to 2.7% (a 5.5x drop, and 0% in read-only or static suites), showing how targeted maintenance restores existing benchmarks at low cost. As the initial milestone of the long-term Benchmarks' Last Exam initiative, this synthesis launches sustained expansion across wider benchmark coverage and deeper evaluation-quality dimensions so that pursuing leaderboard gains directly advances genuine model capability.


### arXiv 2611.27618 - Potential emergence in LLM agents: a statistical physics perspective

- categories: cs.LG, cond-mat.stat-mech, cs.AI, cs.CL, nlin.AO
- published: 2026-11-20T13:46:24Z
- authors: Zhuo-Yang Song, Hua Xing Zhu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27618

There has been significant recent interest in understanding the behaviour of LLM agents from the perspective of human preferences or corpus statistics. In this paper we propose agent states as intermediate variables through which post-training can be measured and predicted. From a statistical physics perspective, we study the emergence of a global potential which governs the transition of state in LLM agent reasoning. We introduce a partition function to model the ideal KL-regularised transition distribution and track potential structure, mean reward and state entropy. We demonstrate our approach in two Qwen3.5-9B tasks: safety-test selection and physics-reasoning auditing. We observe that the potential structure strengthens during post-training as predicted from our framework, while joint changes in reward and state entropy provide consistency evidence. States thus provide a quantitative description of how post-training organises agent behaviour.


### arXiv 2611.27601 - PromptTTSD: Controllable Text to Spoken Dialogue Synthesis with Open-Ended Text Descriptions

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T13:29:30Z
- authors: Sulong Yang, Zhi Zeng, Xiupeng Wu, Juewen Hu, Pring Wong, Shuwu Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27601

Spoken dialogue synthesis aims to generate multi-turn conversational speech from dialogue scripts while maintaining speaker consistency and contextual appropriateness. However, existing models largely lack fine-grained controllability, which is critical for capturing the dynamic, context-dependent style shifts in natural conversations. While prompt-based TTS has enabled such control for single-speaker speech, existing datasets heavily rely on predefined tags, which fundamentally limits their capacity to capture open-ended styles. To address this gap, we propose a scalable pipeline for extracting conversational speech from multi-narrator audiobooks, featuring an improved two-stage alignment algorithm to preserve dialogue continuity, paired with LLM-based extraction of style descriptions directly from narrative text. We release PromptDialog, a large-scale multi-speaker conversational speech dataset comprising 8,383 hours of Chinese speech and 120 hours of English speech, with open-ended style descriptions for 79% of utterances. We further introduce PromptTTSD, a spoken dialogue synthesis model built via a prompt-aware fine-tuning strategy that integrates style prompts into text-speech interleaved sequences. PromptTTSD is an open-source spoken dialogue synthesis model that supports both zero-shot voice cloning and natural language style control. Experiments demonstrate that PromptTTSD not only maintains strong fundamental synthesis capabilities but also achieves competitive open-ended style control compared with proprietary models, and substantially improves contextual appropriateness in multi-turn dialogues.


### arXiv 2611.27576 - Unlocking Heterogeneous Accelerator Flexibility in AI Data Centers: Sizing and Workload Allocation

- categories: eess.SY, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T13:12:31Z
- authors: Chenghao Lyu, Ying Yu, Wangkun Xu, Fei Teng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27576

AI data centers are an emerging electricity demand, but limited local grid capacity could delay their connection and expansion. While existing operational strategies consider spatio-temporal workload flexibility, few studies address the strategic sizing and allocation of diverse accelerator archetypes with different capital and energy-efficiency trade-offs. This paper unlocks the flexibility potential of heterogeneous accelerators by jointly sizing and allocating different AI workloads (training, delay-tolerant batch work, and online inference requests) across sites and hours to meet the time-varying grid constraint at minimum cost. Workload-specific throughput and energy coefficients link these decisions to facility power. In a representative two-site case, heterogeneous sizing reduces daily cost by 6.5% relative to spatio-temporal scheduling with fixed capacity. When evaluated using workload-specific performance, a capacity design based on workload-averaged hardware coefficients costs 7.7% more than the proposed design. The proposed design also eliminates the envelope violations of an unconstrained design at a 0.25\% daily cost increase. These results show how hardware heterogeneity and workload flexibility jointly shape economical, grid-compliant capacity plans.


### arXiv 2611.27559 - Can Language Models See the Circuit in Pixelated RFIC Passives?

- categories: cs.AI, cs.AR, physics.app-ph
- published: 2026-11-20T12:55:27Z
- authors: Jonathan Zhou, Kaushik Sengupta
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27559

The emergence of inverse-designed, pixelated passives for microwave and millimeter-wave applications has been a major development in the field of RFIC design. These devices, which appear to be QR-code-like in structure, exist in a generalizable, high-dimensional design space that includes conventional passive topologies. However, these structures are naturally unintuitive for designers, and failures or deviations in performance are difficult to debug. In this work, we ask whether a frontier language model can understand, predict, and edit the responses and layout structure of pixelated passives. The agent, run with all tools disabled, is first asked to read layouts on 18x18 and 27x27 tile grids and, in a single reply, predict the input impedance and loss of the structure from 20-100 GHz. On eight GA-synthesized layouts and five random fills, the predicted Smith-chart locus follows full-wave simulation results closely, with a systematic shift that places features about 16% lower in frequency than EM. On multipath loop structures it identifies notches successfully but shows a smaller frequency bias, about half that of an ideal transmission-line model that ignores bends. The agent is then asked to debug seven designs that fail their specification, and given a budget of five EM simulations and at most twelve changed tiles, it is able to improve all seven and bring five designs within specification. These experiments suggest language models can serve as an interpretability and debugging tool for traditionally non-intuitive inverse-designed passives.


### arXiv 2611.27556 - Bringing AI Assistance to the Beznau Nuclear Power Plant: A Hierarchical System of LLM Agents for Reactor Monitoring

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T12:52:34Z
- authors: Wenqi Zhou, Leonardo Baldo, Edwin Kolbe, Manuel Arias Chao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27556

Beznau Unit 1 in Switzerland, in operation since 1969, is the most experienced nuclear reactor still running worldwide. Five decades of incremental modernization have left its monitoring systems, alarm logs, variable catalogs, technical specifications and investigation reports in separate silos that were never designed to be queried together. As a result, investigating a core instrumentation anomaly means slow, manual gathering of knowledge scattered across databases, documents, and experienced colleagues. We present a hierarchical multi-agent assistant built on large language models (LLMs) to support these investigations and reduce the need for tedious manual searches in SAP systems. An orchestrating agent breaks down an engineer's question and hands the parts to specialized sub-agents, each with read-only access to one source. Domain expertise is written as natural-language procedures that plant engineers can read, review and revise. The system is strictly advisory, with every answer traced back to its sources. We also report organizational lessons from introducing agentic AI into a culture built on safety, traceability and conservative change. Beyond the technical architecture, the Beznau case study examines how an assistant can be introduced into a setting where evidence must be traceable, changes carefully reviewed, and operational decisions retained by plant personnel, paving the way for the responsible use of agentic AI in industrial plant monitoring.


### arXiv 2611.27554 - Vega-Fafnir: Is Greedy Decoding the Same Decoding? Where Rounding Noise in Speculative Verification Comes From and What Removing It Costs

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T12:51:06Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Navin Kumar R
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27554

Serving engines treat the ways of running a greedy language model as interchangeable: cached or not, padded or not, alone or batched, one token or several verified at once (speculative decoding). In finite precision they round differently. We measure how often the greedy text of Qwen3-0.6B (bfloat16, 128 new tokens, GSM8K and ARC prompts) differs from a reference that decodes one token per pass. Verifying 4 guessed tokens per pass changes 60% of 150 prompts and padding changes 68%; every first divergence has a top-1 minus top-2 logit gap of 0.375 or less. A split of the noise shows that cache entries written by multi-token passes are about as noisy as the multi-token pass itself. A margin gate (fall back to one token when the gap is below a bound calibrated on separate GSM8K prompts) whose fallback reads that cache does not help: 58 of 100 prompts differ, against 61 of 100 for plain verification. Rebuilding the cache with one-token passes leaves 0 of 100 different but costs 7.2 model passes per token, against 1.0 for the reference. Fixed-width decoding makes drafted and plain decoding identical (0 of 100 prompts differ) at 0.75 passes per token. On shifted MMLU and HellaSwag prompts the rebuild gate leaves 0 of 60 different, plain verification 48 of 60. On 138 further and shifted prompts the bound was exceeded at 0.6% of positions and missed 0 of 309 flips. One GPU, one drafter, small models.


### arXiv 2611.27550 - Altair-Nidhogg: Which Model-Merging Theories Hold for a One-Pass Decision Encoder? Soups, Task Arithmetic, TIES and DARE Against Joint Training and Prediction Ensembling

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T12:47:06Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Navin Kumar R, Akhil P
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27550

Laya is a small model that answers a multiple-choice question in one pass: each option has a name and a written description. A new skill costs a training run, so we test whether published recipes for merging fine-tuned models (weight averaging, task arithmetic, TIES, DARE) work for it, and whether the theories behind them hold. All 156 models start from one released checkpoint: four plain fine-tunes, five specialists (each adds one kind of training row), joint fine-tunes on all rows, and prediction ensembles. Items are held-out, synthetic and LLM-labelled. In-family, each skill is scored on new items of its own kind, and "kept" is the share of a specialist's gain over the plain fine-tune. Averaging four specialists keeps 52% [38, 64] with +1.7 points of general accuracy; adding the full vectors keeps 42% [3, 63] with -9.0; TIES, which trims and resolves sign conflicts, keeps 64% [50, 76] with +1.5; joint fine-tunes, which see all the rows, keep 120% [104, 162] and 116% [104, 149]. DARE does not repair the full sum (-0.4 points), and ensembles of the models' predictions keep 26% to 54% depending on how they are combined. Swapping the seed of one specialist moves the average by up to 8 points (23 for the full sum). Of 10 predictions fixed in advance, 1 held, 1 held partly, 7 failed and 1 could not be tested. Out-of-family (benchmark questions, code-mixed tickets), the four-way average, joint models and ensemble stay within 2.6 points of the plain fine-tune, but full-vector sums lose up to 7.7. Labels are LLM-made, data synthetic, one base model.


### arXiv 2611.27543 - On the Limits of Observability for Evaluating Coverage in Machine Unlearning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T12:38:56Z
- authors: Ziluowen Luo, Jun Yin, Songyang Liu, Ji Qi, Chuang Hu, Kyrie You Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27543

Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of any requested subset of training data while preserving utility on the remaining data. Empirical evaluations measure predictive accuracy and infer training-set membership (MIAs) from model outputs or intermediate activations. However, by focusing on selected forget sets, these evaluations can overlook Coverage, the range of requests a method can fulfill. To assess this coverage, we first conduct a stress test of observability with 502 retrained models, finding that hidden embeddings reveal known deletions missed in predictions. To characterize capacity limits on coverage, we show that distinguishing all $n$ singleton deletions with linear scores can require a shared representation of rank $Ω(\log n)$. We further extend the bound to arbitrary subsets and show when partial deletion can appear complete. Building on this analysis, we propose CEO, a framework for evaluating unlearning coverage under limited observability. Its compositional color-shape benchmark makes forgetting and retained utility measurable from the full output distribution and provides a leave-one-out retraining oracle for each target. From a fixed number of oracle observables, we estimate empirical target coverage, the fraction of targets reliably flagged when deleted and rarely flagged under any other deletions. To recover missed deletions, CEO further expands each observable representation into high-dimensional activation space with the frozen encoder of a SAE trained on the original model. Extensive experiments of 20 baselines shows that even on this simple synthetic task, only retraining from scratch covers all deletion targets.


### arXiv 2611.27529 - Fix Where It Fails: Root-Cause-Guided Agentic Harness Optimization for Scene Graph Generation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T12:26:00Z
- authors: Jee Eun Kim, Donghyeon Choi, Sangjune An, Hae Beom Lee, Kyong Hwan Jin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27529

Scene graph generation (SGG) aims to detect objects in an image and predict the relationships between them. The standard PredCls, SGCls, and SGDet settings show how much performance drops without ground-truth boxes and labels, but not where or why a model fails. Knowing this would allow fixing a model where it fails, without retraining. We propose RoHa-SGG (Root-cause-guided Harness optimization for SGG), which extends agentic harness optimization to SGG. It optimizes the harness, the inference code around a frozen SGG model. RoHa-SGG has two main LLM agents. A root-cause analysis (RCA) agent finds the components where the model fails and diagnoses why, and a proposer agent searches for a harness that fixes these causes. We evaluate RoHa-SGG on Visual Genome and PSG with representative two-stage, one-stage, and multimodal-LLM SGG models. The analysis shows that the components where ground-truth triplets are lost differ across models and datasets. RoHa-SGG improves every metric on all five model-dataset combinations. Graph-constrained R@100 and mR@100 rise by up to 15.8 and 26.4 points for the multimodal LLM and by up to 3.6 and 10.1 points for the two-stage and one-stage models. With root-cause guidance, the harness search reaches the final score of an unguided search up to three times faster.


### arXiv 2611.27521 - PaMuk-Bench: A Parallel Multilingual Multitask Benchmark for Vision-Language Tasks

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T12:13:25Z
- authors: Silviu-Florin Gheorghe, Radu-Stefan Popa, Adriana Valentina Costache, Adrian-Gabriel Chifu, Alessia Amelio, Paul Irofti
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27521

While vision-language models (VLMs) currently obtain notable results for highly-studied languages, e.g. English, they exhibit significant performance drops in certain low-resource languages, especially due to the lack of annotated datasets. To this end, we propose PaMuk-Bench, a novel parallel multilingual multitask benchmark for vision-language tasks. Our benchmark includes a set of over 10K curated English prompts that are translated into six languages: Arabic, Danish, French, Hindi, Italian, Romanian. Parallel translations, which are obtained via a semi-automatic process, along with accompanying generated and retrieved images enable the comparative development and evaluation of VLMs across a wide range of tasks: text-to-image generation, image captioning, content-based image retrieval, image generation difficulty estimation, image retrieval difficulty estimation. We empirically show that zero-shot English and multilingual pre-trained VLMs compare consistently worse than their fine-tuned counterparts, confirming that PaMuk-Bench is a valuable resource that can drastically reduce the language gap across a variety of VLM architectures and tasks. PaMuk-Bench is available at https://github.com/ghesil/pamuk_bench.


### arXiv 2611.27516 - PhiJEPA: Denoising Latent Predictions for Visual Representation Learning

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, stat.ML
- published: 2026-11-20T12:07:30Z
- authors: Makoto Yamada, Yao-Hung Hubert Tsai
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27516

Can a predictor trained to infer hidden image regions still help a world model plan when part of an object disappears from view and the visible context is blurred? We introduce PhiJEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that adapts PhiNet's two-stage prediction to masked Transformers. From sparse, photometrically corrupted context, a Transformer block predicts hidden tokens of an independently corrupted teacher view. A token-wise MLP then maps these predictions to clean teacher tokens. Controlled ablations show that the combined objective improves dense features over the tested one-stage alternatives. Pre-trained on ImageNet-1K with $5.6\times10^{19}$ training FLOPs, PhiJEPA achieves 69.3 mIoU on linear PASCAL VOC segmentation, exceeding the evaluated I-JEPA and DSeq-JEPA checkpoints in segmentation accuracy, with 25\% fewer training FLOPs than I-JEPA. It also improves hidden-region segmentation over Simple I-JEPA (49.1 vs.\ 44.3 mIoU). As an application, we use the frozen encoder in DINO-WM on PushT, and reuse the pre-training predictor, which JEPAs usually discard, to complete occluded tokens without fine-tuning. In open-loop planning with simultaneous object occlusion and Gaussian blur ($σ=2$ pixels), predictor completion raises success from 33\% to 65\%, compared with 25\% for Simple I-JEPA with its predictor and 40\% for iBOT with pixel inpainting. Together, these results support two-stage latent prediction as an efficient approach to learning visual representations that remain useful under photometric degradation and support completion under partial observation.


### arXiv 2611.27509 - Self-Supervised Continuous-Time Depth Learning and Ego-Motion Estimation from Asynchronous Events

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-20T11:57:00Z
- authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Kumar Ghosh, Hadfield Simon
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27509

Event cameras register brightness changes pixel by pixel with microsecond precision, whereas the dense depth that could supervise them is costly to acquire and exists only at isolated instants. Condensing the stream into frames eases learning, but at the expense of the very timing that sets event sensing apart. We propose a self-supervised approach in which each event is assigned its own depth in continuous time, and the camera motion is recovered from the same observations. Supervision arises from geometry: lifted into 3D by its predicted depth and displaced by the camera motion at the instant it fired, an event should coincide with others produced by the same edge, so any misalignment exposes an error in depth or motion. Differentiable rendering then turns these per-event estimates into dense depth maps at target times within each window, faithful to the events behind them. On MVSEC, the approach outperforms prior event-only self-supervised methods in most sequences and depth ranges, with the widest margins at night, and recovers camera rotation more accurately than an earlier unsupervised event-based method. Ablations isolate the roles of local spatiotemporal context, the temporal backbone, and the alignment objective. These results position motion-compensated event alignment as a principled source of supervision for continuous-time depth and ego-motion estimation.


### arXiv 2611.27504 - The Assistant Persona Differs Across Languages in Activation Space

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:52:33Z
- authors: Daniel Yoo, Naama Rozen, Evan Harris
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27504

Large language models behave differently across languages, and are noticeably less safe in lower-resource languages. This gap is well established behaviorally, but not mechanistically. Directions in activation space, including the assistant persona direction, are almost always extracted from English and assumed to generalize across languages. We extract the assistant persona direction in 31 languages in Qwen3-32B, and find that the resulting axes differ substantially: their cosine similarity to the English axis ranges from 0.378 to 0.911. Moreover, each language's assistant persona direction and its magnitude are correlated with the language's resource level. We also find that the model's default assistant state is farther from the helpful assistant pole in lower-resource languages. Finally, steering against a language's own axis amplifies persona-based jailbreak more than steering against the English axis. These results indicate that the model does not carry a single assistant representation across languages. More broadly, the assistant persona may need to be an explicit target of multilingual alignment.


### arXiv 2611.27500 - Turnout: Adaptive Attention Switching for Efficient Reasoning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:49:13Z
- authors: Anna Kuzina, Mart van Baalen, Paul N. Whatmough, Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27500

Post-hoc transformer linearization replaces the key-value cache of full attention, which grows linearly with generation length, with a fixed size state. This change reduces memory consumption but often hurts accuracy. In reasoning tasks, which require long generations, this effect is especially pronounced. In this work we introduce Turnout, a method that mitigates accuracy loss as a result of linearization, while maintaining its memory-reduction benefits. We apply post-hoc linearization to a pretrained full-attention model, training a linear path through distillation while leaving the original full-attention path unchanged. During response generation, the resulting hybrid model retains both paths and uses a learned policy to control when each layer switches from full to linear attention. We train the policy with reinforcement learning using a reward that balances answer correctness and memory use. Empirical evaluation of our method shows that the learned adaptive policy achieves a better accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier on reasoning tasks compared to fixed switching and static hybrid baselines.


### arXiv 2611.27495 - Trained on Harm, Still Refusing: A Training-Time Defense for Code Generation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:40:10Z
- authors: Miaoqing Tian, Ruohan Li, Qian Lu, Haipeng Qu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27495

Fine-tuning enables safety-aligned code language models to adapt to diverse downstream tasks. However, training on untrusted data can severely undermine their ability to refuse harmful requests. Notably, we find that models comply with harmful requests even though harmfulness information remains highly decodable from their internal representations. Moreover, an initial preference for refusal does not necessarily translate into sustained refusal behavior throughout generation. Together, these findings reveal a critical gap between recognizing harmful requests and consistently refusing to fulfill them. To address this gap, we introduce SAFEHOLD, a novel provider-enforced defense framework that preserves safety alignment during harmful fine-tuning by treating refusal as a sustained generation process rather than a single response decision. Specifically, SAFEHOLD preserves the aligned model's full-vocabulary response distribution at the initial generation position through decision-boundary anchoring, while supervising the complete refusal sequence through refusal-path reinforcement. We conduct extensive experiments evaluating safety, utility, and internal mechanisms. SAFEHOLD reduces average ASR by 72.03 percentage points relative to undefended fine-tuning, outperforming five SOTA defenses by 24.32 to 70.87 percentage points. Meanwhile, it improves average HumanEval pass@1 by 10.24 percentage points over undefended fine-tuning, while maintaining an average XSTest over-refusal rate of only 2.73 percent. Overall, these results demonstrate that SAFEHOLD effectively mitigates safety degradation during harmful fine-tuning while preserving benign-task utility.


### arXiv 2611.27492 - Learning Unknown Oscillators From Bandit Feedback

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:37:16Z
- authors: Priyank Jayraj, Poonam Goyal, Navneet Goyal
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27492

We study semi-bandits driven by unknown oscillators. Each round, a learner selects two of four reward channels and observes their individual rewards. Frequency errors change future reward rankings and the resulting measurements. We show when committing to one fitted oscillator lets its greedy choices expose prediction errors. Our controller bounds reward loss (regret) over a continuous confidence set and holds the longest block within budget. An online regression policy gathers observations when the check fails. Regret grows at a square-root rate, up to logarithmic factors, across all frequencies and extends to two competing oscillators. On 96 fresh synthetic instances, checked commitment uses 49 fits instead of 1,023, with 1.5% higher mean regret than fitting every round. Matched isolated timings give a 4.69-fold speedup. The confidence sets also certify trajectory regret.


### arXiv 2611.27491 - Alignment without Transfer: Tokenization Impacts Cross-lingual Representations during Multilingual Language Model Pretraining

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:36:23Z
- authors: Jonathan von Rad, Pontus Stenetorp, Yao Lu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27491

Tokenization represents one of the most crucial steps in mulilingual NLP pipelines. Yet, tokenizers routinely under-allocate vocabulary to low-resource languages. While improved allocation leads to better performance, it remains poorly understood how tokenization shapes cross-lingual representations and transfer during pretraining. To address this, we pretrain 39 mono- and bilingual 1B decoder-only models on FineWeb2-HQ across English, German, French, Arabic and Chinese, varying only the tokenizer. Our results demonstrate that improved vocabulary allocation (i) substantially raises cross-lingual representation alignment in early-to-mid layers, an effect that prior work measuring only the output layer could not observe, (ii) increases cross-lingual parametric knowledge sharing in feed-forward neurons, supported by correlational and causal evidence, and (iii) an alignment-transfer dissociation, where cross-lingual transfer remains essentially unchanged despite higher alignment. Moreover, transfer is significantly lower for cross-script bilinguals even under fair vocabulary allocation, indicating that the script barrier is not merely a tokenizer artifact. All of our checkpoints and code are publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.27489 - Efficient Natural Language to Temporal Logic Translation for Real-Time Physical AI

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LO, cs.RO, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-20T11:35:56Z
- authors: Oliver Schön, Adrien Banse, John Lygeros, Lars Lindemann
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27489

Robots can be controlled to perform tasks with guaranteed correctness when the task instructions are stated in a formal language. Large language models (LLMs) have been widely used to allow users to provide such instructions in natural language (NL), by translating NL into formal language. When deploying such translators on a robot, however, we face limited computing and energy resources, while offloading computation or data to the cloud may be infeasible or undesirable. Yet an LLM is a general-purpose text generator, whereas a formula in a fixed formal language fragment follows a well-defined syntax. We therefore ask whether text generation is needed at all. For the formal language of linear temporal logic (LTL), we show that this translation can instead be broken down into answering a set of questions with pre-defined answers. We then construct a System-One model, a recent class of NL-based classifier, to provide such answers and enable us to translate a natural language instruction into an LTL instruction. On three benchmarks, our translator matches or exceeds the accuracy of the best local LLMs available, at up to one order of magnitude lower runtime and energy consumption. We show that it generalizes to unseen atomic propositions and temporal patterns.


### arXiv 2611.27483 - From Manufacturing Event Logs to Simulation: Enabling SMEs with Agentic AI for SimPy Simulation Generation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:31:12Z
- authors: Richard Reider, Lina Döring, Sebastian Lang, Tobias Reggelin, Sebastian Trojahn, Pascal Reusch
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27483

High expertise and cost barriers exclude small and medium-sized enterprises from discrete-event simulation (DES). We introduce GenAI4SimPy, a multi-agent framework powered by Qwen 3.6 (27B), an open-weight large language model, that automates end-to-end DES generation from manufacturing event logs to executable SimPy models through tool-augmented reasoning and human-in-the-loop validation. Evaluated on three execution blocks from a public smart factory dataset, the framework achieved a mean absolute percentage error of 7.9\% and coverage of failure rate for all three blocks and of throughput for two of three. A fidelity paradox was observed: accurate process-level parameters did not ensure system-level behavioral fidelity, with flow-time and throughput errors driven by misinterpreted observation-window truncation and absent system-level calibration. These results demonstrate that local open-weight models can perform DES generation at accuracy comparable to cloud-based alternatives, while identifying parameter extraction as necessary but insufficient for behavioral validity. Source code and results are available at https://github.com/RaikoPipe/genai4simpy


### arXiv 2611.27467 - ROSWORLD: When Digital Task Competence Meets Physical Execution

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T11:17:29Z
- authors: Caoyuan Ma, Wendi Liu, Zhimin Zhang, Muyao Niu, Yujie Lu, Zengxi Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27467

Computer-use agents now solve most desktop tasks through direct software input, but a robot using the same computer must turn GUI knowledge into physical contact with a keyboard and mouse. We introduce ROSWorld, a benchmark that couples a physically simulated robot workstation to a live OSWorld desktop, so that a robot and a desktop agent pursue the same goals under the same official evaluators. The evaluated model receives observations only from the workstation camera views and operates the peripherals through either a single-arm rigid probe or dual-arm dexterous hands. Across five frontier models and 40 tasks in ten applications, models that complete 31-39 tasks on the desktop complete at most 23 with the probe and 28 with the hands. Paired trajectory analysis shows that models adapt their interaction strategies toward keyboard input, yet physical execution frequently fails through missed presses, incorrect targets, and unintended contacts. ROSWorld makes the gap between knowing what to do on a computer and physically doing it measurable.


### arXiv 2611.27465 - Cecilia: Towards Layout-Inclusive Optical Music Recognition

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:16:05Z
- authors: Congren Dai, Zhancheng Guo, Xiaobing Li, Maosong Sun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27465

Reconstructing an editable music score requires musical events, sung text and the instructions that organise them on the page. Existing recognition targets often omit part of this information, while direct MusicXML prediction repeats substantial structural markup. We introduce Cecilia, a compact representation of canonicalised MusicXML that retains musical content, lyric attachments and layout instructions. Cecilia-OMR predicts this representation through metadata and measure-bounded music segments from shared visual memory. A learned count head determines the number of output segments. Across paired complete scores under a common tokeniser, the ratio of mean MusicXML to Cecilia sequence lengths is 27.73. Cecilia-OMR outperforms Legato 2 on Camera String Quartets and both Lieder conditions, with a four-condition OpenScore macro normalised edit distance of 0.2232 against 0.2998. Separate evaluations of lyrics, notation trees and rendered layout reveal complementary aspects of reconstruction and quantify the contribution of layout instructions. Training-stage, reward and candidate-selection studies further examine how recognition changes across model adaptation and decoding.


### arXiv 2611.27463 - Supervaluationism about vagueness and indeterminacy: A judgment-aggregation perspective

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T11:12:40Z
- authors: Christian List, Paul Egré, Franz Dietrich
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27463

Supervaluationism about vagueness and indeterminacy is based on the idea that some sentences, or predicates within them, have multiple competing interpretations or specifications. A sentence is deemed determinately true if and only if it is true according to all specifications; and determinately false if and only if it is false according to all specifications. In all other cases, the sentence is assigned no determinate truth-value. Supervaluationism, however, is only one way of taking into account the verdicts of different specifications -- in technical terms, "aggregating" those verdicts. In this paper, we introduce insights from the theory of judgment aggregation to study a broad class of different aggregation functions. This allows us to offer an axiomatic characterization of supervaluationism, which, in turn, locates it precisely within a larger logical space of possible approaches to vagueness and indeterminacy. Among other results, we axiomatically compare supervaluationism with subvaluationism, the epistemic theory of vagueness, and Strong Kleene and LP logics.


### arXiv 2611.27462 - On the Challenges of Using LLMs in High-Risk AI Recruitment: Guiding Questions for Legal Conformity with the AI Act

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.HC
- published: 2026-11-20T11:10:53Z
- authors: Clara Punzi, Stefano Tramacere, Andrea Fedele, Sezer Kutluk, Roberto Pellungrini, Gizem Gezici
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27462

The EU Artificial Intelligence Act (AI Act) imposes detailed obligations on providers of high-risk AI systems but offers limited operational guidance, particularly when these systems integrate third-party General-Purpose AI (GPAI) models. This paper introduces a toolkit that fosters the development of legal-by-design AI-assisted recruitment systems by translating the core legal obligations of the AI Act's conformity assessment---specifically Articles 9--15 and Annex IV---into techno-normative requirements and corresponding guiding questions. While recruitment pipelines may involve components with varying technical complexity and differing levels of algorithmic transparency, this paper focuses on the most challenging case: the integration of third-party GPAI models into critical stages of the selection process, including resume and job description parsing, applicant scoring, and shortlist generation. This scenario presents significant challenges for downstream providers of high-risk AI systems, as it underscores the difficulties in obtaining the technical information needed to demonstrate compliance with the AI Act's techno-normative requirements, both prior to and during market deployment. After introducing the toolkit and illustrating its application through a simplified example, the paper explores informational dependencies between upstream and downstream GPAI model providers. These dependencies may produce informational gaps across the AI supply chain, potentially hindering the collection of evidence necessary for the AI system provider to ensure legal compliance. The resulting lack of technical transparency negatively affects the deployer, who may struggle to maintain meaningful oversight of the system, and ultimately impacts the persons subject to the AI output.


### arXiv 2611.27447 - Mechanism Parity for Simulation-Based Validation of Autonomous-Driving Perception

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T10:43:41Z
- authors: Danial Safaei, Siddartha Khastgir, Matthew D. Higgins, Jeroen Ploeg, Mohsen Alirezaei, Son Tong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27447

Simulation is central to intelligent-vehicle validation, but visual realism or output agreement between a real scene and its synthetic counterpart does not prove that a perception system used the same evidence in both domains. This paper addresses that mechanism-parity gap for paired real-synthetic autonomous-driving perception. It unifies Decisive-Feature Fidelity, which compares decisive evidence maps in a frozen system under test (SUT), with Concept-Decisive Feature Fidelity, which projects decisive evidence into semantic scene concepts for diagnosis. The framework is a validation diagnostic rather than a standalone safety certificate: it asks whether output-consistent simulation also preserves the mechanism by which the perception model reaches that output. The empirical evidence comprises ten retrained diffusion ControlNet generators evaluated on a deterministic exact-geometry KITTI-Virtual KITTI 2 subset. Generated counterparts use a 1248-by-376-pixel padded inference canvas and are cropped back to the exact KITTI dimensions before evaluation. The final benchmark reports target-image, output-consistency, and mechanism metrics recomputed from a single audited output set, then adds metric-disagreement cases, bootstrap uncertainty, threshold sensitivity, and concept/cache integrity audits. Across the benchmark, output and appearance fidelity are at most weakly and inconsistently rank-correlated with decisive-mechanism distance for all three frozen SUTs, and a constructed positive control shows the estimator detects a known planted mechanism that output agreement misses, supporting decisive-evidence diagnostics for intelligent-vehicle simulation validation. All numerical claims are restricted to the declared exact-geometry benchmark, ten generators, and three frozen SUTs.


### arXiv 2611.27443 - Learning When to Ask: Advisor-Aware Post-Training for SLM Agents

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:39:02Z
- authors: Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27443

Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for cheap, high-throughput agentic deployment, but they remain weak at multi-step reasoning and tool use that long-horizon tasks demand. We study how to post-train an SLM to seek advice from a stronger model and act on that advice itself, following an advisor-executor pattern. Out of the box, this pattern fails across open- and closed-source small-model families: wiring an SLM to an advisor is a harness-design matter that is not enough on its own. A post-training recipe is needed to teach the model when to ask, in a cost-aware fashion, and how to follow advice across steps. Our method, ADAPT (ADvisor-Aware Post-Training), is a multi-stage recipe that instills this behavior, learning entirely from the policy's own rollouts supervised by the environment's outcome, with no learned reward model or human annotation. Across three domains, compared to an untrained harness baseline, ADAPT improves success rate in embodied text reasoning (TextWorld) from 24.5% to 77.3%, code generation (LiveCodeBench) from 21.8% to 56.0%, and deep web search (GAIA) from 17.5% to 49.3%, while querying the advisor sparingly (~1.0-2.1 calls per task). On the two long-horizon domains it closes 70-85% of the gap to an oracle that perfectly times when to ask for advice, and on code generation it exceeds that oracle. The gains persist when the advisor is replaced by one unseen in training, showing that the learned help-seeking policy is robust to a changing advisor rather than tied to the one it was trained with.


### arXiv 2611.27436 - THRIFTY: Cost-Constrained Reasoning-Data Synthesis for Small Language Models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:32:38Z
- authors: Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27436

Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for efficient, specialized deployment, and are typically trained on reasoning trajectories distilled from a larger teacher LLM. Generating this synthetic data is a major cost of the pipeline, especially when the teacher is a frontier model. Existing collection methods, however, are largely cost-unaware, or at best spread a fixed sampling budget uniformly across queries. Such allocation ignores the wide variation in problem difficulty and the strategic over- or under-sampling it invites. Thus, we propose THRIFTY, a Thompson-sampling bandit controller that spends a fixed generation-token budget to collect diverse correct reasoning trajectories, casting the task as a bandits-with-knapsacks problem with a novel diversity-aware objective. It tracks each problem's success probability with an online Beta posterior and spends each token where it expects the most new distinct correct solutions. Because repeated solutions add progressively less value, THRIFTY adaptively stops resampling saturated problems, concentrates budget on under-covered ones, and retires the unsolvable at the budget's opportunity cost. On math (AIME, MATH-500) and code (LiveCodeBench) benchmarks, THRIFTY gathers up to 22% more diverse correct trajectories per generation token than both the standard uniform-sampling recipe and the strongest adaptive baseline, with the largest gains in the scarce-budget and hard-problem regimes.


### arXiv 2611.27434 - No Audio, No Transcripts, No Problem: LLM-based ASR Adaptation Using Only Domain Documents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:31:40Z
- authors: Sergio Burdisso, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Thibault Bañeras-Roux, Manjunath K E, Kadri Hacioglu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27434

Can a speech recognizer be adapted to a new domain with no target audio and no transcripts, using only written documentation? This is the realistic onboarding setting: a new customer hands over raw documentation, not the transcripts that text-only adaptation normally assumes. We turn a domain's documents into adaptation text in two ways, directly as written sentences or rendered by a large language model (LLM) into synthetic spoken dialogs, and feed them to a recently proposed denoising-based text-only adaptation method. We show that, across in-domain, out-of-domain, and cross-domain settings on two corpora, document-only adaptation can match or closely approach real transcriptions; whether written or conversational rendering is best depends on speaking style and the amount of text generated. To our knowledge, this is the first study of document-only adaptation of LLM-based ASR. As no existing corpus pairs audio, transcripts, and documents, we also built and released a document-grounded benchmark.


### arXiv 2611.27427 - Neurodegenerative Differential Diagnosis with Knowledge-enhanced Structural Reasoning

- categories: cs.CE, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T10:26:43Z
- authors: Mingxuan Wang, Siyuan Du, Yanfeng Wang, Ya Zhang, Chencheng Zhang, Jiangchao Yao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27427

Accurate differential diagnosis of neurodegenerative disorders remains challenging due to overlapping clinical manifestations and heterogeneous, often incomplete multimodal observations. Existing multimodal models can integrate imaging and clinical measurements but often overlook disease-specific evidence patterns and pairwise relations among competing diagnoses. We therefore propose Knowledge-enhanced Structural Reasoning (KnowSR), which aligns multimodal patient representations with a literature-grounded knowledge bank of disease prototypes, signed evidence directions, and pairwise diagnostic relations. Specifically, in KnowSR, patient-specific knowledge alignment and target-aware pooling inject structured disease evidence, while anchored relational reasoning reconciles pairwise diagnostic margins over a sparse differential graph, thereby collectively enhancing differential diagnostic capability. Across extensive experiments, KnowSR consistently outperforms 19 baselines on patient-level six-class diagnosis under patient-disjoint five-fold evaluation. This shows that grounding patient evidence in structured medical knowledge enables more effective differential diagnosis than naive multimodal fusion alone.


### arXiv 2611.27426 - Cleave: Severing Guardrails and Auditing Spectral Coupling in Open-Weight LLMs

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:25:54Z
- authors: Feng Huang, Qiang Huang, Yue Gao, Mei Zhang, Bin Liu, Lihua Ju
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27426

Open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely downloaded for their strong coding and reasoning capabilities and deployed locally beyond the provider's control. To prevent misuse, developers embed safety guardrails into these models before release. However, we find that these guardrails rely on the residual writing weights to trigger refusals. Based on this insight, we propose Cleave, a training-free attack that directly edits these weights to remove safety restrictions while preserving general capabilities. With Cleave, malicious users can either remove all guardrails or selectively lift only cybersecurity restrictions. Because this selective removal retains up to a 99% refusal rate on other harmful requests and avoids weight rank collapse, it evades both behavioral spot-checks and static weight audits. Across five open-weight LLMs from 7B to 320B parameters, Cleave achieves up to a 67.97% attack success rate on seven safety benchmarks, substantially outperforming existing white-box baselines. On 356 cybersecurity tasks and 15 live vulnerable services, it further boosts executable exploit generation by up to 24.0 times and compromises up to 66.7% of the targets. To counter this threat, we propose a dual-stage defense that combines pre-release subspace diagnostics with post-release weight-coupling audits, achieving 100% detection recall at a 1% false-positive rate.


### arXiv 2611.27425 - TrojanMaxxing: One-shotting VLM Backdoors with Weight-Space Programming

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T10:25:22Z
- authors: Ahaan Dabholkar, Harshvardhan Patel, Michalis Polychronakis, Saurabh Bagchi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27425

Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can reason directly about the weights of other neural networks. Given only a natural-language objective, an LLM can inspect a model's weights, understand what they compute, and edit them directly without gradient-based optimization. This capability, termed weight-space programming (WSP), has been used to improve model performance on math reasoning, medical QA and other benchmarks. We propose TrojanMaxxing, a novel strategy to backdoor vision-language models (VLMs), by reframing it as a WSP task. We use Claude Opus 5.5 and Mythos 5.2 agents to backdoor VLMs without any optimization or training methods. The agents inspect the model weights, apply targeted edits, and co-design an imperceptible visual trigger that activates the backdoor. We then study the agents' strategies and present a thorough evaluation of the trojaned models on various benchmark datasets. TrojanMaxxing achieves attack success rates of 93%-100% on content moderation and 78%-100% on OCR while maintaining clean accuracy. Perhaps most consequentially, we show that injecting backdoors in a training-free manner is possible by simply, "asking an agent to do it".


### arXiv 2611.27424 - Jagged Robustness: Uneven Safety Transfer and Emerging Vulnerabilities under LLM Quantization

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:24:25Z
- authors: Ramneek Agnihotri, Yingte Xu, Hans Vath
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27424

Quantization enables efficient inference in language models through model parameter compression. This work audits the impact of quantization on capability and safety domains including model safeguards, instruction-following, ethics and bias. Through benchmarks, we find that model safeguards and bias can degrade under quantization, while other capabilities such as truthfulness remain more stable in Qwen3-4B. We define this uneven transfer of LLM capabilities under quantization as Jagged Robustness. We find multiple factors driving Jagged Robustness: task complexity, evaluation method and generation coherence. Moreover, we verify the degradation of model safeguards by observing increased compliance with unsafe requests under quantization, and demonstrate universal adversarial suffix attacks that are benign for the full model but jailbreak quantized models. The findings indicate that it is risky to assume that the performance and robustness can transfer evenly across different capability domains from full models to quantized models.


### arXiv 2611.27423 - ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:24:05Z
- authors: GiHyun Kim, JinMo Yang, Hyung-Sin Kim
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423

Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.


### arXiv 2611.27410 - Detecting Word-Substitution Poisoning of a RAG Knowledge Base with Relative Entropy and Runtime Verification

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:15:58Z
- authors: Ali Yousefi, Nikos Komninos, Hafizul Asad
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27410

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system answers questions using passages it retrieves from a knowledge base, so users must trust that knowledge base. We study an attacker who quietly edits existing passages by swapping words for synonyms (PWWS, TextFooler) in a cybersecurity RAG system built from MITRE ATT&CK and CAPEC. Using a reviewed 44-question benchmark, repeated paired runs and a control corpus in which only the layout changes, we find that poisoning lowers judged answer correctness from 0.714 to 0.555. The damage comes mostly from lost evidence and refusals, not from false statements. To find the edited passages, we use relative entropy. Comparing a whole passage with normal text is a weak signal. Still, one term of the relative entropy, the log-ratio between a word and its most probable synonym, works well: with a small language model as the reference, it reaches an AUROC of 0.95 and stays near chance on the control. Used as a retrieval filter, it halves how often edited text reaches the generator, and an attacker who must keep each passage as probable under the language model fails on all 57 sections it tries. Filtering does not restore correctness, because the edited passage is usually the one that held the answer. We therefore add a runtime monitor that checks each answer against trusted MITRE STIX data and remembers the passages it rejects. On its own, it reduces exposure to edited text from 0.41 to 0.26; when it may add the trusted MITRE entry as a source, correctness rises to 0.786. We report the costs and failure cases of every defence alongside its gains.


### arXiv 2611.27401 - Beyond Linear Decodability: Understanding and Improving Instruction Following in World Action Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:07:53Z
- authors: Bokai Ji, Jiapeng Chen, Yi Zhang, Zeyuan Ding, Yong Dai, Xiaozhu Ju
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27401

World action models (WAMs) exhibit strong manipulation capabilities yet often fail to follow test-time language instructions. Prior work shows that non-following (NF) WAMs encode information about the instructed target, but this information does not control their behavior. By comparing NF WAM with its instruction-following (IF) counterpart, we find that target information is substantially more linearly decodable in IF WAMs, despite being encoded in both. Motivated by this observation, we introduce linear decodability supervision (LDS), a simple yet effective auxiliary training objective that promotes the linear decodability of the instructed target information. On LIBERO-CF, InternW0-$Δ$ with LDS achieves a success rate of 74.6% and a grounding rate of 89.2%, surpassing the strongest baseline by 26.8 and 42.9 percentage points, respectively. The effectiveness of LDS challenges the prevailing interpretation that encoded target information does not contribute to WAMs' behavior. To reconcile this apparent contradiction, we propose a prior-instruction competition mechanism, which explains NF and IF behavior as arising from the dominance of prior and instruction signals, respectively. We provide causal evidence for this mechanism by evaluating two training-free interventions derived from it. We reinterpret LDS through this mechanism, showing that it acts as a form of instruction signal enhancement. Finally, more comprehensive evaluations across diverse manipulation tasks and visual domains demonstrate the consistent effectiveness of LDS.


### arXiv 2611.27398 - Taking Apart and Putting Together: Tracking Objects Through Physical Transformations

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T10:03:29Z
- authors: Davide Gardenal, Mingxuan Liu, Flavio Vella, Quentin Guimard, Elisa Ricci
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27398

We introduce Transformation Tracking and Understanding (TTU), extending video object tracking to environments where objects undergo physical assemblies and disassemblies. TTU requires continuously tracking objects through transformations, identifying new entities, maintaining a state graph of their relationships, and assigning semantic labels to transformations. While foundation models (e.g., SAM 2/3) maintain tracklets through appearance changes, they cannot update semantic identities as objects physically evolve. Prior work addresses only disassemblies, relying on expensive searches triggered only when tracking fails. We propose Tracking Instances via Proposals and Overlaps (TIPO), a training-free framework that continuously infers transformations via a generate-evaluate-judge pipeline. TIPO generates split and merge proposals from disjoint mask components, scores them against mask-decoder priors using inner and external overlap, and queries an MLLM on the top proposal for visual verification and semantic labeling. To benchmark TTU, we curate Video Instances, States, and Transformation Annotations (VISTA), a dataset including state graphs with 124 assembly and 132 disassembly events across 50 sequences and over 26,000 frames. TIPO achieves state-of-the-art tracking and state-graph accuracy on VISTA, while operating $6\times$ to $15\times$ faster than prior methods. Project page: https://davidegardenal.github.io/tipo-tracking


### arXiv 2611.27390 - Evidence sufficiency assessment enables cost-effective review allocation in on-premise clinical AI agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:56:46Z
- authors: Yiwen Qiu, Che Sun, Shu Shen, Wentao Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27390

On-premise clinical AI agents must auto-accept high-confidence diagnoses, deferring others to clinician review, yet token probabilities and self-reported confidence miss errors, while more reliable five-run consistency incurs fivefold compute. We propose training-free decision-point evidence sufficiency assessment and a three-tier sequential triage protocol. Masking reasoning and diagnosis, the same 4B model scores sufficiency from objective evidence via prior-debiased probabilities over five ordered levels. Calibration freezes a nominal top-50% threshold, selecting single-run, five-run consistency, or full-deferral tiers by whether the one-sided 95% Wilson lower confidence bound of pass rate among accepted cases meets the 95% clinical target line. Across 1,605 MIRA-v2, CDM, VivaBench, and in-hospital liver malignancy primary cohort cases, MIRA-v2 single-run top-50% pass rate reached 98.91%, matching five-run consistency, saving ~80% compute; CDM achieved 96.00% test attainment by escalating to the five-run consistency tier; cross-specialty, missing-imaging, and combined enriched cohorts entered the full-deferral tier, capping calibration false-certification rate at 0.00%-3.50%.


### arXiv 2611.27383 - PanoUAV-Track: Full-Spherical UAV Visual Tracking in the Wild

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:47:37Z
- authors: Bingyu Li, Lichang Yang, Zhiyuan Zhao, Junyu Gao, Xuelong Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27383

Existing UAV tracking benchmarks predominantly rely on perspective cameras with limited fields of view. Omnidirectional cameras enable full-spherical observation, yet tracking from moving UAV platforms remains largely unexplored. Unlike ground-level panoramic tracking, UAV-mounted omnidirectional cameras observe targets across both azimuth and elevation, introducing geometric challenges under equirectangular projection (ERP). To investigate this setting, we capture approximately \textbf{40 hours of real-world 8K panoramic UAV video} and construct \textbf{PanoUAV-Track} through temporal clip extraction, target annotation, and the integration of simulated sequences. The resulting benchmark comprises 1,895 sequences, 507,961 frames, and 2,872 annotated objects, covering real-world daytime and nighttime scenes alongside simulation, and supporting both visual and language-based target specification. Through extensive evaluations, we reveal a clear \textbf{perspective-to-spherical} generalization gap and identify tracking degradation associated with latitude-dependent distortion and ERP seam discontinuity. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{PanoUAV-Tracker (PUTrack)}, a geometry-aware tracking framework combining latitude-adaptive search and seam-aware periodic sampling. PUTrack improves zero-shot and in-domain tracking, particularly for seam-crossing targets. Code and benchmark resources will be available\footnote{\textcolor{tabpink}{https://libingyu01.github.io/PanoUAV-Track/}}.


### arXiv 2611.27381 - VMSA: Video Memory Skill Agent for Long Video Understanding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:46:06Z
- authors: Changin Choi, Jungwon Park, Jungmin Ko, Jimyeong Kim, Wonjong Rhee
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27381

Long-video question answering requires retrieving and integrating relevant evidence from videos spanning hours or even days. Recent agentic approaches organize video information into external memories and iteratively retrieve evidence to answer questions. However, effective retrieval and reasoning remain challenging, as agents must determine what information to search for, which memory sources to access, and how to connect evidence across distant temporal intervals. To address these challenges, we propose Video Memory Skill Agent (VMSA), a framework that jointly designs structured video memories and memory skills for long-video understanding. VMSA constructs three complementary memory components. Segment Caption Memory and Entity Event Memory serve as searchable evidence sources, while Entity-Aware Episode Memory provides broad yet detailed video context to guide evidence retrieval and reasoning. To further support effective memory utilization, VMSA induces memory skills from successful agent trajectories, incrementally refining or extending a compact skill library to guide iterative evidence retrieval, reasoning, and verification. Across seven long-video question-answering benchmarks, VMSA achieves the highest accuracy on all three week-long egocentric benchmarks, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 9.4% on Ego-R1 Bench, and the best or competitive performance on four additional benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of all three memory components and memory skills, with memory skills also improving temporal evidence localization.


### arXiv 2611.27372 - ReActWAM: Action-Consistent Caching for Efficient World-Action Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:41:22Z
- authors: Zhaochen Guo, Yunke Wang, Siyu Xu, Zijian Wang, Chenghao Xia, Chengbin Du
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27372

World-action models (WAMs) often generate future visual states alongside robot actions, introducing substantial computational overhead during inference. During closed-loop execution, consecutive inference cycles often predict similar action sequences, leading to redundant computation. However, reducing this redundancy requires distinguishing consistent actions from changes that demand fresh computation. To address these challenges, we propose ReActWAM, a training-free framework that combines action-intent-guided prefix skipping with inner-chunk prediction reuse. A coarse-to-fine assessment evaluates motion changes, visual consistency, and action prediction conflicts. For eligible chunks, we draw on first-step differences to adapt the cached latent and network features to the current condition, providing an estimate at a later denoising step that bypasses the redundant prefix. Subsequent inference refines this estimate while reusing stable denoising predictions within the chunk. Experiments across LingBot-VA, FastWAM and the Cosmos family on LIBERO-Long and RoboTwin 2.0 demonstrate $1.41\times$--$5.44\times$ GPU inference speedup, with minimal degradation in task success rates. Real-world experiments on a Franka Research 3 (FR3) robot further achieve $1.46\times$ end-to-end speedup while maintaining success rate.


### arXiv 2611.27369 - AMARIS: Merging Generalist and Specialist LLMs via Adaptive Subspace Inheritance

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T09:39:58Z
- authors: Hwee Young Kang, Irwin De Tao Chin, Wen-Haw Chong, Hai Leong Chieu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27369

Domain specialization of large language models (LLMs) often improves target-domain performance but degrades broad capabilities. Model merging is an attractive training-free alternative, yet common parameter-space strategies (for example, interpolation and sparsified averaging) provide limited control over task-specific interference. We introduce Adaptive Model Alignment via Ranked Inherited Subspaces (AMARIS), a covariance-driven framework that transfers specialist updates only along selected activation directions. AMARIS formulates merging as budgeted subspace inheritance: it seeks high specialist activation capture under a regularized base-output perturbation budget, then builds a low-rank projection gate from ranked generalized eigendirections. This yields a single static merged checkpoint that concentrates transfer in high-utility, low-interference directions. We evaluate AMARIS on Qwen3-based merges (8B and 32B) across finance, instruction-following, and multilingual SEA specialization. In our evaluated settings, AMARIS reaches Pareto operating points that are competitive with strong training-free baselines, including 98.37% specialist retention with 95.16% core retention for the 8B finance setting, 103.55% core retention with 90.28% specialist retention for the 8B instruction-following setting, and 99.20% core retention with 94.80% specialist retention for the 32B SEA setting. These results support subspace-constrained merging as a practical route to compositional capability integration without additional training.


### arXiv 2611.27368 - Testing for Coordination Failures in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-20T09:39:32Z
- authors: Dennis Gross, Jørn Eirik Betten, Helge Spieker
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27368

Deep parallel cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (CMARL)} trains multiple agents that all act at every time step to optimize a common objective. Like single-agent RL, CMARL can produce unsafe behaviors that violate implicit safety requirements not captured by the reward, and these behaviors are hard to explain. However, testing CMARL policies for safety has received less attention than the safety testing of single-agent RL policies. In this paper, we propose DisQ, a test case selection method that identifies failure-revealing states in value-based CMARL by exploiting a signal specific to trained Q-learning agents. The signal is the Q-value gap, an explainable metric that flags a state when the agents' estimates of the achievable return diverge by more than a calibrated threshold. DisQ selects such states for execution and skips those without a gap. Our experiments in four CMARL benchmark environments show that, in most of the evaluated configurations, DisQ improves failure detection over single-agent RL testing methods applied to CMARL, namely random testing and search-based testing with genetic algorithms. Our main contributions are the Q-value gap, an indicator of potential miscoordination computable from a single state without executing the system, and DisQ, a test case selection method that adapts existing single-agent testing methods to CMARL.


### arXiv 2611.27365 - WePM: Hierarchical Conflict Reconciliation for Unified MLLMs

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:34:56Z
- authors: Zhiqiang Yuan, Qiwei Yan, Liwei Yang, Jinzhe Liu, Yang Liu, Zhongyan Hou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27365

Bringing multimodal intelligence from the cloud to users' devices keeps sensitive data local and reduces inference cost, but deploying a specialized model per capability duplicates vision-language capacity under tight memory and compute budgets. We formalize heterogeneous capability density (HCD), measuring how broadly and effectively a model serves heterogeneous task families per unit of resident inference memory. High HCD across representation, generation, and decision raises two challenges: these capabilities need visual evidence at different spatial granularities under a fixed token budget, and separately collected task corpora yield conflicting updates to shared parameters, while cross-task paired supervision is costly and ambiguous for open-ended inputs. We present WePM, a family of pocket models unifying representation, generation, and decision within one vision-language backbone. Multi-Granularity Visual Budgeting (MGVB) allocates a fixed visual-token budget across global, regional, and fine-grained banks; Unified Listwise Knowledge Distillation (ULKD) enriches separately collected data with soft teacher guidance, avoiding cross-task paired annotation; Stable and Efficient Multitask Optimization (SEMO) regulates each task's contribution through explicit loss weights and exposure schedules, with sequential gradient accumulation under memory constraints. In the joint representation-generation setting, WePM outperforms all baselines across five evaluation groups, raising the Joint score from 38.15 to 52.74. In single-task evaluation, WePM-4B attains the best average score over 30 embedding, 39 generation, and seven typed-decision benchmarks (72.86, 62.41, 61.11). Against the separate-model stack with the highest HCD, WePM achieves slightly higher balanced performance with 61.9% less resident weight memory, a 2.64-fold higher HCD.


### arXiv 2611.27353 - WaXup: Weight Averaging of Experts as Data Augmentation for Robust Introspection

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:25:27Z
- authors: Marc Boëlle, Lucas Dixon, Alexandre Ramé
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27353

As the capabilities of large language models keep improving, traditional mechanistic interpretability methods struggle to keep pace. In contrast, trainable introspection methods emerge as a promising alternative; they repurpose the model itself as a self-diagnostic tool, trained to verbalize what it has learned during finetuning in natural language. While training on datasets of finetuned experts achieves encouraging results, it currently lacks robustness and generalization. Building on the data augmentation framework of MiXup, we introduce WaXup, a method that augments the introspection training set through linear interpolation in the weight space. This is motivated by the linear mode connectivity property: weight averaged experts interpolate the behaviors of the initial experts. We evaluate WaXup on three benchmarks: hidden-behavior models, a meta-dataset of real-world NLP tasks and AuditBench, a challenging alignment auditing suite of models adversarially trained to conceal their behaviors. WaXup improves the accuracy of generated descriptions as well as robustness to various distribution shifts. We hope to improve the reliability of trainable introspection methods towards better and safer models.


### arXiv 2611.27352 - PAVE: A Harness for Geometrically Consistent Road Network Modeling with Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:25:01Z
- authors: Yajun Liu, Jinxi Wu, Juan Pablo Millán Raya, Xuesong Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27352

Transportation models, whether they simulate traffic or support planning, are only as reliable as the road networks they run on, down to the lanes and turns at each intersection. Practitioners still take such a network from GIS files and supply its junction and movement data by hand, a task that recurs with each new base year and design alternative, or they generate it from open map data. OpenStreetMap's lane tags give the correct layout for only 13 of the 44 approaches we test; open map data are often incomplete or inaccurate. Satellite and street-level imagery, in contrast, cover all 44 and show their lanes as painted. Vision-language models (VLMs) can read such imagery, but asked directly they lose geometric consistency when the same lane must be matched across a satellite crop, street-level photos taken from different poses and an ID-labelled network. We therefore present PAVE (Perceive, Anchor, Verify, Edit), a harness in which code measures what can be measured, traffic engineering knowledge enters as general rules and checks, and the model makes only the open judgements, each anchored to network IDs so that checks catch answers that contradict a measurement, a second reading or a traffic rule. Scored against human labels at signalised intersections, PAVE raises the share of exactly correct lane layouts from 49-64% with a single request to 86-91% for two VLMs, with most of the gain coming from reading one approach at a time on traced lanes. The same division of work edits a real network to a planning brief: the model names the links and nodes of each part of a facility, and code builds the edit from a design template. Of 18 edits, 16 pass every network check, against none when the model writes its own code, and 2 contain a design or coding error, against 7. An analysis shows where VLMs lose geometric consistency and which check catches each failure.


### arXiv 2611.27350 - GRACE: Controlled Evidence Interventions for Diagnosing and Improving Knowledge Integration in GraphRAG

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:23:21Z
- authors: Minh Pham, Quang Nguyen, Phong Kieu, Cong Tran
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27350

GraphRAG combines a language model's parametric knowledge with retrieved graph evidence, yet aggregate accuracy cannot show whether failures stem from missing paths, distractors, or conflicts with model knowledge. We introduce GRACE, a benchmark built by executing LC-QuAD 2.0 queries over Wikidata and applying four controlled interventions to each evidence subgraph: severing a support path, adding type-compatible distractors, attaching high-degree hubs, and replacing the supported answer with a counterfactual entity. Questions and option positions remain fixed across the five states. The validated benchmark contains 4,028 questions and 20,140 question-graph pairs. These comparisons reveal that question-only routing cannot distinguish evidence states, while relevance guidance can improve answerable states yet hurt when the support path is severed: relevant facts may not jointly suffice to answer. We propose QCFSE-SA, which combines typed local attention, global graph attention, and question-conditioned evidence guidance with a calibrated graph-support gate. With the factual context and generator held fixed, it improves controlled-state accuracy from 57.17% to 62.78% (+5.61 points; question-clustered 95% CI [+5.04, +6.17]) and broken-state accuracy from 55.41% to 67.85%. Over-abstention on semantic distractors remains an open challenge.


### arXiv 2611.27349 - EcomWriteBench: A Real-World Benchmark for E-commerce Writing Tasks

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:22:20Z
- authors: Kaicheng Liang, Jingyi Cui, Hongwei Wen, Feng Wang, Simiu Gu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27349

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used throughout e-commerce workflows to generate content that informs purchasing decisions and supports customer interactions. Evaluating these systems requires accounting for user needs, product evidence, structural constraints, and business requirements that are not fully captured by general-purpose writing benchmarks. We introduce EcomWriteBench, a real-world benchmark containing 3,861 queries from 69 e-commerce writing tasks across 13 categories and five languages. Through task-level extraction, cross-task consolidation, and refinement guided by human feedback, EcomWriteBench constructs a shared pool of 37 abilities and identifies the subset applicable to each task. Ability-wise scoring enables us to compare state-of-the-art LLMs on EcomWriteBench and characterize their relative strengths and weaknesses across fine-grained e-commerce writing abilities. We further propose a preference-calibrated weight-learning framework that converts human pairwise preferences into interpretable scoring rubrics through global, category-level, and task-level aggregation weights. This framework provides a reusable approach for translating human preferences into automated evaluation by combining ability discovery, ability-wise scoring, and preference-calibrated aggregation, and can potentially be applied to a broad range of benchmarks beyond EcomWriteBench. On held-out queries, our scoring framework achieves 88.96% agreement with human preferences, compared with 78.54% for conventional LLM-as-a-judge scoring.


### arXiv 2611.27344 - Learning Interaction Dynamics via Motion-Region Guidance for Robotic Video World Models

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T09:20:22Z
