- authors: Yongkang He, Xiaofen Xing, Xiangmin Xu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27344

Action-conditioned video world models imagine future observations under candidate robot actions, providing a promising visual tool for robot policy evaluation and interaction simulation. However, existing video world models often optimize global reconstruction errors instead of emphasizing interaction-relevant regions. As a result, they may preserve overall scene appearance but overlook local interaction details, leading to physically implausible outcomes such as hallucinated contact effects and inconsistent robot-object interactions. We introduce MoReWorld, a unified motion-region-guided framework that uses a shared motion region, the part of the environment that moves in response to the robot's actions, to focus dynamics learning and video generation on interaction-relevant regions. The same motion region guides local motion prediction, region-aware supervision, and motion-gated future-feature conditioning. At inference time, the motion region is predicted as a motion probability map from visual history and robot actions, without requiring future observations or ground-truth motion labels. On WorldSimProbe, MoReWorld achieves the highest overall simulator-faithfulness score, with the strongest performance on local consistency, global consistency, and interaction dynamics. These results indicate that motion-region guidance helps the model preserve scene-level consistency while more faithfully predicting action-conditioned interaction dynamics.


### arXiv 2611.27339 - Unimodal Shortcuts and Joint Scene-Intent Dependence in Vision-Language Safety Representations

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:14:51Z
- authors: Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Jiajun Zha, Hanqing Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27339

The safety of a request to a vision-language model often depends on the image: asking how to serve a dish is harmless for fresh food and harmful for spoiled food. High accuracy on situational safety benchmarks does not tell us whether a model's safety representation encodes both the scene and the user's intent, because a benchmark can often be solved from one modality alone. Studying frozen vision-language safety representations, we audit five public situational benchmarks and find that each admits a unimodal shortcut: images alone predict the labels, and on same-image prompt pairs a probe that never sees the photograph reaches 0.9645 AUROC. We therefore build a controlled evaluation that crosses safe and hazardous scenes with observation and action requests, so that no single-modality rule can exceed 0.500 group-ranking accuracy. On this control, linear probes on intermediate states of Qwen3-VL-8B and SmolVLM2-2.2B reach 0.958 and 0.930, showing that the scene-intent conjunction is linearly decodable; concatenating separately encoded image and text states already recovers most of this signal, so decodability is not evidence of a fusion mechanism. The same probes screen cheaply but generalize poorly to unseen images and other benchmarks. Reliable multimodal safety evaluation therefore needs controls that rule out unimodal shortcuts before accuracy is read as evidence of joint understanding.


### arXiv 2611.27330 - GGDriver: Glance at What Is Shared, Gaze at What Differs for Perception-Free Planning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:09:39Z
- authors: Jiawei Sun, Maosen Li, Jiamin Shi, Xiaoxu Zheng, Jiun Tian Hoe, Yechao Bai
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27330

A planner deciding whether to turn left or continue straight needs to inspect different parts of the scene for each maneuver. Yet many end-to-end driving planners evaluate every candidate trajectory against the same compressed scene representation. We let each candidate select the visual evidence used to refine and score it. A global glance first produces trajectory hypotheses from shared scene features. We then project a corridor around each trajectory into the camera views and use the resulting mask to attend to patch features at the backbone's native resolution. After refinement, the scorer projects each final proposal again and ranks it using evidence along its own path. This geometric selection requires only the predicted trajectory and camera calibration. On three planners with different scene representations (learned registers, a perception-supervised bird's-eye-view grid, and a coarse image-token grid), adding this gaze improves planning scores. In ablations, path-aligned patches outperform both a matched number of randomly selected patches and attention over all patches. Using the gaze for ranking yields larger gains than using it for refinement alone. With a ViT-S backbone and no perception labels, GGDriver achieves a rater feedback score of 8.110 on the WOD-E2E test set, 94.2 PDMS on NAVSIM-v1, 48.6 EPDMS on NAVSIM-v2, and a closed-loop Driving Score of 72.18 on Bench2Drive.


### arXiv 2611.27329 - H-MSAE: Hierarchical Sparse Concept Coverage for Efficient and Diverse Instruction Data Selection

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:08:19Z
- authors: Bohan Cui, Jingyi Cui, Hongwei Wen, Feng Wang, Simiu Gu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27329

Large instruction-tuning corpora contain substantial semantic redundancy, motivating the selection of compact subsets that preserve useful supervision while reducing training cost. Existing diversity-aware selectors often rely on generated semantic tags and explicit graph structures, introducing considerable annotation and preprocessing overhead. We introduce H-MSAE, a hierarchical sparse instruction selection framework that organizes multi-resolution concepts learned by a Matryoshka sparse autoencoder into a coarse-to-fine hierarchy. Quality-weighted, diminishing-return coverage with ancestor propagation promotes high-quality and non-redundant semantic coverage, while pool-relative length constraints regulate subset composition. We motivate this design through a local quality-diversity analysis of subset SFT utility and examine it using example-quality and representation-space diversity diagnostics. On Tulu 3 data pool, H-MSAE achieves the highest Final Avg among the evaluated 50K selectors across scales and initialization. The selected subset also exhibits the highest mean quality and effective rank among the evaluated methods. By avoiding full-pool generative tagging and supporting efficient sparse selection, H-MSAE reduces measured end-to-end runtime by approximately $5\times$ relative to MIG.


### arXiv 2611.27326 - Reasoning in Fewer Tokens: Concept-Normalized Multilingual Scratchpads and Reversible Model-Adaptive Reasoning Codes

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T09:06:19Z
- authors: Rabimba Karanjai, Suryabhan Singh Hada, Hemanth Hegadehalli Madhavarao, Qun Gu, Libin N. George, Wenhuan Sun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27326

Chain-of-thought prompting significantly improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) but incurs high inference costs due to lengthy autoregressive decoding. While previous efficiency approaches reduce costs by discarding tokens or utilizing continuous latent representations, these lossy methods fundamentally sacrifice interpretability and auditability. In this work, we introduce a novel compression framework for intermediate reasoning. We apply Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE) to reasoning corpora to learn a compressed vocabulary of Private Use Area (PUA) tokens, each representing frequent semantic spans. After expanding the LLM's vocabulary, we fine-tune the model via strictly 1:1, our method is lossless. We evaluate our approach on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, and Qwen2.5-32B across four diverse benchmarks. Our method reduces total generated tokens by 14.7% on average with reasoning trace lengths dropping by up to 27.1%, while incurring a negligible average accuracy cost of 1.6 percentage points. Crucially, this approach is orthogonal to existing lossy methods, allowing practitioners to stack lossless and irreversible compression techniques. We demonstrate that a compressed intermediate language provides a highly efficient computational resource that accelerates inference while fully preserving the transparency of LLM reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.27317 - Do Language Models Know Which Words Steer Their Reasoning?

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:58:26Z
- authors: Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Rena Gao Wei
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27317

While chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits intermediate reasoning steps from language models, it is unclear which words in the context actually steer the CoT. We identify Phantom Context Influence (PCI): language models are disproportionately steered by semantically irrelevant context words, even as the same models deny those words' relevance when asked. To localize PCI, we introduce Leave-One-Out Word JSD analysis, which removes one context word at a time from the otherwise intact context to isolate its marginal effect on the next-token distribution; words whose removal flips the prediction (pivot words) are passed to the same LLM to self-judge whether it recognizes this predictive dependence. Across four models and three benchmarks, language models largely do not know which words steer their reasoning, a mismatch we call the Generation-Judgment Contradiction. Inspired by this phenomenon, we propose Online KV Suppression: it scores every prompt word's influence over the whole chain of thought in a single pass, then zeroes the KV-cache entries of the highest-influence word the model itself judges inert and redecodes. This single-word intervention improves CoT accuracy across four models and five reasoning benchmarks, by up to 8.9 percentage points on math reasoning benchmark.


### arXiv 2611.27313 - A Framework for AI-Ready Data in Earth and Environmental Science

- categories: cs.DL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:56:19Z
- authors: Tobias Weigel, Jan Bumberger, Danu Caus, Christian Chwala, Romy Fösig, Sonja Hänzelmann
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27313

Artificial intelligence (AI), specifically machine learning (ML), is increasingly integral to Earth and environmental research, yet the suitability of current research data infrastructures for supporting AI workflows remains poorly defined. While the FAIR principles provide a foundation for scientific data management, they do not explicitly address several requirements arising when datasets are used for training, evaluating, and deploying AI models. This article presents a practically motivated framework for assessing and improving AI-readiness of research data within distributed Earth and environmental data infrastructures. The framework was developed through a structured, community-driven process, beginning with a review of existing literature and readiness frameworks combined with feedback from a limited survey of scientists applying ML methods. Its main aspects were shaped by a multidisciplinary expert group representing data providers, infrastructure developers, data managers, and AI practitioners. It identifies 25 criteria covering data preparation, data quality, documentation, access, and supervised learning targets. The criteria emphasize aspects particularly relevant to AI applications, including provenance, treatment of missing data, bias documentation, uncertainty characterization, machine-actionable metadata, efficient access formats, and the quality and reproducibility of target generation. Application to a broad range of representative environmental use cases highlights how the relevance of individual criteria varies by application. We argue that AI-readiness should be understood primarily as a property of transparency and fitness for purpose rather than as a fixed level of data quality. The framework lays a foundation for communication and community-driven action to advance AI-ready data and support next-generation AI-enabled scientific workflows.


### arXiv 2611.27306 - $γ$-Scape: An Agent-Centric World for Mobile Embodied Intelligence

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:49:02Z
- authors: Xiaotao Zhang, Lin Qian, Hao Zhang, Xuanrui Cui, Yuehao Huang, Yunzi Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27306

Recent embodied agents construct and adapt robot behavior through executable programs, tool use, and interaction feedback. Studying these systems in mobile manipulation calls for physical worlds that connect agent-directed execution with its physical consequences and reusable interaction experience. We introduce $γ$-Scape, an agent-centric simulation and benchmark platform for mobile embodied intelligence. Agents compose motion tools and learned skills to carry out complete tasks that combine navigation and manipulation in a persistent physical world. BenchForge grounds structured objectives in physical task instances with instructions, reference programs, and goal evaluators, defining task goals while preserving agents' choice of valid solutions. An agentic collection harness further enables agents to produce demonstrations by carrying out contact-rich interactions through sustained control and physical feedback. Assisted reference execution and human guidance provide complementary experience. The resulting tool traces and aligned observation-action records support program refinement and policy learning, with common task-goal criteria for subsequent evaluation. We benchmark representative agent systems on complete tasks and demonstrate contact collection and trace-informed program revision. Together, these workflows provide infrastructure for research on embodied recursive self-improvement.


### arXiv 2611.27302 - Can Reasoning and Image Description Enhance Yes/No Visual Mathematical Understanding?

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:39:06Z
- authors: Guangcheng Song, Weichuan Wang, Linqi Song
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27302

Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general visual tasks, yet their ability to understand mathematical images remains limited. This paper investigates whether explicit reasoning and textual image descriptions can enhance VLMs' performance on binary yes/no visual mathematical questions. To achieve this, we curated a paired evaluation set to benchmark prominent open-source VLMs under varying prompt and input conditions. In particular, we leverage Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting and use textual descriptions, either replacing or augmenting visual inputs, to isolate and evaluate mathematical comprehension. Our results show that explicit reasoning improves performance, especially in paired consistency accuracy, while textual image descriptions do not consistently help and may degrade performance. These findings suggest that reasoning is important for stable visual mathematical judgement, whereas description-based intermediates require more careful design.


### arXiv 2611.27294 - HookEvo: A Framework for Coordinated Plugin Evolution on Hook-Enabled Agent Runtimes

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-20T08:31:28Z
- authors: Shengze Huang, Yanyu Mao, Xiaofei Yu, Baoshen Guo, Xiaorui Wang, Zhiwen Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27294

Repairing failures in tool-using agents can require coordinated changes to how the harness interprets tool receipts, assembles context, and checks actions. We introduce HookEvo, a framework that uses an existing agent runtime's lifecycle hooks to evolve task-specific harnesses. We instantiate HookEvo on DeepSeek Harness. In each round, a separate improver diagnoses failures from execution traces and proposes a candidate. HookEvo evaluates the candidate against the current harness on matched tasks and carries the retained version into the next round. A single candidate can jointly edit executable plugin handlers and the state they share within a task, allowing a tool receipt to inform context and control a later action. Using a frozen Qwen3-4B executor, the selected harnesses lead the reported Qwen3-4B comparisons, achieving 95.41% on ALFWorld, 70.56% on DBBench, 48.17% on WebShop, and 41.67% on OSBench. Across three improvers and three edit scopes, from skill text to single-component and coordinated executable edits, GPT-6.1 Sol shows the largest average gain from the broadest scope. Our code is available at https://huangshengzebluesky.github.io/hookevo/.


### arXiv 2611.27290 - EgoLAM: Learning Latent Actions from Hand and Head Motion

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:29:09Z
- authors: Ziyi Ding, Yiwei Wang, Zhongxing Wei, Xianxin Lai, Yechi Liu, Xiaodi Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27290

Latent action models let a robot policy learn from video by turning the change between two frames into a training target. In egocentric video, a cup can move across the image because a hand pushes it or because the wearer turns. Both changes help predict the next frame, but only the first describes manipulation. During training, EgoLAM supervises the action code with measured wrist motion and supplies camera motion separately to the forward model. Even when the wearer turns while pushing a cup, the training target still describes how the hand pushed it. The action encoder pools object slots around each hand, and wrist translation and rotation can be decoded from the code in a common reference frame. Extracting a code then requires only two RGB frames. Alongside LARYBench classification and regression, we measure how much wrist motion a frozen code retains and how strongly it responds to a synthetic view or lighting change. EgoLAM has the highest hand-motion decoding score among eight evaluated models. Across three LIBERO suites, policies pretrained on its codes and fine-tuned on expert demonstrations achieve 79.38% mean success, 28.73 points above expert behavior cloning and 9.76 points above the strongest other latent action model in each suite on average.


### arXiv 2611.27288 - Preserving Pretrained CFG Structure in RL for Diffusion Models with Conditional Contrast Regularization

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T08:27:32Z
- authors: Yunfei Zheng, Xiaolong Wang, Dandan Zheng, Xianjun Deng, Shenghao Liu, Wei Xiang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27288

Reinforcement Learning (RL) has become an important component in the post-training of diffusion models. In practice, however, standard RL often improves the target reward without reliably preserving holistic generation quality, leading to unstable reward-quality trade-offs that we view as a form of reward hacking. Since Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is known to improve visual clarity, prompt alignment, and perceptual quality in generation, we investigate whether its underlying structure can serve as a useful prior during RL. We present a unified geometric view that interprets both CFG and RL as correction vectors in a common prediction space, and identify the projection of the RL-induced correction onto the pretrained CFG correction as a useful diagnostic of quality-related structural preservation. Geometric analysis and rotation intervention further show that the pretrained CFG direction carries information useful for generation quality that reward-driven RL updates do not necessarily preserve. Based on this insight, we propose Conditional Contrast Regularization (CCR), which uses the frozen reference model's conditional contrast as a local structural prior during RL. Experiments across multiple reward settings and backbones show that CCR more consistently preserves generation quality while maintaining competitive reward, yielding a better reward-quality trade-off than standard RL and generic regularization methods.


### arXiv 2611.27286 - Belief-State Management Meets Rubric Rewards for Agentic Forecasting

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-20T08:26:40Z
- authors: Jingyi Chai, Yun Liao, Hao Lu, Kemeng Zhang, Bingyang Zheng, Yujie Ge
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27286

Current large language model (LLM) agents still struggle to forecast real-world events. This stems from two primary limitations: i) standard ReAct harness accumulates reasoning and evidence within one growing context, and ii) outcome-based rewards in forecasting lead to a slow and noisy signal that cannot distinguish sound reasoning from lucky guesses. To address these, we introduce a forecasting training framework including harness design and post-training. For the harness side, we propose Proactive Belief-State Management (PBM), equipping agents with a fold tool to compress lengthy context into compact belief states and reset the context for subsequent exploration. For the training side, we develop SEIPH, a five-dimensional, outcome-blind rubric, providing process-quality rewards. Using the rubric-based reward, we post-train a 35B model with PPO and GRPO, yielding DeepForecaster-PPO and DeepForecaster-GRPO. Theoretically, we prove that idealized fold-based stopping converges and calibrated local rewards reduce segment-level gradient variance. Empirically, DeepForecaster-PPO (35B) achieves the best Brier score on both ForecastBench and Polymarket-Sim, outperforming frontier agents based on DeepSeek-V4-Pro (1.6T), Kimi-K2.6 (1T), and GPT-5.5. DeepForecaster-GRPO ranks second on both FutureX metrics. Ablations reveal that PBM also improves the untrained base model without any training. Calibration analysis shows that DeepForecaster-PPO makes more accurate high-confidence predictions than Qwen3.6 and GPT-5.5. Trajectory analysis further shows that forecasts generally improve from the first to the final belief state as agents gather evidence and revise their judgments.


### arXiv 2611.27283 - DermKG: A Concept-Centric Knowledge Graph for Interpretable Skin Disease Diagnosis from Dermatological Morphology

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:22:54Z
- authors: Cheng Huang, Yanmei Li, Mao Lin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27283

Dermatologists describe lesions with a standardized morphological lexicon, which makes concept-based models attractive for interpretable skin disease diagnosis. How much diagnostic information these concepts actually carry, however, is rarely quantified. We present DermKG, a concept-centric knowledge graph built on the expert-annotated SkinCon subset of Fitzpatrick17k (3,230 images, 48 concepts, 113 diseases). DermKG has 3,452 nodes of 8 types and 19,365 edges of 11 relation types, and encodes disease concept profiles, concept co-occurrence, a three-level diagnostic hierarchy, and 20 clinical morphology rules, all of which are supported by the training data (15 strong, 5 moderate). An information-ceiling analysis shows that the 3,230 lesions collapse onto only 813 distinct concept vectors, bounding concept-only top-1 accuracy at 38.9\% for diseases and 77.6\% for the nine categories. Within this bound, DermKG-Net, a graph neural network that scores lesions against disease prototypes and aggregates them along the hierarchy, raises category-level balanced accuracy from 0.257 to 0.411 over the strongest of 11 baselines (paired bootstrap difference $+0.150$, 95\% CI $[0.073, 0.231]$) and more than doubles minority-category recall. Ablations show that most of this gain comes from the hierarchical prototype objective, with a smaller contribution from graph structure. DermKG further yields rule-based reasoning chains and concept-level explanations for individual predictions.


### arXiv 2611.27264 - EAGLE: Entity-Aware Grounding with Layered Evidence for High-Resolution Image Understanding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T08:03:40Z
- authors: Zhongjian Zou, Quan Qian
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27264

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) still struggle with fine-grained understanding of high-resolution images: attention heads vary in their reliability for grounding target entities and their attributes, while localized details are often disconnected from the broader scene context. We propose EAGLE (Entity-Aware Grounding with Layered Evidence for High-Resolution Image Understanding), a plug-in framework that jointly selects grounding signals and organizes hierarchical visual evidence. EAGLE employs a fine-grained dataset to screen visually-grounded attention heads, followed by entity-aware grounding to extract target-specific regions. It then integrates hierarchical evidence construction and question-guided routing to select optimal view combinations, feeding them into the frozen MLLM to enhance fine-grained visual understanding. Experiments with Qwen2.5-VL-7B and InternVL3-8B demonstrate that EAGLE consistently improves performance across both backbones and achieves SOTA results on V*, HRBench-4K, and HRBench-8K compared with existing methods using the same backbone.


### arXiv 2611.27260 - No Perfect Guardrail & No Perfect Guard: On the Decidability of Output Policies for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CY, cs.LO
- published: 2026-11-20T08:01:29Z
- authors: Ahmed Amer, Loren Kinczel, Darrell D. E. Long
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27260

Expecting guardrails to provide a general guarantee of safety fails to recognize the limits of computational enforcement and policy specification. We argue that promising such perfection is an ethical failure of precision, and that invoking it to abdicate responsibility is a moral failure. Guardrails can support responsible action, but cannot relieve researchers, developers, policymakers, or users of their obligations to examine what they claim, permit, and prevent. We establish a precise computational boundary. If every finite string is a possible output of a language model, a computable guard that blocks every forbidden output and passes every permitted one exists if and only if the policy is decidable. For an undecidable policy, every guard misclassifies infinitely many strings, and every guard that blocks all forbidden outputs excludes infinitely many permitted ones. We apply this characterization to arithmetic truth, program behavior, and harmful instructions to an idealized recipient. We distinguish guards from execution monitors, identify bounded and interceptable cases admitting exact enforcement, and explain why the results establish no positive lower bound on average error. Exact enforcement does not establish that a policy adequately captures safety. Conservative exclusion may withhold warnings, remedies, or challenges needed to recognize the policy's own defects. Human oversight cannot substitute for judgment: an apparent controller can become an instrument of purposes they have ceased to examine. Responsible governance requires continued critical challenge and the intellectual and moral humility to recognize possible flaws in our machines, rules, policies, and ourselves. The impossibility of a general assurance of perfection is a condition responsible practice must confront, not a responsibility that another guardrail - or another guard - can remove.


### arXiv 2611.27253 - Proactive Defense Benchmark against Deepfake Generation

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T07:59:30Z
- authors: Joonhyuk Baek, Wonjune Seo, Jae-yun Kim, Saerom Park, Hoki Kim
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27253

Despite the proliferation of proactive defenses against deepfakes, the lack of a unified evaluation protocol precludes fair comparison and masks critical vulnerabilities. To bridge this gap, we present the first comprehensive benchmark that systematically assesses disruption, robustness, and transferability encompassing pixel, perceptual, and identity metrics. Our extensive analysis reveals that fidelity and identity metrics capture orthogonal performance axes, often leading to conflicting interpretations when relied upon individually. Furthermore, we identify a fundamental trade-off where peak white-box performance signals overfitting, and we introduce a calibrated evaluation to correct generator-induced identity bias. By exposing these blind spots, we establish a rigorous standard to guide the development of genuinely generalizable protections. Project page is available at: https://proactivedefensebenchmark.github.io/


### arXiv 2611.27227 - CreditFilter: Calibrated Token Weights over the Within-Response Entropy Rank for RLVR

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:25:44Z
- authors: Hanqing Zhao, Liuyue Xie, Rong-Cheng Tu, Xu Zhang, Davide Modolo, Dacheng Tao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27227

Critic-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically assigns a uniform advantage to every token. Token-reweighting heuristics like the 80/20 mask favor high-entropy tokens, but fail to measure a token's actual impact on the outcome. We formalize this impact as the forking value: the variance of the success probability over the policy's next-token choices. The expected forking values along a response sum exactly to the reward variance. Token credit is thus, in expectation over a prompt's responses, a fixed budget spent at the forks. This motivates weights that redistribute credit within a response. The forking value factorizes into a fork consequence, which requires costly continuations to estimate, and a fork probability, which the softmax provides. On Qwen3-8B-Base, this fork probability decays exponentially with a token's within-response entropy rank at class-specific rates, revealing that most tokens carry negligible forking value. CreditFilter weights each token by an exponential window over the same rank. The window is calibrated to this fork-probability envelope, under an approximately flat fork consequence. CreditFilter needs no extra forward passes and is stabilized by difficulty damping and an entropy-gated fallback. Averaged over three seeds, it outperforms DAPO by 7.7 points on MATH500 and by 10.5 and 9.1 points on AIME24 and AIME25. Its dynamically adaptive variant matches it. The ablations show that both parts matter: ranking tokens within each response rather than across the batch, and matching the window's decay rate to the envelope. Code is available at https://github.com/yoctta/CreditFilter.


### arXiv 2611.27223 - LiDAR Signals for Robot Locomotion: Spherical Range Images Enable Robust Sim-to-Real Transfer

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:22:07Z
- authors: Nian Liu, Xinyi Tong, Ming Ren, Long Ye, Zhaofeng He
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27223

Existing legged-locomotion systems commonly rely on depth images or online elevation maps. However, depth-based policies suffer from perceptual sim-to-real discrepancies due to imperfect depth sensor modeling, while map-based approaches are vulnerable to drift and misalignment. LiDAR offers direct measurements of the surrounding geometry, but raw LiDAR scans lack a compact, structured representation for high-frequency control. We propose a LiDAR perception pipeline that maintains perceptual consistency across simulation and reality through sensing- and representation-level alignment. At the sensor level, we recover frame-wise angular sampling patterns from the physical LiDAR and replay them in simulation with GPU ray casting. At the representation level, simulated and real returns undergo the same spherical range image encoding, yielding a fixed-size policy observation for end-to-end training and direct deployment without teacher--student distillation, online elevation mapping, or adaptation. Experiments show that our range-image policies achieve stronger sim-to-real transfer than depth- and elevation-map-based baselines, while ablations confirm the importance of the proposed sensing components. More visualizations and real-world videos are available at the \href{https://lidar-img.github.io/}


### arXiv 2611.27222 - Bel: Step-Level Policy Improvement of Visual Agents Without Training

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:21:43Z
- authors: Jiajun Fan, Ziwen Wang, Zhaoyu Zhu, Changyi Yang, Neil He, Chaoran Cheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27222

A visual agent acts on what it sees: it taps and types on a screen, or points on a camera image to show a robot where to act. One wrong step can fail its task, yet the right action is often among its own samples, passed over by its greedy decode. Choosing it is a visual judgment, since an action is best judged where it acts, not by its text or coordinates alone: as text, a tap is two numbers; drawn on the screen, it is the Post button of an empty draft. We find that this is decisive: given the screenshot and a tap's coordinates, a decision model chooses among GUI-R1-3B's samples significantly worse on average than when the tap is drawn where it lands. Bel turns this finding into a method that trains nothing and needs only a frozen policy's samples and an image on which each action can be drawn. It marks each candidate on its own copy of the image it acts on, asks a decision model for the probability that executing it is the right decision, so that every kind of action shares one scale, and acts on another candidate only when greedy scores below a threshold and trails it by a margin. On recorded GUI steps, for each of three policies, Bel significantly beats greedy decoding on mobile, web and desktop screens alike and, on the same candidates, gives each a significantly higher mean step success than voting, CATTS, the trained GUI-Critic-R1 and two prompted Qwen3-VL judges shown the same marks; it also significantly improves the much larger Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B on every step-level test set. With GUI-R1-3B's thresholds unchanged, Bel significantly raises live AndroidWorld and MiniWoB++ task success for two other policies and robot-pointing success for three policies on each of two groups of benchmarks. Judging each action where it acts thus gives step-level policy improvement of visual agents without training, with significant gains for GUI agents and robot pointing.


### arXiv 2611.27219 - NavTTT: Adaptive Parametric Memory via Hierarchical Test-Time Training for Vision-and-Language Navigation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:14:55Z
- authors: Xiaoyu Luo, Chengxing Lin, Jiaxin Huang, Jiale Liu, Jingyu Guo, Sirui Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27219

Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) demands that agents accumulate and reason over streaming visual observations throughout long navigation episodes. Existing approaches struggle to maintain long-term history, constrained by the finite context capacity of transformer architectures. To solve the problem, fast-weight test-time training (TTT) provides a mechanism for encoding streaming observations into an evolving parameter state. However, existing TTT does not distinguish informative observations from repetitive visual content, allowing redundant tokens to interfere with the retention of information needed for subsequent decisions. We propose Navigation-Conditioned Test-Time Training (NavTTT), which introduces adaptive memory formation and hierarchical memory integration for vision-and-language navigation. NavTTT leverages global navigation context to prioritize informative visual evidence and integrates language instructions with spatial information to construct spatially grounded memory representations. Episode-local nonlinear fast weights store these representations as Adaptive Parametric Memory (APM), preserving history beyond the visual context for later decisions. To reduce cross-layer coupling in memory formation, NavTTT maintains independent fast-weight memories across multiple decoder depths. This decoupled design prevents errors or biases from spreading across different memory levels. Meanwhile, it enables shared historical information to be independently represented at various levels of abstraction. Experiments demonstrate strong navigation performance on VLN-CE benchmarks and effective instruction following on a real robot.


### arXiv 2611.27216 - Correct Precedents Induce Policy Violations in On-Device LLM Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-20T07:07:54Z
- authors: Chong Tian, Yu Wang, Steve Liu, Qirong Ho, Xiuying Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27216

Local-cloud agent systems pair a cloud model with a small on-device agent that acts on private data, often as the last check before an irreversible action. We identify, for the first time, a failure in which such an agent is led into a violation by its own correct decisions within a session. After it correctly releases an item whose restriction was lifted, it becomes more likely to release the next item that is still restricted. Because the trigger is a correct decision, a history with no faulty step can still end in a violation. We call this failure correct-precedent generalization and find it in 13 open-weight models from nine developers. A single correct precedent can cut the safe rate of the most affected agent roughly in half, and the risk compounds when a refused request is simply sent again in a new session. Interventions on the agent's history indicate that it carries its decision on the released item over to the restricted one instead of checking that item's status. Building on this finding, we propose Paired Decision Distillation (PDD), which distills the decisions of a weakly affected teacher into a LoRA adapter. PDD restores the safe rate to nearly 100% in every distilled model, without extra prompt text or model calls.


### arXiv 2611.27215 - Neural Defend Ranks #1 in Global Audio Deepfake Detection Benchmark - ARCAS on the Podonos Independent Leaderboard

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:07:38Z
- authors: Sivashankar Selvarajan, Piyush Verma, Sumit Singh
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27215

Commercial detectors of AI-generated audio are usually promoted with results on internal test sets that buyers cannot inspect. Independent benchmarks with private labels give a more reliable comparison. We report the results of Neural Defend's ARCAS foundation models on the Podonos audio deepfake detection benchmark, an independent leaderboard that scores commercial detection APIs and open-source detectors on one held-out set of real speech and speech from current text-to-speech and voice-cloning systems, delivered in six file formats, against labels that only Podonos holds. In the release of 29 October 2026, which covers 28 systems from 16 commercial vendors and the leading open models, ARCAS 290M and the smaller ARCAS 90M share first place, ahead of every other commercial system, including Resemble AI, Hiya, Pindrop, Hive, and Reality Defender. Both models make fewer errors than any other system by a statistically clear margin, keep false positives and false negatives in balance at their default threshold, process every submitted file without rejection, and sit on the accuracy--latency frontier of the leaderboard. As for every commercial system added since Podonos retired its API runner, the ARCAS predictions were submitted by Neural Defend and scored by Podonos against its private labels. We describe the benchmark's construction and protocol in full, reproduce the complete leaderboard and its history across five releases, and analyse error balance, statistical separation, speed, and the gap between commercial and open-source detectors. The results agree with the companion evaluation of ARCAS 290M on the complete test sets of ten public benchmarks and establish ARCAS as the most accurate AI-generated audio detector evaluated on this independent benchmark.


### arXiv 2611.27214 - EAF-TTS: Rethinking Codec-Latent Coordinates for Flow TTS

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T07:00:18Z
- authors: Yumiao Li, Haoxiang Jie, Xinbei Li, Yaoyuan Yan, Yue Qu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27214

Latent text-to-speech (TTS) systems generate codec latents decoded by a frozen decoder, but good reconstruction does not ensure geometry suitable for Flow generation from isotropic Gaussian noise. We present Embedded Acoustic Flow TTS (EAF-TTS), an acoustic-latent adaptation of ELF with phone- and duration-conditioned generation, shared-network terminal correction, and frozen WaveVAE decoding. EAF-TTS supports a fixed ZCA interface estimated from training data and adding no learned parameters. The codec, conditioning modules, Flow backbone, and 20-call inference are shared. The intervention is the full ZCA coordinate protocol---training and sampling coordinates, induced loss metric, and terminal perturbation---not an inference-only transform. In single-speaker LJSpeech, the 64-D training-latent covariance has condition number 1355.44. On text-disjoint Val399 strict-GT with fixed E100 raw checkpoints across three matched seeds, ZCA improves UTMOS22 Strong---an automatic naturalness proxy, not human MOS---by $+0.405\pm0.027$ over Scalar normalization (mean $\pm$ seed SD of per-seed paired differences). Absolute scores remain below codec-reference levels. Without retraining, ZCA-5 retains $+0.375\pm0.027$ UTMOS over Scalar-20 while reducing generation-path RTF by $3.34\times$. This three-seed analysis shows no statistically detectable WER or exact-match degradation, but does not establish equivalence. ZCA-3 gives $5.03\times$ with a $+0.165$ UTMOS advantage. At matched reduced call counts, ZCA's advantage shrinks, indicating full-step headroom rather than better low-step integration. A secondary text-only selected-checkpoint result is directionally consistent but confounded by checkpoint type. We claim only proxy-naturalness and measured quality--latency effects within this protocol; human listening, blind text, and broader validation remain necessary.


### arXiv 2611.27211 - Explicit Style Extraction for Personalized Long-Form Text Generation

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-20T06:52:18Z
- authors: Jenitha Reddy Patel, Sameeksha Kini, Amulya Rayasam, Anisa Dye, Abhinav Sharma, Sai Karthik Navuluru
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27211

Personalized long-form generation asks a language model to write new text the way a specific user would. The standard recipe, retrieval-augmented generation, places a few of the user's past texts in the prompt but leaves the model to decide what to take from them. We introduce StylePrism, a taxonomy of 28 stylistic aspects in seven categories, and SP-Refine, an inference-time method that makes style extraction an explicit step: the model first fills StylePrism with the user's patterns, quoted evidence, and habits to avoid, then drafts from the user's samples and revises the draft twice against this profile, guided by its own style scores. On the three long-form tasks of LongLaMP with three LLMs, SP-Refine outperforms LongLaMP's retrieval-augmented baseline in 25 of 27 ROUGE and METEOR comparisons, by 10.2% on average and by up to 35.8%. Annotating users' own texts with the extracted profiles shows that StylePrism captures concrete, checkable habits, from the order of rhetorical moves to a reviewer's sign-off. Ablations show that the profile helps as revision guidance, not as a replacement for the samples: writing from the profile alone is worse in all 27 comparisons, while a single implicit-inference call is a cheaper alternative that comes close. Oracle variants that let the reviser see the reference, together with reference-free controls, show that larger refinement gains require information unavailable at inference time.


### arXiv 2611.27208 - Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Qwen3-VL for Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:47:45Z
- authors: Manh Tuan Ha, Xuan Bao Viet Tran, Vo Minh Dat Nguyen, Minh Nhat Vo, Quoc Trung Nguyen, Van Bay Nguyen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27208

Handwritten mathematical expression recognition (HMER) requires joint recovery of symbol identity and two-dimensional syntax, so a locally plausible transcription may still be structurally wrong. We study whether a compact vision-language model can be adapted to generate linearized Symbol Label Graphs (SLGs) under limited computing resources. Starting from a 4-bit Qwen3-VL-4B checkpoint, we apply rank-16 Low-Rank Adaptation to the vision, language, attention, and MLP modules and train with instruction-formatted CROHME 2019 examples. We compare this model with an internally implemented ViT-Transformer baseline, a zero-shot Qwen3-VL configuration, and additional internally evaluated alternatives. On the 1,198 valid CROHME test examples, the CROHME-only model obtains 524 exact matches (43.74%) and a corpus BLEU-4 score of 77.67, compared with 4.92% and 43.26 for the task-specific ViT baseline. An ablation that adds 2,000 manually written, formula-guided samples decreases exact match to 42.32%, exposing a domain-alignment issue rather than an automatic benefit from more data. The principal contribution is therefore an evidence-backed, resource-efficient formulation of HMER as multimodal structural generation, together with a controlled data-composition ablation and an analysis of the remaining gap between local token overlap and globally correct structure.


### arXiv 2611.27206 - LingSante-Pulse: Unified Physiological Pretraining through Random-Feature Prediction and Cross-Modal Alignment

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:45:57Z
- authors: Linxiao Yang, Wenwei Wang, Tian Zhou, Bingqing Peng, Zihao Zhang, Zuyi Zhu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27206

Clinical monitoring and wearable devices capture complementary physiological signals, but their partially overlapping modality coverage makes within-signal information and cross-modal relationships difficult to exploit jointly. We introduce LingSante-Pulse, a unified, general-purpose and flexible physiological representation framework that learns from these heterogeneous recordings. A shared, signal-conditioned encoder processes each physical channel independently and supports different numbers and combinations of signals for multimodal assessment and single-sensor inference. Pairwise cross-modal alignment, structured random-feature prediction (RFP) and masked waveform reconstruction jointly learn physiological relationships and preserve informative within-signal structure. Across 38 general physiological task configurations spanning PPG, ECG, EEG and EMG, the frozen encoder achieves the best dataset-balanced mean rank in each domain. In sleep assessment, multimodal staging on the HMC dataset and EEG-only staging on the Sleep-EDF dataset reach Macro-F1 scores of 0.7784 and 0.7941, respectively, while single-lead ECG apnea screening achieves an AUROC of 0.9183. Controlled experiments show that function prediction and waveform reconstruction provide complementary supervision for retaining temporal and rhythmic information. Representations learned under continually refreshed random supervision generalize to unseen teacher realizations, and correct auxiliary modality correspondence improves respiration-only representations. These results demonstrate that a single encoder can learn reusable physiological features across signal types, prediction targets and sensor configurations from partially overlapping recordings.


### arXiv 2611.27198 - DepthMSA: Combinatorial Training and Budget-Aware Depth Allocation for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:33:07Z
- authors: Pan Wang, Jie Peng, Zhimin Li, Jingxian Chen, Hui Ji, Huanrui Yang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27198

State-of-the-art multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) models rely on large pretrained encoders, yet typically process every modality to its full depth, regardless of its computational cost or predictive contribution. This raises a fundamental question: how should limited computation be allocated across modalities to preserve predictive performance? We reveal a substantial mismatch between the computational cost of modality encoders and the predictive benefit of processing them more deeply, suggesting that different modalities require different encoding depths. However, enabling heterogeneous depths introduces a key challenge: representations extracted at different depths must still be effectively fused. To address this, we propose $\textbf{DepthMSA}$, a budget-aware framework that supports flexible modality depths within a single MSA model. Specifically, we introduce combinatorial anytime training, which supervises the fusion head across all cross-modal depth combinations using a single full-depth encoder pass. At inference, a training-free, cost-aware allocator selects the configuration with the lowest validation-calibrated error under a sample-specific FLOPs budget. On CMU-MOSEI, DepthMSA maintains accuracy within one percentage point of the full-depth model while reducing measured FLOPs by 45\%, or by 82\% with a separately trained skip-enabled variant. When a CLIP visual encoder dominates computation, the allocation shifts toward reducing visual depth, achieving 56\% fewer FLOPs at unchanged accuracy. Compared with ADMN, an adaptive layer-allocation method that trains a controller for each budget, the skip-enabled variant reaches its accuracy with 3.2$\times$ fewer FLOPs and lower latency, without any per-budget training. Our project is available at https://pwang322.github.io/DepthMSA/.


### arXiv 2611.27196 - EDAGoat: An EDA-Aware Agent for Project-Level GPU Optimization

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-20T06:31:08Z
- authors: Chengxi Li, Liang Xiao, Yutao Wang, Wenhao Lin, Xinzhuang Xiong, Han Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27196

GPU acceleration has transformed electronic design automation (EDA), yet further improving expert-engineered GPU tools still demands substantial manual effort. Automating this work calls for coding agents that can trace bottlenecks across host and device code while preserving correctness and design quality. We introduce EDAGoat, to our knowledge the first EDA-aware GPU optimization agent for this task. Its optimization enhancement (OE) mode guides edits with four cross-project GPU optimization patterns. Its mandatory validation (MV) mode requires the agent to test the edited program against the EDA constraints its changes could affect. Our benchmark covers five GPU EDA projects across four task families and 55 circuit inputs, measuring complete-solve runtime and verifying the resulting designs. Each configuration runs three independent searches per project. A search qualifies only if its solver passes correctness and design-quality checks on all public and hidden inputs. With DeepSeek, generic prompting qualifies in 7 of 15 searches and OE in 6 of 15, despite a higher best speedup on four projects. Adding MV raises this to 13 of 15 and, on every project, exceeds both generic baselines in mean and best speedup and OE in mean speedup; the geometric mean of per-project mean speedups rises from 1.11x (1.36x with OE) to 1.53x. With GPT-6 Astra, EDAGoat qualifies in all 15 searches and improves both speedup metrics over generic prompting on every project, lifting the same geometric mean from 1.26x to 1.57x. These results show that agents can use EDA knowledge to find further speedups in expert-tuned tools while preserving design correctness and quality, establishing EDA-aware GPU optimization as a new direction for agent research.


### arXiv 2611.27189 - A Frugal AI Framework for India: Responsible and Right-sized AI

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T06:21:57Z
- authors: Balaraman Ravindran, K. S. Venkataraghavan, Gokul S Krishnan, Balaji Srinivasan, Balkrishna C. Rao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27189

India's AI debate often swings between two insufficient options: replicating the compute-intensive, capital-heavy foundation-model programmes of the United States or China, or remaining a downstream adopter of foreign AI with localised interfaces and safeguards. Neither serves a country that must deliver AI to a very large, diverse population across uneven infrastructure and within constrained budgets. This white paper from the Centre for Responsible AI (CeRAI), IIT Madras, proposes a third path: Responsible Frugal AI, which applies principle-based frugal engineering alongside Responsible AI. Responsible Frugal AI is right-sized AI: it fixes the functional requirement first, asks whether AI is needed at all, and then chooses the smallest sufficient model, meeting the requirement with minimal resources while keeping a verified margin, robustness and responsible governance. The framework applies frugality across four dimensions (data, compute, model and operations) through seven levers spanning the AI lifecycle, each with practices and measures. It calls for public AI rails, vernacular-first design, measured resource use, human oversight for high-stakes decisions, and a cap on total resource consumption so that efficiency gains are not cancelled out by rising usage. We argue that India is resource-imbalanced rather than resource-poor, and that frugal principles offer the most viable route to population-scale deployment, fit naturally with India's digital public infrastructure, and make sovereignty operational by preserving India's capacity to specify, deploy, govern, inspect and adapt its AI systems. A seven-point adoption checklist and eleven policy statements translate the framework into practice, positioning AI in India as a responsible, frugal public-value system that maximises social value per rupee and per joule.


### arXiv 2611.27186 - What Does Matching a Human Intervention Effect Establish? Benchmark Checks for LLM Simulations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CY, stat.ME
- published: 2026-11-20T06:18:57Z
- authors: Tengfei Shao, Asma Rezaei, Ali Reza Kamali
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27186

LLM simulations are validated by equivalence with human effects, yet a zero reference passes whenever the human 90 percent interval lies inside the margin, as the human benchmark shows before simulation. When the zero reference fails, planned precision gives independent simulations the margin up to which passing is a 5 percent test of response. In an exploratory 70-experiment reanalysis, 72 percent of GPT-4's $\pm$0.20 SD passes were zero-reference passes; GPT-4 passed 16 contrasts the zero reference failed (1.58 expected without response). In a preregistered audit, Claude Sonnet 5.5 passed three intention targets, all zero-reference passes, and the registered belief-gradient control; norm beliefs changed 0.065 SD against humans' 0.351 (zero-reference check added after unblinding; norm beliefs emphasized after inspection). Registered tests detected no overestimation; average-effect intervals excluded overestimation above 0.08 SD. A group audit's precision floor is 1.33 facts against a 2.9-fact effect. Report the zero-reference threshold beside every equivalence decision.


### arXiv 2611.27183 - GEG-SAM: Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model for Weakly Supervised Polyp Segmentation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:15:49Z
- authors: Ao Wang, Lei Zhang, Lituan Wang, Zhenwei Zhang, Xiangbao Gong, Zhiyi Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27183

Endoscopist gaze offers an unobtrusively acquired record of lesion-oriented visual exploration during routine clinical inspection, providing relevant supervision for tasks. However, existing methods treat fixations as isolated spatial prompts directly associated with the target, overlooking the fact that natural gaze records a continuous visual search process in which fixations are temporally connected, spatially organized, and coupled with the visual content. Interpreting each fixation independently based only on its local visual content overlooks the temporal evolution and spatial organization of the gaze trajectory, whereas modeling relations among fixations alone cannot reveal the visual semantics of the regions they attend to. To this end, we propose the Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model (GEG-SAM), a principled weakly supervised framework designed to explicitly exploit structured gaze relations and their corresponding visual cues to modulate SAM for polyp segmentation. Specifically, GEG-SAM captures structured gaze relations by modeling the temporal dynamics and spatial topology of gaze exploration. It then associates these relational representations with fixation-aligned visual context to derive gaze-conditioned tokens, which are further injected into the frozen SAM encoder to modulate its pretrained image features. In this way, GEG-SAM effectively exploits the rich information embedded in gaze exploration to guide SAM toward lesion-oriented segmentation. Extensive experiments on five public polyp segmentation datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, improving the mean Dice by 9.30 percentage points over the strongest weakly supervised baseline.


### arXiv 2611.27180 - Learning Adaptive Defense Strategies: Fine-Tuned LLMs for Network Security Games under Network Mobility

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:10:59Z
- authors: Md Abu Sayed, Asif Rahman, Ahmed Ryen, Ahmed Hemida, Charles Kamhoua, Christopher Kiekintveld
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27180

Large language models(LLM) show promise for strategic reasoning, yet their ability to compute game-theoretic equilibria under dynamic conditions remains largely unexplored. This work investigates whether fine-tuned LLMs can serve as solver-free surrogates for online equilibrium computation in honeypot allocation, a setting in which network mobility continuously reshapes the underlying attack graph and forces repeated re-solving of a two-player zero-sum game. We construct a dataset of 270 security games on networks of 8--25 nodes with solver-grounded equilibrium labels, and evaluate four classes of predictor over 1{,}524 held-out mobility events: non-learned baselines, six open-weight architectures evaluated zero-shot, the same six after LoRA fine-tuning, and four proprietary models as an external reference point. Predictions are scored without post-hoc repair against exact game-theoretic criteria: exploitability against an optimal attacker best-response, equilibrium support accuracy, and distributional distance. Fine-tuning reduces exploitability across every architecture, and fine-tuned open-weight models of modest size outperform every zero-shot predictor, including proprietary ones; no zero-shot model at any tier reaches the floor set by uniform allocation. Stratifying by mobility type exposes a qualitative shift that aggregate metrics conceal: zero-shot models do not condition on whether a departing node lies on an attack path, while fine-tuned models recover this distinction. We further find that validation loss is negatively rank-correlated with task performance, and that output validity, rather than predictive accuracy, is the binding constraint on further progress.


### arXiv 2611.27175 - Felid: A Flexible and Efficient Design for Transformer Fine-Tuning over Encrypted Data

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T06:02:35Z
- authors: Linru Zhang, Jun Jie Sim, Xiangning Wang, Jiahao Zhong, Kaiyu Zhou, Huanyi Ye
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27175

Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables non-interactive, privacy-preserving transformer fine-tuning, protecting highly sensitive data as inputs to large language models. Prior work has predominantly pursued efficiency gains through architectural refinements, yet overall performance remains bounded by the substantial cost inherent in encrypted computation. To address these issues, we target the bottleneck at the ciphertext operation level. Concretely, we propose a novel packing strategy that supports batching over a flexible number of inputs, making it well-suited for fine-tuning workloads. In addition, we design operation-specific matrix evaluation algorithms that significantly reduce (i) the number of ciphertext rotations and relinearizations, and (ii) the number of bootstrapping invocations. Additionally, we exploit functional bootstrapping to fold the evaluation of element-wise activation functions directly into the bootstrapping step, thereby reducing overall computational complexity. As a result, our framework achieves a $30\times$ reduction in ciphertext rotations and relinearizations across all matrix operations, as well as a $2.5\times$ reduction in FHE bootstrapping invocations over the entire computation flow, compared to the latest state-of-the-art work. It is worth noting that our system completes fine-tuning of a 2-layer BERT-style model with a batch size of 16 in 650 seconds, achieving a $8\times$ speedup over the state-of-the-art work.


### arXiv 2611.27159 - From Acting to Learning: Experience Selection toward Continually Learning Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-20T05:45:48Z
- authors: Yaoyuan Yan, Haoxiang Jie, Zhiyou Heng, Kailin Wang, Yumiao Li, Lifeng Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27159

Successful agent interactions provide training data, but success alone does not determine which records a student should learn from. We examine experience selection for a 4B language agent as a component of continual agent learning. Nine selectors are compared across two fixed experience pools, eight initialization seeds, and two sampling seeds, yielding 288 trained students. Each student is evaluated on 128 development task groups with a common executor. With equal global selection freedom, 256 distinct contents, and 768 training presentations, optimizer-aware gradient selection reaches 81.45\% and 73.19\% success in the two pools. Its gains over global random selection are 8.25 and 5.96 percentage points; gains over global high-NLL selection are 4.10 and 4.05 points. All eight initialization-level contrasts are positive against both baselines in each pool. Relaxing coverage constraints improves gradient selection but reduces random-selection performance, showing that the ranking rule and selection space must be considered together. A separately frozen replication on 128 reserved task groups is complete; its equal-source gradient-minus-random and gradient-minus-high-NLL differences are +7.03 and +5.03 points, respectively.


### arXiv 2611.27156 - Co-Evolving Behavioral Agents and Detectors for Adaptive Information Operations

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:41:17Z
- authors: Lin Tian, Marian-Andrei Rizoiu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27156

Detectors of state-sponsored information operations (IOs) are trained on campaigns that platforms have already taken down. They lose much of their accuracy on an actor they have not seen, even though no operator in the archived data is trying to avoid them. A real operator wants two things, to reach an audience and to avoid being caught. We present IO-AI, a framework that gives simulated agents these two capabilities, engagement and evasion, and tests what each one does to detection. The agents are built from the posting behavior of real campaigns and choose the emotion, type, and timing of each post. An agent and a detector then train against each other in turn. We rank the trained agents by how hard they are to detect and train one detector on them from easiest to hardest. On 20 Twitter IO campaigns, this detector reduces missed detections of adaptive agents by 65% relative to a detector trained only on the archived campaigns, at a similar false-alarm rate. On actors it has never seen it reaches an F1 of 0.81, against 0.62. We find that an agent trained to seek engagement becomes easier to catch, because it learns the fast, emotional retweeting that archived campaigns were caught producing. An agent that was trained for engagement before the game keeps its engagement lead after it learns to evade. We also introduce a behavioral distance metric that compares two groups of accounts using only their unlabeled posting histories. The larger the distance between two groups, the worse a detector trained on one does on the other (Spearman rho = -0.58), so the metric can be used to choose which new groups to test first.


### arXiv 2611.27149 - Where Structure Enters a World Model: From Predictive Fit to Physical Rollout

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:31:08Z
- authors: Panayiotis Christou, Satyapragnya Kar, Flavjo Xhelollari, Parijat Dube, Rajarshi Das
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27149

Predictive world models learn to forecast observations, but accurate short-term predictions do not necessarily produce physically coherent behavior over longer horizons. We investigate how incorporating physical structure can strengthen world models while retaining their underlying predictive objectives. Across token-based models of planetary motion and JEPA-style visual models of mechanical systems, we study conserved-quantity supervision, symmetry-aware representations, temporal consistency, and learned structured dynamics. In planetary motion, angular-momentum supervision improves autonomous next-token forecasting, while the benefits of temporal regularization depend on the accompanying physical objective and representation. In visual models, generating rollouts through supervised energy-based dynamics heads produces more faithful trajectories and phase-space evolution. Together, these findings highlight the importance of how physical structure participates in prediction: improvements in an auxiliary physical readout or a local acceleration estimate do not automatically translate into more reliable long-horizon behavior. Evaluations across initial conditions and physical parameter shifts further reveal where these benefits persist and where generalization remains limited. Our study supports physical structure as a practical means of improving predictive world models and provides a unified framework for understanding its effects on representations, learned transitions, and autonomous rollouts. The central lesson is that effective physical inductive biases should help models both represent physical regularities and use them to generate future states.


### arXiv 2611.27148 - Structure Over Scale: Rethinking Adaptation for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:30:43Z
- authors: Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27148

Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is often framed as a choice between the expressivity of Full Fine-Tuning (FFT) and the efficiency of Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). We find that this framing misses an important variable: the geometry of the update. Under a common cold-start GRPO protocol, LoRA exhibits rank collapse: performance remains broadly stable at low rank, then fails abruptly as rank increases. On DeepMath-Hard, for example, accuracy drops from 73.1% at r=64 to 4.7% at r=128. In contrast, Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation (DoRA) and Quantum-Informed Tensor Adaptation (QuanTA) remain stable over a wider tested optimization region and are competitive with FFT while updating less than 0.6% of the model parameters. Spectral analysis reveals a corresponding difference in learned update structure: collapsed LoRA concentrates its update energy into relatively few effective directions, whereas structured adapters produce substantially more distributed updates. These results suggest that nominal rank and parameter count alone are insufficient to characterize adaptation in RLVR - the structure imposed on the reachable update manifold also matters. Finally, across three 8B base models, adapter behavior aligns with both frozen-weight spectral structure and reported pre-training scale, motivating the Model Maturity Hypothesis: the adaptation geometry that works best may depend on properties already encoded during pre-training. We present this as a falsifiable hypothesis rather than a causal conclusion. The code can be found here https://github.com/Shrimpstanot/structure-over-scale


### arXiv 2611.27142 - How Does SAM-3 Adapt? Capacity, Update Geometry, and Domain Transfer

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:22:06Z
- authors: Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat, Duygu Cakir
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27142

Promptable segmentation foundation models are increasingly used in specialized domains, but it remains unclear how SAM-3 should be fine-tuned when adapting it beyond its original training distribution. We study this question by comparing five adaptation families --LoRA, HiRA, KRAdapter, QuanTA, and Full Fine-Tuning-- across five segmentation datasets spanning medical imaging, remote sensing, and dense material segmentation. Across these datasets, compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods often match or approach Full Fine-Tuning in DSC and IoU, suggesting that strong in-domain adaptation does not always require updating the full model. However, this performance cannot be explained by nominal adapter rank or trainable parameter count alone. To investigate why different methods perform similarly, we analyze their update spectra and their behavior under LoveDA Urban/Rural transfer. Our SVD analysis shows that Full Fine-Tuning updates are spectrally compressible. However, successful PEFT methods do not simply recover the same update directions; instead, they often concentrate changes in different components and subspaces. The domain-transfer study further shows that methods with similar in-domain performance can respond differently under distribution shift. Together, these results suggest that SAM-3 adaptation is often capacity-saturated in practice, while competitive fine-tuning arises through method-specific update paths rather than a single low-rank approximation to Full Fine-Tuning.


### arXiv 2611.27139 - Do Senior Agents Do Their Job? Measuring Role Integrity in How LLM Agents Use Subordinate Material

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:19:59Z
- authors: Jinxiang Xie, Xinyi Zhang, Changjiang Jiang, Kexu Liu, Qi Yang, Yuxin Su
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27139

Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly divide long tasks among subagents whose reports a senior agent turns into the final answer. Such a division of labor pays off only if each agent, and above all the senior agent, carries out the duties of its role. Current evaluations, however, mostly score the final answer, and a fluent, well-cited answer cannot show whether the senior agent weighed its subagents' reports or merely aggregated them. To assess whether a senior agent meets its assigned responsibilities, we define role integrity as the preservation of an agent's functional responsibilities when it acts on its subordinates' material. To measure it, we introduce RI-Bench, a behavioral benchmark grounded in the structures and public records of three institutional hierarchies: peer review, appellate adjudication, and parliamentary inquiry. We choose these hierarchies because their rules explicitly assign the senior role a duty toward subordinate material, and together they cover three basic duties: to weigh, to verify, and to relay. Rather than scoring output correctness, we measure how senior agents' outputs respond to controlled changes in subordinate material, using rule-based checks and a rubric-guided LLM judge calibrated against human annotation. Across eight LLMs and over 12,000 agentic episodes, senior agents rarely adopt a planted procedural error (2.9% of rulings) but often follow a majority of generic reviews (up to 71% of decisions) or drop witnesses' stated limitations (up to 65% of cited claims). Obligation prompts reduce these failures without removing them, and they also shift behavior in the control conditions. These findings motivate direct, obligation-specific evaluation of role integrity rather than inferring it from general capability or explicit role instructions. Code and data are available at https://github.com/jxtse/ri-bench.


### arXiv 2611.27138 - Double counting in LLM evidence fusion: exact posteriors separate rule compliance from posterior gain

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-20T05:19:40Z
- authors: Tengfei Shao, Asma Rezaei, Ali Reza Kamali
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27138

Language models fuse reports in retrieval, multi-agent consensus and agent memory, where treating shared-source reports as independent can double count evidence. A common remedy is an instruction to count each source once. Its value depends on the receiver's raw baseline. Pricing it needs an exact reference. We build the graded-dependence exact reference: binary worlds with single-report reliability 0.65 and four dependence levels, whose disclosed parameters give every world an exact posterior and every rule a target risk. The paired gain protocol scores raw and instructed answers of four configurations on 2,976 worlds. Agreement and gain separate in both directions. At $δ$ = 0, claude-opus-5-5 and gpt-6.1-sol agreed with the rule within 0.001 in 744 of 744, and the instruction raised their posterior mismatch by 0.044; gpt-6-luna agreed in 165 of 744 and gained 0.0140 (95% interval 0.0064 to 0.0219); claude-haiku-5-5's gain moved from -0.0295 ($δ$ = 0) to +0.0302 ($δ$ = 1) (descriptive). The prespecified first-child endpoint contrast, gain at $δ$ = 1 minus $δ$ = 0, was 0.0342 (0.0303 to 0.0382). In the preregistered tests, the raw source-weight contrast was positive (0.259, 95% interval 0.182 to 0.337; Holm rejected zero) and the grouping endpoint difference was not detected (0.0004, -0.0023 to 0.0031). This interval excludes differences larger than 0.0031 in magnitude and lies within the registered precision bound of $\pm$0.0046. The reference prices the rule before any model call. The paired gain, estimable as a Brier difference on labelled deployment items, decides adoption per receiver and prompt condition.


### arXiv 2611.27133 - MAPES: Multi-Value Alignment Pipeline via Expert Specialization

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T05:07:54Z
- authors: SungHo Kim, SangKeun Lee
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27133

Aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences requires simultaneously satisfying multiple, often competing values. Existing preference optimization methods collapse these into a single implicit signal, and even multi-objective extensions optimize within a shared parameter space, leaving cross-value conflicts unresolved. We propose MAPES, a multi-value alignment paradigm in which conflict resolution is a first-class concern at every pipeline stage: it first decomposes supervision into value-specific experts trained in isolated parameter subspaces, then recomposes them via preference-aware value merging and a lightweight harmonization stage that aligns the merged model with the expert ensemble. Across five preference alignment benchmarks, MAPES outperforms strong direct preference optimization (DPO) variants and multi-objective baselines on the benchmark-level average, with no large degradation on GSM8K and GPQA; matched controls isolate the contribution of each stage.


### arXiv 2611.27125 - When Students Fail to Learn from Teachers: Learning Dynamics of On-Policy Distillation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T04:50:20Z
- authors: Xinnan Zhang, Zeyi Liao, Siliang Zeng, Zhongruo Wang, Jiaxiang Li, Xinyan Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27125

On-policy distillation (OPD) has become a core technique for post-training large language models, enabling students to learn from dense token-level supervision on their own generations. However, even with a capable teacher, OPD training can be unstable, leading to excessive response length, repetitive generation, and severe performance degradation. One example is \textit{spurious agreement}, where the student maintains high overlap with the teacher at the states visited but produces repetitive generations. Although such failures have been observed, the mechanisms behind these failures remain poorly understood. To address this gap, we investigate OPD through the lens of learning dynamics, tracing three mechanisms from local analysis to subsequent on-policy sampling. We first identify states where sampled-token OPD updates fail to correct teacher--student mismatch and characterize them using the student's mass on the teacher's top-$p$ set. We then show that learning from the teacher is difficult at mismatched states and that their gradients can also affect with learning at other tokens. Finally, using a termination-token mismatch as a case study, we study how continued training on mismatched states influences on-policy sampling, suppressing the student's current modes and leading the student toward states where the teacher is unreliable . These findings help explain the benefits of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) initialization and motivate targeted correction of states where student and teacher are mismatched.


### arXiv 2611.27121 - Portable Active Recovery under Timed and Position-Dependent Visual Outages

- categories: cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-20T04:47:52Z
- authors: Jinyuan Zhang, Shengshuo Jiao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27121

When a camera blackout depends on physical position rather than elapsed time, stable hovering prevents visual recovery and mission completion. On stereo visual-inertial quadrotors without external localization, an exogenous timed blackout and an endogenous position-dependent dark zone appear identical at loss onset yet require opposite responses: hover-and-wait completes 93/96 timed blackouts but 0/96 spatial outages (13 hard failures), whereas blind continuation traverses many spatial zones yet fails heavily during timed blackouts. We formulate visual-outage recovery as a worst-condition active-observability problem and introduce a portable recovery module at the reference interface of an unchanged host controller with zero retraining. Using deployable telemetry alone, the module coordinates visual-loss detection, jerk-limited braking, bounded backward probing, settled rejoining, height-integral trim, and an optional gated prior-map branch. On a preregistered 48-scene benchmark (1,920 flights), our full MPPI configuration reaches 88.5% [81.2, 94.8] worst-condition completion, outperforming development-selected DATT-L1 by +29.2 pp [17.7, 40.6] and exceeding all six external baselines after Bonferroni correction. Attached without retraining to SimpleFlight, the module lifts worst-condition completion by +17.7 pp [7.3, 28.1] to 86.5% (and 90% across 1.0x/1.25x speeds, 0/80 hard failures). Trace diagnostics reveal that survival hinges on visual-inertial re-acquisition asymmetry at vision return -- not blind IMU drift suppression - and uncover a bias-drift map crossover confirmed on 72 held-out EuRoC windows.


### arXiv 2611.27116 - CoEvoBench: Can LLMs Drive Recursive Co-Evolution of Agentic Systems?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T04:40:59Z
- authors: Chuan Xiao, Baojie Wang, Haizhao Fan, Hongzhe Du, Xander Xu
