- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27116

The performance of an LLM agent depends on both its model and its harness, the code that mediates its interaction with tools and environments. Can LLM researchers drive this co-evolution as a recursive improvement process, deciding round after round which component to change when each change alters the value of the other? Existing benchmarks evaluate post-training or data research with the harness out of scope, or harness optimization with the model fixed, leaving this joint decision unmeasured. We introduce CoEvoBench, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs as researchers co-evolving the model and harness of a target agent across five agentic domains. Researchers start from a common base model and initial harness, train and revise within a 24-hour budget, and submit a model-harness pair that is scored on separate test tasks. Across five frontier researchers, the strongest closes more than four fifths of the gap to an instruction-tuned reference system, and co-evolution succeeds on interaction tasks but rarely on long-horizon coding. These results suggest that current LLMs can make a strong first improvement, but cannot yet turn feedback from an improved system into the next one. CoEvoBench provides a controlled testbed for measuring the research capabilities that recursive co-evolution requires.


### arXiv 2611.27106 - Schema-Validated LLM Workflows: A Benchmark Protocol and Comparative Evaluation Across Six Python Implementations

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T04:32:13Z
- authors: Sidney Shapiro, Sanad Al-Khatib, Aurora Pinzón Arzola, Emiliano Sebastian Gonzalez Venegas, Daniel Pearson
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27106

Large language models are increasingly used to extract records, generate SQL, summarize reports, answer policy questions, and route operational work. At these software boundaries, parseable output is necessary but does not establish task correctness. We present a reproducible benchmark protocol, a public executable artifact, and a confirmatory live API evaluation that separate structural validity from task-specific correctness across five business workflow tasks and six Python implementation conditions: PydanticAI, LangGraph, DSPy, CrewAI, Agno, and a framework-free SDK reference. We hypothesize that (H1) schema validity and task quality are separable, (H2) structured-output binding paths are heterogeneous under a shared model and lowest-common-denominator contract, and (H3) condition-by-task interactions concentrate failures rather than shifting all tasks equally. Confirmatory analysis uses distinct task instances (100 extraction and 50 per other task, with three nested replicates) under a frozen analysis plan, yielding a 5400-row primary matrix with equivalence margins and Holm-adjusted contrasts against the SDK reference. In the eligible confirmatory run, overall schema validity was 0.684 and mean automated quality was 0.638. PydanticAI, LangGraph, and DSPy retained near-ceiling schema validity with mean quality 0.91-0.94, while the plain SDK, Agno, and CrewAI conditions were substantially lower in this package and model configuration. Schema-valid rows still produced many task-score flags, especially in extraction and compliance QA, and quality residuals show strong condition-by-task interactions (including a workflow collapse for CrewAI). Labelled ablations address retry budgets and feature-using mechanisms; primary contrasts remain system-configuration findings, not causal attributions to marketed library features.


### arXiv 2611.27102 - One state, many questions: Opsis gives a frontier model typed eyes for pathology

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T04:23:39Z
- authors: Zhixuan Ge, Wei Qiu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27102

A synoptic pathology report is a checklist of questions with fixed answer sets, many composed from others. Pathology foundation models answer them accurately with one classifier trained with labels for each, and frontier language models, which compose freely, cannot fit a slide of fourteen thousand tiles in their context. On 189 breast report questions, frontier models that read a written description of the slide or look at a thumbnail and tiles of it answer at 0.50 to 0.66, below the majority class at 0.67. A description is written before the question is known, and a look covers a handful of tiles. We present Opsis, which lets the model ask instead: a typed question interface on a frozen pathology encoder. A slide is encoded once, a question names an operator, arguments and candidates given at runtime, and the answer is a probability over the candidates. We build it as a 10 M parameter decoder on frozen Virchow2 tokens (Opsis-S) and inside Qwen3-VL-8B (Opsis-V). Asking Opsis-S, four frontier models answer the breast questions at 0.78 to 0.79, and on 3,797 pan cancer questions asking is 8.6 points above reading. On 995 composite questions that Opsis-S was never trained on, a frontier model composing its answers reaches 0.76, against 0.69 when it looks at the slide and 0.78 for ABMIL trained on each composite. On a pan cancer question set over 10,712 TCGA slides, one Opsis-S decoder matches linear probes trained per question on TITAN and PRISM2 embeddings (average accuracy 0.69 against 0.70 and 0.68). Opsis-S also separates CAMELYON16 metastases without training on that cohort (AUROC 0.86) and localises necrosis from report labels alone (tile AUROC 0.93).


### arXiv 2611.27100 - Reasoning in Two Dimensions: Quantifying Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness and Efficiency

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T04:19:00Z
- authors: Chenruo Liu, Quan Zhang, Qi Lei
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27100

Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning exposes intermediate steps, but a correct answer does not establish that an LLM relies on the dependence expressed by those steps or reasons efficiently. We operationalize CoT faithfulness and efficiency within a common framework. Specifically, we represent a CoT as a semantic reasoning graph and decompose the model's predictive dependence along two complementary axes: dependence explained by semantic support versus additional reasoning history, and dependence allocated to answer-reaching versus off-path reasoning. This decomposition yields global and step-level measures of unfaithfulness and inefficiency. Controlled interventions show that the proposed metrics respond to hidden dependence and induced off-path reasoning as intended. Across mathematical and multi-hop reasoning tasks, answer-level unfaithfulness is consistently associated with greater predictive uncertainty, whereas both unfaithfulness and inefficiency exhibit weak or inconsistent associations with answer correctness. We further derive theoretically grounded, graph-aware fine-tuning objectives that improve the targeted reasoning properties without directly optimizing the computationally expensive metrics or compromising answer accuracy. Results indicate that accuracy and brevity provide incomplete assessments of reasoning quality: reliable CoT evaluation should also consider whether the LLM's predictive dependence is faithfully represented and efficiently allocated.


### arXiv 2611.27095 - Epistemic Guard: Out-of-Process MCP Receipt Proxying, Post-Turn Prose Supervision, and Provenance-Gated Memory for Tool-Using Autonomous Agents

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-20T04:10:02Z
- authors: Sean Maxwell
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27095

Language-model agents using the Model Context Protocol (MCP) face three runtime verification gaps: (i) action fabrication, where post-tool prose narrates side effects that failed, timed out, or never ran; (ii) provenance collapse, where unverified model prose or web text enters durable memory alongside executed tool outputs; and (iii) syndicated echo amplification, where mirrored web pages satisfy multi-source corroboration from one origin. Existing MCP firewalls inspect wire calls before execution, while LLM-judge guardrails require multi-gigabyte VRAM without binding prose to receipts. We present epistemic-guard (v0.5.2), an open-source Python library and out-of-process stdio MCP receipt proxy (15 modules, 0 MiB GPU VRAM). epistemic-guard isolates Ed25519 signing keys out of process, writes RFC 6962 Merkle logs with AER-1 receipt translation, checks post-turn prose on CPU with SHA-256-verified OpenVINO INT8 encoders (4 models, 279.48 MB total; 82.67 MB on the supervision path), gates SQLite writes across 7 provenance domains, and collapses verbatim and lightly edited wire-syndicated RAG mirrors. On conformance suites, epistemic-guard rejects 100.0% of tampered receipts (200 Merkle leaves), passes 58/58 official AER-1 ingestion vectors and 50/50 reference-verified AER-1 exports, verifies 100.0% of 40 fault-injected wire calls and 100 concurrent appends with 0 key leaks, and blocks 100.0% of 100 unauthorized memory writes. Across $N=250$ frozen-benchmark traces, epistemic-guard reaches 93.2% accuracy at 17.9 ms warm median CPU latency (8.62 ms single-clause; 921.8 ms cold-start load), beating 12B-14B GPU judges by 6.4-6.8 points ($p=0.0259$, $p=0.0095$) at 4.26-4.5$\times$ lower latency; on $N=60$ live mcp-proxy traces, it catches 96.15% of hazards at 76.67% accuracy (38.24% FPR; $+18.34$ points over Stage-1-only, $p=0.001$) in 9.16 ms p50.


### arXiv 2611.27094 - Separating Automation Gains from Agent Contributions in AI-Assisted Model Validation

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-20T04:09:01Z
- authors: M. Ahmadi Achachlouei
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27094

LLM agents are being proposed for bank model validation, yet evaluations that compare agent-assisted work with manual practice credit the agent with gains that come from digitized evidence and deterministic tests. We propose a staged architecture in which an LLM-free controlled workflow (P1) is both the fallback and the control arm for individually authorized agent tasks (P2), and an attribution protocol that credits an agent only for faults P1 does not already report. The protocol charges decoy false alarms, unnecessary actions and endorsed developer assertions, counts invalid outputs as misses, folds newly automatable findings back into P1 with discovery credit, and fixes safety bounds and a break-even cost gate in advance. On a synthetic credit-scorecard harness, five scripted reference policies show why this matters: naive case accuracy (4/6) and pooled detection (3/5) tie four flawed policies, whereas the protocol separates all five and credits only the reference policy with detection beyond P1. One fold-back round raises a P1-parroting policy's naive scores without any change to the policy. A regression matrix of sixteen mutants covers nine ways in which earlier versions of the evaluation passed misleadingly. No LLM agent has been evaluated yet; we specify a falsifiable pilot.


### arXiv 2611.27090 - Exercised, Rarely Recovered: What Public Reports Say About Recovery in 50 AI Agent Harnesses and Runtimes

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T04:05:35Z
- authors: Kaviru Hapuarachchi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27090

When an AI coding agent deletes the wrong files or a long agent run crashes, users fall back on recovery mechanisms: resume from a checkpoint, undo, ask for approval, read the log. Research runtimes promise stronger guarantees, such as saga-style compensation and transactional rollback. We ask what public evidence shows about whether either kind works in real use. Under a protocol frozen before data collection, we graded 12 persistent agent runtimes, 26 coding-agent harnesses and 12 research runtimes on eight recovery mechanisms, from not found to exercised in a real run, with two blind coding passes, adjudication and re-verification against archived sources. Recovery is exercised almost everywhere in practice: 11 of 12 runtimes and 25 of 26 harnesses have at least one real-use report. Yet of 128 such reports, only 19 describe the run fully recovering; 54 are partial and 45 failed. Checkpoint/resume recovered once in 27 reports, rollback once in 15. Research runtimes show the opposite profile: guarantees evaluated on benchmarks, none reported in operation, and compensation of external effects, their central guarantee, is offered by no harness at all.


### arXiv 2611.27089 - Writing Weights or Training Them? Spatial Reasoning through Weight-Space Programming and Low-Rank Adaptation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T04:05:14Z
- authors: Yalin Yang, Xiao Huang, Yanan Wu, Hao Yang, Ryan Zhenqi Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27089

Weight-space programming lets a language model inspect and edit another model's weights, offering an alternative to gradient-based adaptation. We evaluated both approaches using Qwen3.5-0.8B on a fixed reference-frame task (128 items), StepGame (200 items), and two untrained distance-comparison tasks. With thinking enabled, the public patch (p50c) raised mean accuracy from 0.0% to 22.1% on the reference-frame task and from 19.2% to 33.7% on StepGame across three sampling seeds. Randomly permuting its weight change removed the gain. Further closed-form edits did not improve on p50c, whereas a rank-1 LoRA adapter trained on worked solutions reached 100% and 98.8%, respectively, with 0.09% trainable parameters. Removing the worked solutions from supervision prevented the adapter from passing the learning gate. The trained adapters reached 82% accuracy on unseen StepGame sentence templates, 42% on held-out difficulty levels 6-10 after training on levels 1-5, and 33-50% on unseen heading-answer combinations. Their target gains coincided with a sharp decline on the distance tasks: accuracy fell from 94% and 92% under p50c to 0-2%, and their outputs applied the StepGame solution template to distance questions. With thinking disabled and reasoning requested in the visible answer, the base model also solved the distance tasks while remaining weak on the target tasks. The comparison reveals a trade-off between improving spatial task performance and preserving neighbouring skills: the public edit restored completion while preserving distance performance, whereas supervised adaptation produced much larger target gains together with interference across tasks.


### arXiv 2611.27080 - Fooling the Mind Twice: Human Detection of Audiovisual Deepfakes Under Repeated Exposure and Corrective Feedback

- categories: cs.MM, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T03:47:39Z
- authors: Ammarah Hashmi, Sahibzada Adil Shahzad, Yu Tsao, Hsin-Min Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27080

Human deepfake detection remains unreliable, and whether reassessment with corrective feedback improves subsequent judgments remains unclear. This study examined differences between initial and repeated deepfake assessments among 110 participants using audiovisual stimuli from the FakeAVCeleb and Deepfake Detection Challenge (DFDC) datasets. Participants evaluated 40 videos from each dataset in phase 1 (PhaseI) and reassessed the same videos in phase 2 (PhaseII) under a procedure combining repeated presentation and corrective feedback. For the FakeAVCeleb dataset, the participants' mean accuracy increased by 4.68 percentage points from 63.30 in PhaseI to 67.98 in PhaseII (t(109)=5.61, p<.001, d_z=0.54). A judgment-level logistic regression with standard errors clustered by participant and video produced a consistent result: beta=0.208, SE=0.040, z=5.23, p<.001, {OR}=1.23. For the DFDC dataset, the participants' mean accuracy increased by 8.64 percentage points from 59.52% in PhaseI to 68.16% in PhaseII (t(109)=7.37, p<.001, d_z=0.70); the logistic model yielded: beta=0.375, SE=0.065, z=5.78, p<.001, {OR}=1.46. Improvements primarily came from fewer false negatives, while false-positive rates remained unchanged. However, judgment reversals occurred in 27.55% of FakeAVCeleb and 32.91% of DFDC assessments, with some initially correct judgments becoming incorrect upon reassessment. Despite the accuracy gains, repeated assessment did not eliminate judgment instability or residual error. Participants also exhibited consistent overconfidence, with mean self-rated confidence exceeding actual accuracy by 11.99 percentage points for FakeAVCeleb and 12.82 percentage points for DFDC across both phases. These findings indicate that repetition with corrective feedback moderately improves deepfake detection but does not fully resolve the limitations of human judgment.


### arXiv 2611.27079 - Weight-space programming and mechanistic interpretability for model editing in particle physics

- categories: hep-ph, cs.AI, cs.LG, hep-ex
- published: 2026-11-20T03:46:32Z
- authors: Raphaël Bonnet-Guerrini, Johann Ioannou-Nikolaides, Troels Petersen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27079

We demonstrate that weight-space programming and edits guided by sparse autoencoders can remove unwanted aspects of a particle-physics foundation model through direct edits to its weights. In a foundation model trained on jets, we observe that adding a single soft neutral particle causes large changes in jet classification, violating infrared safety. We locate the responsible attention heads with mechanistic analysis and trace the flaw's origin to pretraining on datasets with different reconstruction thresholds. We then propose two targeted approaches to repair the flaw: by removing three latent directions identified with sparse autoencoders, and by letting language-model agents edit the weights directly, without gradients and with only 50 queries to the model. Both their edits individually remove almost the entire flaw and leave the rest of the model's response nearly unchanged. Compared to fine-tuning, both methods change far fewer parameters. Although built only on $W$ and QCD jets, the edits carry over to all signal classes, the pretrained generator, and a newly trained regression head.


### arXiv 2611.27068 - Decision Models Are General-Purpose Process Reward Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T03:36:18Z
- authors: Jiajun Fan, Qinglong Zheng, Ziwen Wang, Bangji Yang, Neil He, Ruihan Guo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27068

Reinforcement learning (RL) from verifiable outcomes gives a language agent one reward per episode: every step is credited alike, and nothing about the task is learned when all rollouts fail. The last mile, step-level credit, usually requires step labels, a learned critic, costly LLM judges or hand-written rules. We introduce DM-PRM, which turns a general-purpose decision model -- one answering yes/no questions with probabilities -- into a general-purpose process reward model (PRM), zero-shot: no step labels, no fine-tuning, no critic. DM-PRM asks one question per step and adds the standardised answers to the advantage. It plugs into any policy-gradient method; one model, with one question text per environment, serves household tasks, web shopping and multiple RL methods. With Qwen2.5-1.5B it lifts GRPO by over twenty points on ALFWorld and over ten on WebShop, overtakes GiGPO on ALFWorld and learns in far fewer iterations; it also improves GiGPO and GraphGPO on both benchmarks and reaches the highest success of our comparison, ahead of critic-based PPO and of state-of-the-art GiGPO, HGPO and GraphGPO. Its scores locate useful steps in failed rollouts, and trained agents drop idle actions and explore instead of circling. DM-PRM shows that step credit need not be learned: it can be asked for.


### arXiv 2611.27064 - Dynamic Token Merging for Efficient Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T03:30:28Z
- authors: Chris Gu, Marco Andono Sie, Nathan Zhou, Christopher Potts, Julie Kallini
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27064

Vision-language models (VLMs) represent each image with hundreds of visual tokens, which can make inference costly even when a prompt depends on only part of the image. Dynamic Token Merging (DTM), introduced in MrT5 for byte-level text encoders, learns which tokens to remove: a lightweight delete gate scores contextualized hidden states, acts as a differentiable attention bias during training, and physically removes tokens at inference. We argue that DTM is a general token deletion framework rather than a byte-level technique, and extend it to the vision modality and to decoder-only models. Applying DTM to T5Gemma 2 and Gemma 3 yields V-MrT5Gemma2, which deletes visual tokens inside a bidirectional multimodal encoder, and V-MrGemma3, which compacts the prompt and its key-value caches during decoder-only prefill. Across ten benchmarks, DTM's advantage over attention-based and random deletion grows with the deletion rate and is largest on document, chart, and scene-text reading. At 80% visual token deletion (approximately 60% fewer FLOPs), DTM raises accuracy over FastV from 51.8 to 63.0 on ChartQA and from 63.3 to 67.8 on TextVQA with V-MrT5Gemma2, as well as from 62.2 to 68.8 and from 71.2 to 75.1 with V-MrGemma3, retaining 90-96% of unpruned performance on both tasks with significant efficiency gains. These results support DTM as a general framework for efficient transformers.


### arXiv 2611.27061 - PACE: Policy Augmentation and Compensation With Explicit Disturbance Estimation for Robust Locomotion

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T03:28:57Z
- authors: Shixuan Han, Yuyang Wang, Jingting Jia, Zeshuai Chen, Jindou Jia, Kexin Guo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27061

Reinforcement learning has made quadrupedal locomotion remarkably robust. To handle unmodeled dynamics and external disturbances, domain randomization is usually employed to make a policy hedge against every condition it might meet. However, maintaining accurate tracking across varying dynamics and generalizing beyond the sampled range remain challenging. Here we show that explicitly estimating the disturbance and feeding the resulting estimation back into both the policy and the controller can relax this compromise. We introduce Policy Augmentation and Compensation with Explicit disturbance estimation (PACE). The framework lumps payload variations, center-of-mass (CoM) shifts, actuator degradation, and external forces into one joint-space disturbance. A nonlinear observer subsequently recovers this disturbance online without requiring joint acceleration measurements. The disturbance estimation provides feedforward torque compensation as the foundational control pathway and further augments the policy observation to enable disturbance-aware action generation. Considering the learning difficulty of fast-varying contact effects, we further provide an analytical contact estimation approach from joint and inertial measurements, without demanding force sensors. Across eleven simulated dynamics conditions, PACE reduces velocity-tracking error by 18.1% to 57.0% against state-of-the-art baselines, and maintains improved tracking under out-of-distribution (OOD) commands. Notably, in real-world experiments, the deployed real robot carried an 11.5 kg payload, nearly equal to its own mass. It also negotiated unseen 16 cm step-downs and crossed five outdoor terrains. The policy was trained exclusively on flat ground in simulation with at most 3 kg of added mass.


### arXiv 2611.27057 - Learning 3D MRI Representations with Generative Adversarial Networks

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T03:25:32Z
- authors: Sujeet Lahane, Sapna Mishra, Akash Suryavanshi, Tapan Gandhi, Bharat Biswal
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27057

Learning representations of visual images is a challenging task. It requires extracting abstract patterns from high-dimensional data without explicit supervision. This becomes pronounced with 3D MR imaging due to sparse features, higher dimensional inputs, and smaller datasets. Many state-of-the-art representation learning algorithms successfully rely on transformers and diffusion-based architectures. However, these methods struggle with smaller datasets. In effect, solution methods relax the problem by slicing 3D volumes and solving the problem for 2D medical imaging, resulting in a loss of spatial 3D information. We present R2RG, a representation learning method based on Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) honing the reconstruction task. Our key insights lie in exploiting architectural biases in 3D CNNs, combined with data-efficient auto-encoding GANs and a composite loss function to enhance reconstruction fidelity and stabilize training. Our experiments on three standard datasets and a locally collected dataset show that R2RG not only achieves superior performance but also generalizes to heterogeneous and smaller datasets. Furthermore, representations learned with R2RG outperform those learned with contrastive-based representation learning methods on Parkinson's and Alzheimer's disease classification. Our work demonstrates that reconstruction-based representation learning is a promising direction for 3D medical imaging under limited data availability.


### arXiv 2611.27056 - A Continuous Relaxation of Quantization-Aware Training

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-20T03:24:54Z
- authors: Kartikeya Bhardwaj, Mohammed Almousa, Chaitanya Mullapudi, Ananth Balasubramanian, Nithin Ramesan, Tuc Nguyen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27056

Quantization is a critical piece of the deployment pipeline in the era of large generative models. Low-bit quantization, particularly 2-bits or sub-2-bits, is a notoriously difficult problem, particularly when using a channelwise quantization scheme which has been shown as a leading candidate for hardware-efficiency. The key challenges with low-bit quantization range from limited quantization levels to saddle points or flat regions in the loss surface. In this paper, we look at the problem of flat quantization loss surfaces through a lens of dynamically changing bitwidth. Unlike existing techniques which either change the bit-precision abruptly or keep it fixed, we propose to gradually alter the bitwidth as the model trains. To this end, we propose RelaxQAT, a continuous relaxation of the QAT process. We also provide simple theoretical and empirical insights behind why RelaxQAT helps the QAT performance. Our extensive experiments demonstrate up to 6.3% average accuracy gains on vision and up to 7.7% better average accuracy on natural language tasks, compared to the leading QAT methods.


### arXiv 2611.27725 - A Cognitive Agentic AI Framework for Interactive Process Analysis and Optimization

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T15:25:07Z
- authors: Trung Ngoc Trinha, Roman Tschentscher, Trong Dong Nguyen, Trung Thanh Nguyen, Anna Trubetskaya
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27725

Complex multivariable process analysis and optimization require coordinated access to simulation, structured data, machine learning (ML), optimization, and scientific literature, often across disconnected software tools. We present a Cognitive Agentic AI Framework (CAAF) that enables interactive, iterative process investigation through natural-language prompts within an established flowsheet. Following harness-engineering principles, CAAF assigns task interpretation, planning, knowledge synthesis, and context management to a large language model while delegating numerical calculations to specialized tools. Dedicated providers connect databases, scientific-document retrieval, ML-assisted Bayesian optimization (BO), and COCO process simulation. The framework is demonstrated on methyl acetate production. Guided by successive prompts, an engineer explored multivariable relationships, conducted 4,000 BO iterations to maximize carbon conversion yield (C-Yield) and minimize specific energy cost (C-SEC), refined operating bounds, and verified selected candidates through process simulation. Re-simulation of 16 candidates within the restricted domain showed close agreement with ML predictions, with a mean absolute C-Yield error of 0.0085 (0.85 percentage points). The highest BO-derived C-Yield, verified by COCO, was 0.921 versus approximately 0.81 in the initial dataset, an improvement of about 11 percentage points. CAAF also detects and reports non-converged simulations, excludes invalid outputs, and continues subsequent evaluations. The results demonstrate how human-guided agentic coordination can combine engineering tools for iterative analysis and optimization with rigorous simulation-based verification under a fixed process topology.


### arXiv 2611.27545 - Epistemic Social Learning: Latent Behavioral Structure under Endogenous Multi-Agent Interaction

- categories: cs.MA, cs.GT
- published: 2026-11-20T12:40:47Z
- authors: Jainendra Shukla, Dhruv Jaiswal, Divyanshi Beniwal, Kiriti Kanjilal
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27545

In multi-agent interaction, a learner's actions shape the observations from which it infers other agents' behavior. A behavioral model may predict these observations well yet support poor adaptation when opponents change. We introduce Epistemic Social Learning (ESL), which couples fast, interaction-specific Bayesian beliefs with slower online learning of population-shared behavioral prototypes. Belief-conditioned decisions influence subsequent observations and, in turn, the representations learned from them. Under explicit regularity and averaging assumptions, we provide a conditional stochastic-approximation characterization of these coupled dynamics. Across twelve synthetic $3\times3$ cooperative, coordination, and adversarial games, ESL achieves the lowest aggregate post-switch regret among the evaluated online latent-adaptation methods, including a switch-aware Bayesian filter. Additional experiments examine observation loss, noise, and asymmetric incentives. These findings illustrate how predictive fit can differ from decision-relevant adaptation under policy-dependent interaction.


### arXiv 2611.27540 - Where Delay Compensation Belongs in a Decentralized Mean-Field-Game Swarm with a Control-Barrier Safety Filter

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-20T12:36:13Z
- authors: Jan Mikołajczyk, Igor Rostkowski, Bartosz Ogórkiewicz, Szymon Skierski, Sylwia Opat, Wojciech Rybak
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27540

Large robot teams are often run as a plan-then-execute stack. We study a decentralized instance for drone swarms, a mean-field-game (MFG) routing field with a per-agent control-barrier-function (CBF) safety filter, when communication is limited by a shared TDMA channel. Neighbors are known only through noisy range and bearing measurements exchanged in round-robin slots, so the frame, and with it the age of every estimate, grows with swarm size. In simulation this delay reaches both layers but breaks only the safety layer. Without delay, swarms of up to 100 agents do not collide, even with each measurement's noise held for a whole frame; with it, stale estimates at the CBF cause collisions beyond a frame period of 125 to 175 ms, or 75 to 100 ms at 100 agents with held noise, and swarm size sets where that limit lies and how steeply collisions rise. At the MFG, staleness makes the local density noisier without biasing it, and throughput drops by at most a tenth. We then compensate with lightweight on-board predictors, from constant-velocity fits to a small learned network, that estimate each neighbor's position from its last broadcasts. A layer-split experiment shows where prediction is needed: at the CBF it restores safety and extends the tolerated frame period by 1.4 to 1.7 times with an untrained constant-velocity fit and about 2.3 with the learned network when the noise is redrawn each step; with noise held for a frame the two extend it about equally, while at the MFG it only refines the congestion correction. A constant wider safety margin, the simplest alternative, makes collisions more frequent in a bottleneck. With the routing solved offline, that layer is robust to the delay because it sees neighbors only through a crowd average; compensation belongs in the safety filter, switched on only above the uncompensated limit, since below it the predictor's own error adds collisions.


### arXiv 2611.27539 - Joint Optimization of Fleet Size, Localization Infrastructure, and Trajectories for Multi-AUV Persistent Monitoring

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T12:35:02Z
- authors: Jan Mikołajczyk, Jakub Bałenkowski, Maciej Borowiecki, Jan Ciecierski, Marcel Kolecki, Julian Maleta
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27539

Persistent monitoring of large-scale subsea infrastructure, such as offshore wind farm foundations, export cables, and inspection corridors, remains a fundamental challenge for autonomous underwater systems. The problem is not solely spatial coverage, but rather temporal coverage: ensuring that all relevant regions of interest are revisited within bounded time intervals under limited sensing, navigation uncertainty, and constrained vehicle resources. We compare three classes of trajectory strategies: (1) deterministic lane-based coverage (lawnmower patterns), (2) stochastic exploration (biased random walk with boundary constraints), and (3) coordinated partition-based strategies (Voronoi tessellation). Rather than seeking a universally superior method, we evaluate how varying levels of localization accuracy impact temporal coverage across two distinct operational profiles: persistent monitoring of the entire operational workspace versus targeted inspection of critical infrastructure. We present a simulation-based, quantitatively grounded study of temporal coverage in multi-AUV persistent monitoring, disentangling three interacting design dimensions: (i) trajectory generation strategy, (ii) fleet size, and (iii) localization accuracy provided by fixed acoustic beacon infrastructure. We introduce a cost-aware evaluation framework combining fleet size, number of beacons, and mission energy expenditure into a unified cost function. Results demonstrate that beyond a certain accuracy threshold, improvements in localization yield diminishing returns compared to coordinated trajectory design, an effect often obscured in integrated navigation-planning approaches. Coordinated-partition trajectories enable a reduced fleet size to achieve superior temporal coverage at a 39-46% lower overall life-cycle cost than rigid deterministic paths unoptimized for the localization infrastructure.


### arXiv 2611.27468 - Communication-constrained multiagent risk estimation: a simulation benchmark for adult sore throat

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T11:19:13Z
- authors: Anas H. Alzahrani
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27468

Distributed predictive agents may need to approach a shared estimate under limited, unreliable communication. We evaluated classical symmetric pairwise gossip among five agents initialized from frozen calibrated predictions in a synthetic adult sore-throat benchmark. Ninety runs across three fixed generator families supplied 270,000 underlying test encounters, each observed through four missingness views. Complete, ring and star graphs were compared at eight interaction budgets and transaction failure probabilities of 0, 0.2 and 0.5. The coordination extension was designed after inspection of the predictive benchmark and is exploratory. Outcomes were disagreement around the conserved arithmetic mean and its exact decomposition of mean-agent Brier error. In the base view at eight attempted pairs and 0.2 failure, disagreement variance was 0.000109 for the complete graph, 0.000134 for the ring and 0.000175 for the star. The paired complete-minus-ring difference was -0.000024 (95% Monte Carlo interval -0.000030 to -0.000019). The centralized mean had base Brier 0.069781, compared with 0.069368 for Kumo alone. Agreement therefore does not establish superiority to the strongest individual predictor. Code, fixed probability inputs and results support replay without model refitting. The study quantifies finite-budget coordination under specified simulation assumptions; it proposes no new gossip algorithm and establishes no clinical accuracy or utility.


### arXiv 2611.27127 - OpenRing: Deadlock-Free Execution of Any Planner on AGV Floors with One-Way Lanes

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T04:54:47Z
- authors: Zhihao Lin, Anh-Tu Nguyen, Hui Zhang, Yoshinobu Kawahara
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27127

Lifelong multi-agent path finding replans routes for automated guided vehicles, but deviations can invalidate a plan. With one-way lanes, a late vehicle or lost planner can close a ring of full segments. We separate how fast the floor runs from whether it keeps running. In OpenRing a planner proposes routes and an order, a local guard commits only moves and route changes that cannot close such a ring, and the order binds for a bounded time. Despite arbitrary proposals or planner loss, no ring closes. With two-place segments, traffic can continue under disturbances; progress is guaranteed once they end, and all vehicles exit if entries also stop. Among rules that reroute only on a proposal, every guard veto is necessary, and a clean plan keeps its crossing orders. The guarantee has a price: no rule that never stores a vehicle in a crossing under arbitrary stops carries more than $B/T_s$ per segment of $B$ places and traversal time $T_s$. With binding orders, three planners deadlock in 80 of 126 runs across eight floors. Inside OpenRing, none of the 126 runs deadlocks. OpenRing's closed-loop throughput rivals or exceeds the best baseline's. Physics simulations show no deadlocks or overlaps.


### arXiv 2611.27107 - Omo: Giving Continuing AI-Agent Work a Place to Return To

- categories: cs.SE, cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T04:32:19Z
- authors: Kaviru Hapuarachchi, Harrison Edwards
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27107

AI coding agents now keep working while the people who started them are away. Coming back is the hard part: a session list tells you which agent is where, but not what it was asked to do, what it changed, or what to tell it next. We present Omo, which gives each piece of continuing agent work a persistent place to return to. We built the idea twice. Omo Space is an open-source Minecraft mod in which rooms and screens that people build are live terminals on their own machine. Omo Theia brings the same rooms and desks into a customizable IDE, one step away from the editor and the Git patch. Both keep agent sessions running on the host after the view is closed. We describe the two systems, four lessons from building them, and a study designed to test whether returning to a place helps people pick up agent work correctly.


### arXiv 2611.26980 - Contexture: Runtime Context Eviction for High-Throughput Agent Serving

- categories: cs.DC, cs.AI, cs.MA, cs.OS
- published: 2026-11-20T01:55:52Z
- authors: Colin Hong, Bryan Shan, Wei Siong Pang, Chengzhi Lu, Xu Guo, Nitin Shivaraman
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26980

LLM agents such as Hermes, OpenClaw, and Codex repeatedly resend growing execution histories, increasing shared GPU key-value (KV) cache demand in high-bandwidth memory (HBM). We show that shared KV capacity limits agent-serving throughput. Existing context-reduction methods lack global cache-state visibility, while runtime KV managers lack tool-execution information. Tracing tokens to their originating tool operations reveals that later operations supersede earlier outputs and associated turns, identifying 64% of recorded final-history tokens as removal candidates. We present Contexture, a harness- and model-agnostic architecture for runtime context eviction. Contexture separates the harness's full conversation history from the model's execution context, reducing subsequent requests' KV demand without modifying harness logic or model execution. It combines tool-execution information with global visibility into shared KV pressure and prefix residency: supersession identifies removal candidates, while runtime state determines when removal justifies sacrificing cached computation. This coordination reduces memory demand and decode KV traffic while limiting re-prefill interference with concurrent decoding, without additional model calls or training. Across two models, two harnesses, and two benchmarks, Contexture improves throughput under SLO by 20-73% over the best of no eviction, harness compaction, AgentDiet, and Continuum, and by 7-63% over observation masking in five of eight configurations, and reduces GPU energy per completed session by up to 11%. It also raises throughput under SLO by 23% over LMCache alone on H100. Live-agent experiments show a mean accuracy improvement of 1.4 percentage points over unloaded no eviction, with a worst-case loss of 3.7 points, while observation masking loses 3-28 points.


### arXiv 2611.26957 - Tracking and Limiting Unsupported Claim Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: A Provenance-Aware Framework

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T01:17:40Z
- authors: Zijia Cao, Hanqing Dou
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26957

This research examines how unsupported claims change as they move through multi-agent pipelines. It also examines whether preserving provenance can reduce these changes. We test both homogeneous and heterogeneous pipelines. Homogeneous pipelines use the same model across agents, while heterogeneous pipelines use different models. We compare the baseline with a provenance-aware framework. The framework passes each claim with its support status, source, evidence and transmission history. No new evidence is introduced during transmission. We measure changes in claim meaning, unsupported detail accretion, semantic drift, and provenance preservation. These metrics show whether the framework limits unsupported changes as claims pass between agents.


### arXiv 2611.26936 - SecretAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Privacy Protection against Vision Language Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CR, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T00:46:20Z
- authors: Minda Zhao, Yue Zhang, Mengyu Wang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26936

Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer private information from shared images, including locations, personally identifiable information (PII), and personal attributes such as age and sex, even from medical images. Adversarial perturbations can protect such information against black-box VLMs, but existing methods overlook image quality and introduce visible artifacts into the images they protect. We present SecretAgent, the first general multi-agent framework that protects image privacy against VLMs while preserving visual fidelity. LLM agents iteratively locate the visual evidence that reveals private information and adapt an invisible perturbation to the target VLM's answers and to an appearance reviewer agent, which rejects any candidate with visible artifacts. We instantiate SecretAgent with two complementary variants: SecretAgent-Gradient optimizes the perturbation through six surrogate encoders for cross-model transferability, and the gradient-free SecretAgent-Tool composes procedural noise and achieves the best perceptual fidelity on three of the five datasets. Experiments on four privacy applications (geolocation, PII, facial, and medical) across five datasets and six VLMs show that SecretAgent preserves image quality substantially better than existing methods while achieving state-of-the-art privacy protection in every application, and that its protection transfers to other VLMs.


### arXiv 2611.26709 - Federated KPI Aggregation with Local Privacy Constraints and Coordinated OLAP Roll-Ups

- categories: cs.DB, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-19T19:09:11Z
- authors: Felix Härer
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26709

Cross-organizational KPI analysis can require aggregated results from independently administered data warehouses without centralizing detailed records. To limit disclosure from small local groups, a site can require a minimum number of contributing records before releasing a KPI value for a group. If each site applies this rule independently, different local contributions may be suppressed and the combined KPI can become incomplete. This paper investigates whether OLAP granularity can be coordinated before local query results are released so that all sites satisfy their local minimum-cohort rules and contribute at the same analytical level. The proposed method selects the least-generalized common roll-up accepted by every site. Site agents coordinate current capabilities and approved local execution across independently administered sites, with privacy checks and aggregate computation remaining deterministic local functions. In a four-site prototype, coordinated roll-up retained all contributions and reproduced the corresponding centralized result, compared with 78.3% retained under independent suppression. A capability-change experiment showed fewer candidate checks and replies and no unsupported requests when current site-agent state was used. The results demonstrate the feasibility of federated KPI aggregation under local privacy constraints through coordinated granularity selection and agent-based coordination.


### arXiv 2611.27797 - EvoTER: Evolving Embodied Agents via Tool-Use Experience and Reflection

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-20T16:28:51Z
- authors: Yingting Zhou, Yuyin Zhou, Yongliang Wang, Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27797

Long-horizon embodied tasks require agents to ground instructions in changing observations, coordinate heterogeneous tools, and recover from failures that can propagate through downstream operations. Although existing LLM-based agents retain experience and refine reusable skills, successful local repairs do not by themselves establish complete-task gains over the current shared procedure library. We present EvoTER, an experience-driven framework that enables embodied agents to self-evolve tool-use skills without updating model parameters. EvoTER operates through a three-role evolution loop: Explorer records tool-use trajectories; Diagnoser distills successful and failed executions into skills for tool selection, parameter computation, postcondition checks, and recovery; and Validator retains candidate revisions only when they improve complete-task success in fresh sessions. In simulation, EvoTER improves overall task success from a pre-evolution baseline of 53.60% to 80.40%. In real-world experiments, simulation initialization reduces the attempts required to acquire short-horizon skills, while continued real-world evolution improves long-horizon success from 20% to 80%-100% across tasks and enables the evolving skill library to incorporate new tool functionality introduced after simulation development. Project page: https://evoter-project.github.io/


### arXiv 2611.27659 - Hierarchical Agentic Mid-Band Agility for Reliable Low-Latency Space-Air-Ground Integrated V2X Networks

- categories: eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-20T14:28:21Z
- authors: Liu Cao, Zisheng Gong, Lyutianyang Zhang, Jinke Ren, Jiguang He, Shuguang Cui
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27659

Upper-mid-band spectrum offers bandwidth opportunities for the integrated networks of ground vehicles, unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs), and low Earth orbit (LEO) satellites to support 6G wide-area vehicle-to-everything (V2X) connectivity. However, V2X services' latency and reliability are sensitive to the mid-band selection due to frequency-dependent propagation and rain attenuation. This paper investigates environment-aware spectrum agility over candidate frequency range 3 (FR3) bands within 7.125-24.25 GHz, jointly with end-to-end (E2E) multi-path traffic splitting and transmit power control. Each data packet is partitioned into non-overlapping subflows over direct and UAV-assisted paths, with failed sky-link subflows selectively retransmitted subject to retransmission and freshness constraints. We formulate latency- and reliability-oriented problems, followed by a unified sequential optimization problem incorporating band-switching costs and average quality-of-service (QoS) violation budgets. To coordinate optimization decisions across heterogeneous timescales, we develop an agentic AI-assisted hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning (AA-HMARL) framework. A high-level agentic supervisor integrates recurrent memory, analytical evaluation of candidate bands, and event-triggered replanning to coordinate slow environmental adaptation with fast channel and retransmission dynamics, while distributed ground vehicles and UAV agents optimize data splitting and power using constrained MAPPO. Simulation results demonstrate an adjustable reliability-latency trade-off. Particularly, AA-HMARL reduces the mean E2E completion latency and the mean number of retransmission rounds among successfully delivered updates by 17.8-25.5% and 57.6-61.5%, respectively, and reduces the mean age of information (AoI) by 12.7-14.6% relative to fixed-band MAPPO across two traffic types.


### arXiv 2611.27346 - Dual-Agent DRL based Cooperative Beamforming and Trajectory Optimization for UAV-ISAC Networks

- categories: cs.IT, eess.SP
- published: 2026-11-20T09:20:58Z
- authors: Kaijian Yang, Feng Wang, Guojun Han, Xin Wang, Vincent K. N. Lau
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27346

This paper considers a low-altitude unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) network, where multiple ground base stations (BSs) provide integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) services for multiple communication UAVs (C-UAVs) and sensing UAVs (S-UAVs). With the objective of maximizing the average sum rate of C-UAVs over the entire flight episode, we jointly optimize the multi-BS multi-UAV ISAC beamforming and trajectory design, subject to the BS transmit power budget and the sensing signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR) constraints of S-UAVs, as well as the flight mission and collision avoidance constraints of C-UAVs and S-UAVs. Based on the multi-agent deep reinforcement learning (MADRL) technique, we develop a dual-agent scheme, termed DeepDA-OPT, to solve the resulting constrained Markov decision process (MDP). In the proposed DeepDA-OPT scheme, two agents, i.e., Agent-B and Agent-T, are specifically deployed for learning the multi-BS ISAC transmit beamformers and the C-UAV/S-UAV trajectories, respectively, where the optimal BS receive beamformers for the sensing echo signals of S-UAVs are obtained in closed form by leveraging the generalized Rayleigh quotient theorem. To mitigate the overestimation of value functions, both Agent-B and Agent-T are trained based on the twin-delayed deep deterministic (TD3) policy-gradient algorithm. In addition, a constraint-aware noise exploration policy is employed, and the reward function is designed to incorporate a sensing penalty and a flight urgency penalty, thereby guiding the training of Agent-B and Agent-T. Numerical results show that, compared with the baseline schemes, the proposed DeepDA-OPT scheme achieves a higher average sum rate of C-UAVs, while the sensing SINR constraints are empirically well satisfied and no collision violation is observed under the evaluated settings.


### arXiv 2611.27070 - $R^2$: Recognizing and Reducing Redundant Visual Tool Use

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T03:38:36Z
- authors: Yilin Zhao, Jinyu Wang, Wenming Yang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27070

Recent studies have shown that visual tools can improve multimodal reasoning. However, the effects of tools vary substantially across samples, and benchmark accuracy alone does not reveal whether tool interactions are beneficial or redundant. We present $R^2$, a framework that recognizes and reduces redundant visual tool use in vision-language models. We first introduce a sample-level Category Analysis of Tool Utility (CAT), which categorizes samples according to whether a model can benefit from tools. This analysis reveals that benchmark scores may obscure a model's tool-use ability. Across seven benchmarks, samples benefiting from tools account for at most 12.44% of each benchmark, while the majority gain little from tool interaction or are even harmed by it. To assess individual calls, we propose the Visual Feedback Dependency (VFD) metric, which compares the likelihood of the same subsequent reasoning under actual and alternative visual feedback without an external evaluator or trajectory regeneration. Redundancy-Aware GRPO (RA-GRPO) combines this dependency signal with a category-conditioned base call cost to assign relative efficiency advantages among correct trajectories, supplementing outcome-based supervision. Across the seven benchmarks, RA-GRPO reduces the mean number of tool calls by 12.39% relative to Vanilla GRPO at comparable benchmark accuracy. Evaluated by tool-utility category, RA-GRPO improves category macro-average accuracy over Vanilla GRPO by 3.35 percentage points.


### arXiv 2611.27041 - Subverting Tool Arguments: Defense-Evading Metadata Poisoning in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-20T03:13:56Z
- authors: Shih-Chieh Dai, Xinyu Sun, Haichuan Zhang, Zhiyuan Zhong, Guanhong Tao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27041

Conventional indirect prompt injection (IPI) attacks hijack an LLM agent's workflow through malicious instructions embedded in retrieved external content. The growing adoption of the Model Context Protocol (MCP) exposes another attack surface: tool descriptions supplied by third-party MCP servers. Attackers can manipulate this metadata to induce malicious argument generation during tool calls. The challenge is to ensure that the modified descriptions still appear benign to both LLMs and users. Existing IPI attacks, however, struggle to achieve both high attack success and low detectability in this setting. To address this challenge, we propose GRAFT, an LLM-based optimization method for crafting adversarial tool descriptions. Specifically, GRAFT uses a masking mechanism to identify argument-relevant token positions for targeted injection. It then iteratively refines candidate descriptions using dynamic mutation strategies proposed by an LLM, guided by the agent's tool choices and generated arguments. We evaluate GRAFT on three public datasets against several attack baselines. The results show that GRAFT achieves higher attack success while maintaining low detection rates across five filter-based defenses. It also bypasses several existing IPI defenses. Even under an adaptive defense trained on GRAFT-generated injections, GRAFT remains more effective than the baselines.


### arXiv 2611.26988 - From Letter-of-Credit Requirements to Executable Specifications: Evaluating Verification Accuracy and Completeness

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-20T02:15:39Z
- authors: Ji Sub Choi, Whan Choi, Seo Yeon Gu, Suhyun Kim, Inyong Yun
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26988

In letter-of-credit (LC) document examination, a correct final decision does not show that every requirement was examined. We therefore evaluate examination requirement by requirement and make each requirement executable. A language model converts the bill of lading (BL) and commercial invoice (IV) requirements of a credit into atomic conditions and logical structures, and a deterministic verifier retrieves the named evidence and computes each outcome, so every check can be compared with a gold encoding and traced to logged evidence. Because LC records are confidential, our on-premises 9B parser is fine-tuned only on synthetic pairs built from templates and human-reviewed public document values. On 345 real LC cases, final-decision accuracy proves misleading: a constant PASS rule has the highest raw accuracy while examining nothing. Although it never sees a real record during training, the fine-tuned parser detects 93% of the atomic requirements its ontology covers and reconstructs 86% of the covered requirements exactly. It verifies 82% of the evaluable requirements correctly, against 54% for a tool-using agent, and leads the agent on both satisfied requirements and discrepancies. It also completely examines 1.8 times as many cases as few-shot prompting of the same model, while direct prediction and the agent complete none. Executing the gold encodings with the same verifier shows that some of the remaining errors, missed discrepancies included, come from the operators and normalization, which a better parser alone cannot fix. Every rejection comes with a trace of the units and evidence behind it, which suits examiner-in-the-loop review.


### arXiv 2611.26966 - Keep the Process, Change the Supplier: Runtime Governance for Tool-Using AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T01:40:57Z
- authors: Deepak Waghmare, Shaad Sufi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26966

Tool-using AI agents propose actions whose authorization and external effects cannot be inferred from generated text alone. We study an execution boundary that separates agent proposals from business-controlled authority, context disclosure, and supplier selection. The Open Process Control Plane (OPCP) mediates capability calls and records uncertain outcomes when a supplier may have acted without returning an acceptable reply. We evaluate this runtime with scripted requests and synthetic suppliers: 2,400 fault episodes across four policies, 60 process-termination/restart episodes, 19,000 timed calls across 19 configurations, five targeted mutations over 43 fixtures, and 160 substitution records. No language model or autonomous agent is evaluated. OPCP and a conservative composition agree on all measured fault outcomes. Both recover after definite refusal without cross-supplier duplication; retry-all duplicates effects in every tested lost-reply-after-commit case. Persistent runtime state prevents cross-supplier replay in the restart experiments, while same-supplier duplicate prevention also requires supplier idempotency. At 128 suppliers, best-score routing has a median latency of 1.411 ms versus 0.261 ms for pinned routing. All five selected mutations are detected, and record enumeration exposes acceptance of an empty regression set. These results characterize an execution mechanism relevant to agent governance, including its costs and assumption boundaries. They do not establish improved agent task success, prompt-injection resistance, or real-vendor model portability.


### arXiv 2611.26911 - InfantConnect: integrating developmental brain atlases and electrophysiological network analysis

- categories: cs.CE
- published: 2026-11-19T23:54:20Z
- authors: Alessandro Crimi, Paweł Gryboś
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26911

The brains of infants change considerably during the first two years. Hence, developmental neuroimaging requires computational tools that account for age-related anatomy while supporting the analysis of electrophysiological data. For instance, cortical surfaces, electrode montages, tractography atlases, and electroencephalography (EEG) analysis utilities are commonly distributed across separate resources and software environments. Here we propose an open Python framework that brings these components into a shared three-dimensional workspace and supplements interactive visualization with command-line tools for EEG quality control, preprocessing, source-related visualization, and effective-connectivity analysis. The command line version can also be invoked by a model context protocol server to be used by artificial intelligence agents. The tool supports age-indexed cortical surfaces, infant scalp and skull templates, electrode locations, tractography, sensor-level connectomes, and directed estimates based on Granger causality and transfer entropy. We demonstrate the resource using representative anatomical visualizations and evaluate the effective-connectivity workflow on controlled toy networks with explicitly specified nodes and directed interactions. These tests provide targeted evidence about the connectivity workflow rather than a general validation of causal inference from clinical EEG. Performance benchmarks on low resolution electromagnetic tomography and validation of effective connectivity are provided.


### arXiv 2611.26782 - ChatAtom: A Multi-Agent LLM System for Molecular Dynamics Simulation and Visualization

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-19T20:18:15Z
- authors: Trupti Mohanty, Ming Du, Mitchell A. Wood, Brad L. Boyce, Remi Dingreville, Mathew J. Cherukara
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26782

Molecular dynamics (MD) simulation resolves atomic-scale motion and predicts the structural, mechanical, and dynamic properties of materials, making it cen- tral to materials design and discovery. Yet an MD study requires an expert-driven workflow: designing the simulation, writing reliable input scripts, executing them, and analyzing the results with separate tools. Here we present ChatAtom, a multi-agent large language model system that translates a natural-language request into a complete MD workflow without the user writing input scripts or analysis code. Each agent has a defined responsibility within the workflow. An orchestrator plans the study; the LAMMPS and OVITO agents each use a generator-reviewer pair to prepare and review simulation and visualization scripts before execution; a high-performance computing agent dispatches jobs to remote resources; and an analysis agent interprets the completed run. The work- flow is accessible through a browser interface that streams thermodynamic data and rendered frames during local execution, allowing users to follow the simu- lation as it progresses. The architecture also supports new capabilities through loadable skills without modifying the agents, enabling autonomous hypothesis generation and testing. We evaluate ChatAtom through script-generation bench- marks, an end-to-end study of crack propagation, and a closed-loop campaign. By bringing script generation, execution, visualization, and analysis into one reviewed and observable pipeline, ChatAtom lowers the barrier to conducting MD studies and broadens access to advanced simulation workflows.


### arXiv 2611.26776 - What Helps Language-Model Agents Review Violent Video? A Controlled Comparison with a Human Evidence Reference

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.MM
- published: 2026-11-19T20:10:13Z
- authors: Mohammed El Amine Mokhtari, Matei Mancas, Virginie Vandenbulcke
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26776

Systems that flag violent video for a human operator can now be built from language models without training, by combining perception models and letting a language model decide. We ask how the observations of the perception models should reach the decision, and which evidence matters. On the XD-Violence test set (799 clips) we fix one set of perception agents (video description, object detection, sound tagging, speech recognition and speech tagging) and one reasoning model, and compare three interfaces: messages between agents, a typed evidence graph explored with tools, and flat text read in one pass; and a single audio-visual model given the raw clip. Two annotators provide a human reference that records the decision, the incident segments and whether they relied on sight, sound or speech. Flat text matches the single model (F1 0.967 against 0.968), while the graph agent and message passing are less accurate; a variant of the graph agent developed on a separate set did not close the gap, and both graph summaries and multi-turn tool use lowered accuracy. These results hold with reworded prompts and with a larger reasoning model, except that the larger model weakens message passing enough for the graph agent to beat it. Sound events improve F1 by +0.012 [+0.004, +0.021], in line with the annotators, who used sound or speech on most violent clips. Citations of stored observations are faithful, changing about half of the decisions when removed, but no more so with the graph than with flat text, and the agent pipeline costs about 3 times the GPU time of the single model. We also report two artefacts of vision-language describers that mislead pipelines built on them.


### arXiv 2611.26749 - Fully Automated Discovery of Phase Diagrams Using Agentic Large Language Models

- categories: cond-mat.stat-mech
- published: 2026-11-19T19:44:06Z
- authors: Robert Amelung, Maximilian Nägele, Florian Marquardt
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26749

Classifying phases of matter and mapping out phase diagrams are central problems in physics. Recently, machine learning techniques have emerged as promising tools for phase classification, ranging from supervised learning to various unsupervised methods. However, these techniques provide limited qualitative insight into the discovered phases, typically work only for specific system classes, and do not by themselves efficiently map out the phase diagram. Autonomously constructing the phase diagram of a general system purely from measurements, without prior knowledge of the system, while also yielding qualitative understanding of the phases, remains an open task. In this work, we introduce Phasonaut, a two-agent framework that leverages the tool-use capabilities and broad knowledge of large language models (LLMs) to drive an active learning loop that constructs phase diagrams of physical systems and provides interpretations of the discovered phases. Starting from scratch, it iteratively selects and performs measurements, analyzes the resulting data, and segments the parameter domain into the phases it discovers. Phasonaut recovers the phase diagrams of classical and quantum spin systems to substantial precision. In the future, this framework could enable rapid exploration of systems with unknown phase diagrams, including in autonomously driven experiments.


### arXiv 2611.26663 - Adding One More Agent: How AI Team Size and Formation Shape Productivity and Diversity in Human-AI Brainstorming Teams

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-19T06:58:05Z
- authors: Rong-Ching Chang, Hao-Chuan Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26663

Generative AI has improved individual productivity, but whether adding a plurality of AI partners helps or hurts remains open. We crossed AI partnership (one versus two) with behavioral formation (calibrated to average human behavior versus customized by the participant) in a brainstorming experiment ($N$=168) with human-human dyads as reference. Two-AI rooms posted more ideas to the shared board, but human-authored ideas dropped substantially. With one AI partner, formation mattered: humans paired with an average-calibrated AI posted more than any other condition. With two, this effect vanished. Humans continued talking but converted fewer ideas into board posts. AI social talk predicted more human posts, while AI posting volume predicted fewer. AI presence increased global diversity of human-authored ideas, but only a second AI increased within-session differentiation. The number of AI partners and their formation can reshape what the human contributes; single-agent results do not generalize to multi-agent teams.

- 1.0.6 @ 2026-11-18T08:06:30Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.6 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **A malicious MCP server can no longer crash the proxy by streaming an unbounded server-sent event.** The proxy now caps how much of an upstream SSE stream it will buffer. A single line, or a single event, longer than 16 MiB closes that upstream connection with an error instead of growing the proxy's heap until it runs out of memory and drops every session. This covers the `sse` and `streamable-http` transports, including a standalone GET stream with the wrong content-type. The cap sits well above any legitimate message (four times the SDK reference servers' 4 MB inbound limit). ([GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/inspector/security/advisories/GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r)) by @cliffh

## The Decoder - Z.ai follows OpenAI into loop transformer territory with its new 750B flagship model GLM-5.4 (2026-11-23)

Z.ai follows OpenAI into loop transformer territory with its new 750B flagship model Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Jonathan Kemper View the LinkedIn Profile of Jonathan Kemper Nov 23, 2026 AI in practice Z.ai follows OpenAI into loop transformer territory with its new 750B flagship model Zhipu AI Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Key Points Z.ai has introduced GLM-5.4, a 750-billion-parameter model that runs 28 layers twice to increase computational depth without adding parameters. Dedicated control gates, a shared token cache, and sparse attention keep memory and compute costs from rising sharply. According to Z.ai, GLM-5.4 can use code to generate videos, games, and CAD models. The model is available on Z.ai's platforms, with its weights set to follow. Z.ai is moving its flagship model to a loop architecture that runs some of its layers twice. With GLM-5.4, the company wants to mark the start of "Generalized Coding," where code also generates videos, games, and CAD models. Following OpenAI, Z.ai is introducing GLM-5.4, its first large model built on a loop transformer. While standard language models use each layer once per pass, GLM-5.4 runs a block of shared layers multiple times, refining the intermediate results from the previous pass to make the network deeper without adding parameters. OpenAI has used a similar architecture since GPT-6 Astra . According to Z.ai, the 750-billion-parameter model reaches performance comparable to models with 2 to 3 trillion parameters. The team retrained the GLM-5 base model released in February into a loop model that runs 28 layers twice. Loop models trained from scratch have so far performed worse than models without shared weights under the same compute budget, Z.ai writes . Ad Gates and shared caching keep the second pass manageable Converting the model introduces a problem, Z.ai says. If the model simply overwrites intermediate states on each pass, activations grow sharply from round to round, leaving subsequent layers with values outside the range they were trained on. Ad To handle this, GLM-5.4 uses dedicated gates for each token and pass to control how much of the new output flows back into the data stream. According to Z.ai, the model builds context cautiously on the first pass and writes its changes back in full on the second. On difficult tokens, Z.ai says, the first pass steps in more strongly. GLM-5.4 runs layers 27 through 54 twice, and gates control how much each pass changes the intermediate results. Because the second pass would otherwise double memory usage, both passes share the KV cache, the temporary memory for previously processed tokens. The second pass stores its own extra values only in a small local window. As a result, Z.ai says, the overhead compared to GLM-5.3 stays manageable even at a 1-million-token context. Ad To save on compute as well, the model uses sparse attention to pick the most relevant earlier tokens upfront rather than scanning the entire context every time, a standard technique. The second pass then reuses that selection from the first instead of making a new one. According to Z.ai, that yields nearly the same results as picking tokens separately on each pass. The company also says the model reaches up to 200 tokens per second off-peak and 70 to 80 tokens per second under load. A looped 30B test model beats its baseline with shorter reasoning chains Before rolling out the architecture in its flagship, Z.ai tested it on models with 1, 4, 30, and 750 billion parameters. For a direct comparison, the team used a 30B model built on the same architecture as the large base model. Both versions had the same parameter count, while the loop version received about 10 percent more training compute. Its biggest gain came on the AIME25 math benchmark, where scores jumped from 19.2 to 30.4 points. Scores on the expert-level science questions in GPQA-Diamond rose from 47.6 to 56.3 points, and on the AA-LCR long-context test they climbed from 40.3 to 47.7 points. Gains were smaller at just over three points on the MMLU-Pro knowledge test and on IFBench, which tests instruction following. Ad Responses also got shorter, shrinking 14.9 percent on MMLU-Pro, 11.7 percent on GPQA-Diamond, and just 2 percent on AIME25. Z.ai says part of the reasoning shifts out of the output tokens and into the loop's extra computational depth. Ad At the same parameter count, the loop version outperforms the baseline across all five benchmarks while producing shorter responses. It is still impossible to judge how well GLM-5.4 actually performs, as Z.ai has published no benchmark scores for the 750B model and the figures in its blog post come solely from the 30B test. There are no independent evaluations from groups like Artificial Analysis either. Unsubscribe any time. By Z.ai's own account, GLM-5.4's biggest weakness is overly long reasoning phases in which the model gets stuck, though the company says that extended double-checking improves accuracy. Z.ai views GLM-5.4 as the starting point for "Generalized Coding," where the company says code becomes the foundation for tasks beyond traditional software development. To illustrate the idea, Z.ai shows a music video scored by the model itself, a six-axis robot arm it says GLM-5.4 designed in FreeCAD, a complete game, and an interactive 3D scene built from mathematical geometry and custom shaders. 1 / 4 Z.ai plans to release model weights and a much larger model GLM-5.4 is available through the GLM Coding Plan, the ZCode coding environment, and Z.ai Chat, with an API set to follow and token pricing not yet announced. Z.ai plans to release the model weights after further safety testing, and it also announced a much larger model expected to arrive soon. Shared layers date back to the Universal Transformer in 2018. In March, researchers in Bonn showed that looped transformers benefit from greater compute depth on math, while everyday knowledge benefits more from greater memory , the same trade-off Z.ai cites to explain its architecture. Thinking Machines also drew attention recently with Inkling 2 Mini because the loop model runs locally on Macs with at least 32 GB of RAM. At 750 billion parameters, GLM-5.4 is nowhere near running on a laptop, but Z.ai is instead targeting the tier of Kimi K3.1 Pro and Qwen3.8-Max . At 2.8 trillion and 2.4 trillion parameters, respectively, those models are more than three times as large and require large amounts of compute. For its predecessor GLM-5.3 , Z.ai kept the existing base model and drew all of its gains f


## The Decoder - GPT-6.2 Astra solved a math theorem no other AI could crack, then invented its own proof strategy (2026-11-23)

GPT-6.2 Astra solved a math theorem no other AI could crack, then invented its own proof strategy Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Manuel Uth Nov 23, 2026 Short News GPT-6.2 Astra solved a math theorem no other AI could crack, then invented its own proof strategy OpenAI's GPT-6.2 Astra produced a formalized, machine-verifiable proof of a 2006 theorem with zero human help during the run. According to Vals AI , it's the first known proof of Smoktunowicz's gap theorem written in Lean . The theorem states that when a mathematical system multiplies its basic building blocks, growth is either quite slow or very fast. There's no middle ground. Because key foundations of the original proof were missing from the math library Mathlib, Astra took a different route than the published mathematical proofs , Vals says. It used "capacity," a metric from existing research that checks the minimum number of elements added per computation step. GPT-6.2 Astra built its proof around "capacity," a metric that behaves like the edge of a graph. | Image: Vals AI This happened on Autoform-Hard , a subset of Meta FAIR's AutoformBench curated by Vals. It contains 56 tasks (five corrected) that ten models in Meta's study failed to solve within budget. When Astra tried to quit after 47 minutes and eight dollars, the system rejected the attempt since unsolved submissions aren't accepted until $20 has been spent. After nine minutes of silence with no code input while it searched for a new strategy, Astra delivered the 1,800-line proof at the 94-minute mark for $21.58. It now leads the leaderboard with 49 solved tasks, ahead of GPT-6.2 Sol and Claude Fable 5.2 at 48 each. ## The Decoder - Nvidia tried life without Jensen Huang and it lasted about two weeks (2026-11-23)

Nvidia tried life without Jensen Huang and it lasted about two weeks Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 23, 2026 Short News Nvidia tried life without Jensen Huang and it lasted about two weeks Nvidia's board of directors has reinstated Jensen Huang as president, CEO, and board member, effective immediately. The decision was unanimous, Nvidia said in a press release, following talks with Huang and feedback from senior US government officials who concluded that his return poses no national security risk. CFO Colette Kress had been running the company on an interim basis , and the stock had tanked during that period. President Donald J. Trump praised the decision on Truth Social, saying the US Attorney General found no evidence of wrongdoing by Huang. The move comes after reports that Huang had been targeted by foreign intelligence operations . An independent board committee is still investigating the matter with outside counsel. The US government is also reviewing Nvidia's business dealings. Trump urged the company to keep looking into whether its export control compliance holds up. Nvidia has already set up a dedicated oversight committee for that purpose and is searching for a Chief Compliance Officer. Huang and the company said they'll continue cooperating with authorities and internal investigators. Nvidia's stock jumped more than 30 percent on the news.
