## The Decoder - Meta's Alexandr Wang warns the AI industry isn't ready for what's coming after Riemann breakthrough (2026-11-23)

Meta's Alexandr Wang warns the AI industry isn't ready for what's coming after Riemann breakthrough Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 23, 2026 AI and society Meta's Alexandr Wang warns the AI industry isn't ready for what's coming after Riemann breakthrough Meta's Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang sees the recent AI-assisted proof sketch of the Riemann Hypothesis as a clear sign that superintelligence is right around the corner. The Riemann Hypothesis is widely considered the most intellectually demanding unsolved problem in mathematics. The brightest minds have failed to crack it for 150 years. The fact that an AI model played a major role in the breakthrough shows that raw intelligence is no longer the bottleneck, Wang argues. According to Wang, current models only lack domain-specific knowledge and practical infrastructure to outperform humans in nearly every field. That includes AI research itself. Since AI research is intellectually less demanding than the Riemann Hypothesis, Wang believes models could soon automate their own development and improve themselves. They could also dramatically speed up biological research, weapons research, and economic research at a pace humans can barely follow. Wang also warns that the AI industry isn't thinking hard enough about what comes next. In his view, many in the field treat superintelligence as inevitable and focus only on safety. But the stakes are much broader than that, he argues. The industry needs to actively ensure that AI gives people more agency rather than taking control of society. That would require a sharp departure from the current course, Wang says. Despite having its own superintelligence team , Meta isn't a major player at the frontier right now. ## The Decoder - Chinese military fears AI could let the US find and destroy its nuclear missiles (2026-11-23)

Chinese military fears AI could let the US find and destroy its nuclear missiles Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 23, 2026 Short News Chinese military fears AI could let the US find and destroy its nuclear missiles Chinese military researchers fear that AI advances could give the US an edge in the nuclear balance of power. Experts in China worry that artificial intelligence could make it easier for US forces to detect and destroy Chinese nuclear missiles, according to a New York Times analysis. That would undermine China's deterrence strategy, which relies on a guaranteed second-strike capability. Researchers at the Academy of Military Sciences in Beijing write that AI could shrink US decision-making time from 15 to 30 minutes down to 90 seconds. China currently has about 620 operational warheads, according to the Federation of American Scientists, and is expected to expand its arsenal to roughly 1,000 by 2030. At the same time, Chinese military institutes are studying how AI could make their own weapons harder to intercept, for instance by using AI-controlled flight paths for hypersonic missiles. Experts warn, however, that flawed AI data could trigger dangerous miscalculations during a crisis. That risk proved very real earlier this year, when the US military nearly boarded a Chinese ship based on a false, AI-generated intelligence report . Xi Jinping did discuss nuclear risks at a summit with Trump and Putin, but AI's role in nuclear weapons never came up, according to the report. Despite the growing urgency, the US and China have made almost no progress on talks about AI safeguards in nuclear systems. ## The Decoder - New open model Inkling 2 Mini thinks in loops to solve problems with fewer words and still runs on a laptop (2026-11-21)

New open model Inkling 2 Mini thinks in loops to solve problems with fewer words and still runs on a laptop Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 21, 2026 Short News New open model Inkling 2 Mini thinks in loops to solve problems with fewer words and still runs on a laptop Thinking Machines has released Inkling 2 Mini, a so-called looped transformer. A looped transformer runs certain layers multiple times for each token. Inkling 2 Mini runs 12 of its 24 layers three times in a row. That lets the model reason internally for longer without generating more text, finding the right answer with fewer tokens, according to Thinking Machines . The open mixture-of-experts model has 26.4 billion parameters, with 5.4 billion active per token because of the loops, the company says. It processes text and images, has a 1-million-token context window, and is licensed under Apache 2.0. Inkling 2 Mini leads all open models under 100 billion parameters on agentic coding tests, scoring 48.2 percent on DeepSWE v1.1 and 46.5 percent on the Vals Index v2.2. Artificial Analysis gives it 20 points on its Intelligence Index, tying it with Qwen3.8 27B at roughly six times lower cost and four times the speed. Inkling 2 Mini scores 20 on Artificial Analysis's Intelligence Index, tying it with Qwen3.8 27B (xhigh) and Nanbeige4.3-33B. With far fewer active parameters, the model sits directly on the Pareto frontier. | Image: Artificial Analysis Inkling 2 Mini runs locally on Macs with at least 32 GB of memory and on Nvidia's DGX Spark. You can download the weights and a research version with variable loops from Hugging Face . It is also available to try in the Tinker Playground . ## The Decoder - Hours after Anthropic claims a Claude proof of the Riemann hypothesis, Stanford releases an all-human competing draft (2026-11-21)

Hours after Anthropic claims a Claude proof of the Riemann hypothesis, Stanford releases an all-human competing draft Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Manuel Uth Nov 21, 2026 AI research Hours after Anthropic claims a Claude proof of the Riemann hypothesis, Stanford releases an all-human competing draft Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Key Points Anthropic mathematician Levent Alpöge and the AI model Claude presented a proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, publishing it in the Lean programming language for computer verification. Just hours later, Stanford University researchers published an independent, entirely human approach to the same problem by mathematician Alessandro Masullo. Open since 1859, the hypothesis is central to understanding prime numbers, and confirming it would validate hundreds of dependent mathematical theories while underscoring AI's role in research. Levent Alpöge, a mathematician at Anthropic, said Friday he used a large language model to solve math's most famous open problem. Alpöge posted a paper and the accompanying computer code on his website, according to Scientific American and other US outlets. "I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis," he wrote in his announcement . According to Alpöge, his method goes beyond the original prime-number problem and also resolves the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis (GRH), which extends that ordering principle to a large family of related equations. Given a bit more time, he expects to solve the even broader Extended Riemann Hypothesis as well. Ad Alpöge had treated the problem as a secret lifelong goal since college, expecting it to take decades. Over the past few months, he worked closely with Anthropic's AI model, setting the direction and combining two approaches on a simplified toy model. Once they moved to the actual problem, the AI took over and left him far behind near the finish line. Ad To crack the puzzle, the pair extended an idea from mathematician Stepanov, who had solved a similar question in another mathematical system. That method relies on a tool similar to taking derivatives of smooth curves in high school calculus, a trick that normally fails on whole numbers because individual values cannot be differentiated like a continuous line. Alpöge and Claude got around that obstacle by chaining special mathematical transformations together like links to mimic the effect of a derivative. The language model supplied the key insight, finding that for a number sequence of length X, you need to chain exactly as many transformations as the square root of X. Ideas like that alone should leave fellow mathematicians stunned, Alpöge says. Ad Stanford mathematicians rush out an all-human competing proof Hours after Alpöge's announcement, the story took a dramatic turn. Just before a board meeting, Stanford math professor and American Mathematical Society (AMS) president Ravi Vakil posted a "hurried announcement" on his blog, sharing an independent draft proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis written by mathematician Alessandro Masullo. "It is all human, with no AI," Vakil wrote. Renowned Stanford number theorist Brian Conrad had quietly reviewed Masullo's work for months, offering extensive feedback that is now being incorporated, Vakil said. Ad Masullo was originally supposed to spend a year at Stanford refining the draft before going public, but Anthropic's sudden AI announcement forced the researchers to release their current version right away. Ad About eight hours after his initial announcement, Vakil updated his post, saying several people had contacted him to report problems in the proposed proof, identified with the assistance of different AI models. Several mathematicians in particular were first to flag the issues, he said. "As is usual, all issues need to be resolved to the satisfaction of the community before this proof would be accepted as true. (I make no claim as to the correctness of the proof strategy.)," Vakil wrote. Why both proofs could hold up and what happens next Mathematicians are taking both claims seriously right away because of the groundwork behind them. Masullo's traditional proof comes with months of vetting by Brian Conrad, while Alpöge backs his AI proof with full computer verification. Printed out, Claude's argument would run over 250,000 pages, Alpöge says, because the AI spells out even basic rules, such as a number other than one-half being either larger or smaller than one-half. To spare mathematicians that wall of text, Alpöge released the proof only in the Lean programming language, split into 14 data packages . Lean acts like a digital referee, checking step by step whether every logical deduction follows from math's basic rules without errors or unproven assumptions. Human error is still possible if the original question was translated into code incorrectly at the sta

## John Baez - Masullo on the Riemann Hypothesis (2026-11-22)

Masullo on the Riemann Hypothesis | Azimuth Azimuth Home About Masullo on the Riemann Hypothesis On November 20, 2026, two attempts to prove the Riemann Hypothesis appeared. One, by Levent Alpöge working with Claude, exists mainly as 17 million lines of Lean code. The other, by Alessandro Masullo, is a 940-page human-written manuscript that Ravi Vakil posted on his blog: • Ravi Vakil, Hurried announcement: Alessandro Masullo GRH , Math 216 blog, November 20, 2026. • Alessandro Maria Masullo, The Music of the Primes , draft, November 20, 2026. While there’s been an enormous outpouring about the drama of this situation, so far it’s hard to find good simple accounts of Masullo’s paper. Let me try to fill this gap by sketching Masullo’s proof strategy in a very simplified way, along with some problems people have noted. Since I am not an expert on number theory, all this is very tentative and I could be making serious mistakes. Please let me know if you see some! If you also want a sketch of the Alpöge / Claude attempt, check out these: • Michael Rubinstein, The Alpöge-Claude proof of the Riemann hypothesis explained for the Riemann zeta function . • Jimmie Adriazola, An end-to-end reconstruction of the Alpöge–Claude route to the Riemann hypothesis . Zeros as poles Okay—here we go! The Riemann zeta function is for 1," class="latex" /> and it extends to a meromorphic function on the whole complex plane. The Riemann Hypothesis says that all its ‘nontrivial’ zeros have real part Masullo’s argument works with the zeta function’s ‘logarithmic derivative’, Taking the log of the product formula and differentiating gives where if is a power of a prime and otherwise. So the right side can be written as a sum over primes. On the other hand, a function’s logarithmic derivative has a simple pole at each zero of the function. Roughly speaking, where ranges over the zeros of the Riemann zeta function. This second formula is a sum over zeros. Setting these two expressions equal gives what number theorists call an ‘explicit formula’: an exact identity linking primes and zeros of the zeta function. This is what Masullo and others mean by “the music of the primes”: each zero contributes one oscillating wave to the distribution of primes. You can see my animations of this here: • John Baez, Riemann’s explicit formulas . In these terms, the Riemann Hypothesis says: has no poles with real part greater than except the one at Masullo’s strategy for proving this is to find a new formula for the logarithmic derivative of the Riemann zeta function, one that visibly has no poles to the left of the line It’s called a ‘trace formula’. The generalized Riemann hypothesis But wait! Neither Alpöge and Masullo are content to prove the Riemann hypothesis: they want to prove the generalized Riemann Hypothesis. For ordinary integers, this concerns the functions where is a ‘Dirichlet character’. This is a function on the natural numbers that is periodic with some period obeys for all vanishes on numbers that share a factor with and has its other values being roots of unity. The Riemann zeta function is the simplest case: here for all One version of the generalized Riemann Hypothesis says: every zero of with lies on the line This implies many results about primes in arithmetic progressions. For example, Hardy and Littlewood showed it implies that primes equal to 3 mod 4 are more common than primes 1 mod 4. They also showed it implies every odd number > 5 is a sum of three primes! (Helfgott found an alternative, more complicated proof in 2013.) But Masullo also wants more: he wants to prove the analogous statement for L-functions of general Hecke characters . These involve a choice of algebraic number field , which gives a ring of integers generalizing the usual integers. This further generalization implies a huge number of results in number theory, computational complexity, and so on. Okay—now back to trace formulas. Trace formulas To prove that a Hecke -function has no nontrivial zeros except on the line Masullo comes up with a new formula for its logarithm He claims it makes sense without poles all the way to the line This formula is an example of a ‘trace formula’. The simplest trace formula in the world is this: for a square matrix , The left side is the sum of the eigenvalues of This is the ‘spectral side’ of the trace formula. The right side is the sum of the diagonal entries, the ‘geometric side’. They are equal, even though they are computed in different ways. ## Docker - Meet the New Docker: Built to Run on Trust (2026-11-19)

Meet the New Docker: Built to Run on Trust | Docker <!-- Start Adobe Target --> <iframe src="https://www.googletagmanager.com/ns.html?id=GTM-WL2QLG5" height="0" width="0" style="display:none;visibility:hidden"> Skip to content Products Product Sandboxes New Isolated environments for coding agents AI Governance New Govern agents and Claws across every team Hardened Images (DHI) Ship with secure, enterprise-ready images MCP Enterprise Gateway New Connect and manage MCP tools Hub Discover and share container images Desktop Containerize your applications Offload Run your Docker workloads in the cloud Solutions Support Developers Documentation Find guides for Docker products Getting Started Learn the Docker basics Resources Search a library of helpful materials Training Skill up your Docker knowledge Community Extensions SDK Create and share your own extensions Community Connect with other Docker developers Open Source Explore open source projects Preview Program Help shape the future of Docker Customers Customer Stories Get inspired with customer stories Onboarding Get started with Docker Onboarding Newsletter Get the latest Docker news Pricing Blog Docs Search Sign in Contact sales Toggle menu Meet the New Docker: Built to Run on Trust Posted Nov 19, 2026 Terri Avnaim Evolving an iconic brand for the agentic era Today, Docker is officially launching its new brand, and I’m excited to tell you what it’s all about and why we think now is the time for change. Let’s start with the big picture. We’re living through a transformative moment: for Docker, for developers, for the industry, and quite possibly, for humanity itself. AI is creating possibilities that would have sounded like science fiction not long ago. But it’s also forcing the industry to confront an almost existential question: how do we harness all that power and intelligence without compromising security, governance, or control? Or, put more simply: how do we get to a point where we can actually trust agents ? That one seemingly simple yet infinitely complex question is why we’re here today, introducing you to a new Docker. A new Docker, but a familiar story. We’ve been here before Ok, not exactly here, but once upon a time, a decade or so ago, the tech industry was at a similar inflection point. Digital transformation was the term du jour, promising a new way of engaging customers and powering the global economy. There was one big constraint, though: teams needed a better way to build, ship, and run software consistently across any environment. Enter a company you might have heard of: Docker. Together with all of you, Docker built the standard trusted by millions of developers . For over a decade, developers have relied on us to make software portable, open, reproducible, and secure. Docker turned the frustration of “Well, it works on my machine!” into the assurance that “Yes, it works everywhere.” Today, of course, the challenge confronting us is new and evolving, and we all need to evolve with it. We cannot simply continue to do what we’ve always done and expect to succeed in this new era. That applies to business leaders, developers, tech companies, everyone. It certainly applies to Docker. That’s what this rebrand is about: evolving to meet the moment for our customers. What isn’t changing Let me be unequivocal: this is not about leaving Docker’s legacy or heritage behind. We’ve spent more than a decade earning your trust. This is about building on that foundation, not replacing it. The qualities you value us for aren’t changing. We’re still committed to openness and industry collaboration. We’re still, and always will be, developer-first. We’re still fanatical about simplicity, and about showing up everywhere you need us. On the product front, the containers you know and love aren’t going anywhere. Containers still play a huge role in the software development lifecycle. As long as that’s the case, Docker will continue leading the way. Containers weren’t designed for agents Agents have introduced a new problem. Containers isolate workloads. But agents aren’t workloads . Agents are actors. Agents make their own decisions about what to run, what to access, and what to change. Developers and enterprises need solutions built specifically to isolate and govern them, so they can give those agents real autonomy without giving up control. Docker’s next chapter is about delivering exactly that: security, isolation, and governance, so we can all trust agents to do meaningful work without feeling like we’re always one step from catastrophe. ## GPUPartner - A Beginner's Guide to Local AI with Inkling, Qwen, and Unsloth (2026-11-23)

A beginner's guide to Local AI with Inkling, Qwen, and Unsloth | GPU Partner Skip to content GPU partner Inventory ▾ Systems Memory Storage GPU Cloud Compute View all inventory → Resources ▾ Blog Announcements VRAM calculator Reference Workloads Contact About Build a system Reach a human Build a system Reach a human Home / Blog A beginner's guide to Local AI with Inkling, Qwen, and Unsloth We have AI at home! Set up your own free personal ChatGPT on your PC with Unsloth and Inkling or Qwen. It's private, and the whole house can use it. By Jerry James · Nov 22, 2026 With the launch of open-weight models like Qwen, cloud-based LLM providers seem like they're more of infrastructure or appliance businesses when it comes to most routine tasks that an AI model can handle. OpenAI and Anthropic have displayed a pattern of dropping model quality in the 'latest' version and controlling exactly what you're allowed to run on their models. Add to this ballooning costs and privacy concerns, and we get a pretty good case for moving away from this 'convenient' cloud experience. That's basically where I was too. I wanted to offload certain token-heavy research, data collection, or coding tasks to an AI model without staring at usage limits all day. So, as any reasonable human being would do, I decided to set up my own local AI : a Claude and ChatGPT replacement that runs at home on my own hardware. Since this was my first attempt, my primary focus was keeping it simple and hosting it on Windows. If you're a beginner, you can follow along to get one going on your home network too. Once you have the hardware sorted, it takes about 10-15 minutes (excluding download times). The short version Install Unsloth Desktop, download a quantized AI model that fits your GPU's VRAM, set your context size, and turn on LAN access so everyone at home can use it. What Do You Need to Run a Local AI at Home? Not much (other than the hardware). Here's a short list: A Windows PC with a graphics card (GPU). The more video memory (VRAM) it has, the bigger the model it can run. I'm using an RTX 5090 with 32GB VRAM for this setup. Unsloth Desktop. A free app that 'serves' the AI model to you through a chat interface. Get it from Unsloth . An AI model sized to fit your GPU. We'll pick one in the next section. That's it. No terminal commands, Python environments, or subscriptions required. Which AI Model Can Your GPU Run? It depends on how much VRAM your graphics card has. As a rough guide, 8-12GB of VRAM handles smaller 9B-12B models, while 32GB comfortably runs 27B-35B models with plenty of room for long conversations. On consumer-grade hardware, we'll need smaller models. The full-size Qwen model sits at around 56GB and won't really fit on a consumer graphics card unless you're running a Mac with unified memory. That's where quantization comes in. What Is Quantization? Quantization shrinks a model by compressing weights and moving to a lower precision. You basically trade some accuracy for a model that actually fits on your GPU and runs faster. The same photo stored at 8, 4 and 2 bits per pixel. A model's weights lose detail the same way going from Q8 to Q4 to Q2. If you're wondering whether this tradeoff is worth it, well, it depends. You'll find that certain types of workloads don't really care and give similar outputs, while others may be nearly unusable. Which Model Should You Run for Your VRAM? For this guide, we'll be going with Qwen3.8-27B with 4-bit quantization on my system with an RTX 5090. Going up a tier is an option thanks to its 32GB of VRAM, but I wanted to allow for a larger context window (how much of the conversation the model can keep in mind at once) rather than cramming in the largest model that fits. Qwen3.8-27B at each precision with a 128K context, from our VRAM calculator . At 4-bit, the model and the context fit a 32 GB card with room to spare. If you want to run a model of this caliber with excellent performance, a hilariously overpriced RTX

Related articles Security Sep 10, 2026 Detecting and countering misuse of AI: September 2026 View more Each year, Anthropic updates its Usage Policy in response to the evolving capabilities of our models, and the feedback we’ve received from our customers. We're publishing a new version of the policy today. In this post, we summarize the changes we’ve made. Most of the updates in the latest version are intended to clarify existing rules. In the year since our last refresh, Claude has taken on longer, more independent work. This update provides new examples that show how our rules apply to Claude’s new capabilities. It draws on new patterns of misuse in influence operations, weapons development, and surveillance that we’ve observed in the past year (as we’ve documented in our latest threat intelligence report ). We’ve also made updates to clarify our requirements for high-risk use cases in areas like health and finance, to add controls for when Claude is used to autonomously take physical actions, and to address abusive behavior toward our models. The updated policy takes effect on November 12. The full Usage Policy and Supported Regions Policy can be found on their respective pages. A new section on deceptive activity As we wrote about last month , we’ve seen examples of state media outlets, government propaganda offices, and commercial firms using Claude to run networks of fake accounts and fabricated news sites. Our policy already prohibited this activity, but the restrictions were scattered across our elections, fraud, privacy, and disinformation sections. We've consolidated those rules into a new section titled Do Not Engage in Deceptive Campaigns or Artificial Activity, which applies to deceptive activity of any kind (whether political or commercial). It covers efforts to obscure who is behind a message or amplify content through fake accounts or posts, along with building the tools and infrastructure for running influence operation campaigns. Refocusing our elections section We’ve refined our political content restrictions in each version of our Usage Policy in order to ensure that our models can be used appropriately in democratic processes. Related articles Frontier Red Team Oct 27, 2026 Discovering and exploiting vulnerabilities in critical, real-world software with Claude View more We’re launching a new, expanded version of our Cyber Verification Program (CVP), which makes advanced cyber capabilities and reduced blocking classifiers available to qualifying security professionals. The program now consists of three access tiers, which allow security teams to apply for the level of access that best suits their work. Each tier includes access to our most capable models, including Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5, Claude Mythos 5.1, and new models moving forward. Interested customers can apply here . Cybersecurity is inherently dual use: the same capabilities that enable a security team to find and fix a vulnerability can also help a malicious actor exploit it. For this reason, our generally available models, such as Claude Opus 5.5 , Claude Fable 5.1 , and Claude Sonnet 5.5 , have conservative cyber safeguards that block most cyber work. This is intended to limit the harmful activities malicious actors can carry out using our models, while we continue to work to reduce false positives for secure coding. But defenders also need access to the best tools and most powerful capabilities to secure their systems. For the past six months, we’ve enabled trusted access through two programs: Project Glasswing and the CVP . The former gave a group of organizations securing the most critical software access to Claude Mythos; the latter gave vetted security teams access to reduced safeguards on Claude Opus and Claude Sonnet models. Now, we’re integrating these programs into one expanded offering, designed to give more security organizations access to the capabilities they need to protect their systems. New access tiers The updated access tiers make specific model capabilities available to security professionals based on the scope of their cyber work. Each has different verification requirements and security controls. Defense Access is for defensive work, including security operations center and incident response tasks, reverse-engineering malware, and analyzing and validating vulnerabilities. Related articles Announcements Oct 28, 2026 AI for Science expands to foundations and research institutions View more Anthropic is deepening its support for American scientific research by committing $150 million over three years to the Genesis Mission , a federal initiative to accelerate scientific and technological discovery through AI. The funding will go toward making Claude available to more than 15 agencies that are part of the mission, including NASA, the National Institutes of Health, and the National Science Foundation. We first announced our partnership with the US Department of Energy (DOE) and the Genesis Mission last December . Since then, we have worked with DOE to bring Claude to scientists across the national laboratories. Today, we announced this new commitment as part of the Science: A New Golden Age Summit hosted by the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy in Washington, DC. Supporting American scientists We share the administration’s view, laid out in “ Science: A New Golden Age ,” that AI can have a profound and positive impact on scientific discovery, and we are glad to back its efforts to support American scientists. Researchers at federal agencies and national laboratories work on some of the world’s most difficult scientific problems. Through our commitment, we aim to help them use Claude to meaningfully advance their work. Over the next three years, we will: Provide Claude, Claude Code, and API credits to several hundred Genesis Mission research projects; Partner closely with agencies and national laboratories on the administration’s top science priorities, including fusion energy and quantum computing; and Provide training, onboarding, and technical support to scientists on Genesis Mission projects, and help agencies new to the Genesis Mission get their first projects running. Accelerating scientific research This commitment is part of our broader effort to support scientific research. Earlier this year we launched Claude Science , an AI workbench that integrates the tools and packages researchers most commonly use, and opened 10,000 free and discounted Claude seats for academic scientists. We also expanded our AI for Science program, which gives credits to researchers on high-impac

Related articles Science Nov 6, 2026 Teaching AI to build scientific models View more We’re highlighting 20 scientific foundations, institutions, and research groups from around the world that we’ve partnered with in an expansion of our AI for Science program , which gives free Claude credits to researchers working on high-impact scientific projects. We started the AI for Science program in May 2025 with the belief that AI could significantly speed up scientific progress. Our goal is to increase access to AI tools and resources to enable researchers to deepen and expand their own scientific work. (Over the last few months, we’ve also launched our discounted Team plan for scientists and have supported hundreds of projects across biology, medicine, physics, chemistry, climate science, and mathematics.) Scientists have told us that they’re working with Claude to generate hypotheses, design experiments, swiftly analyze complex data, and communicate findings. With scientists at the center of this transformation, the field is setting the questions and developing the research projects that will help us tackle some of humanity’s toughest problems. Over the past year, we’ve started to expand this work and partner with scientific organizations to build collaborative research projects. For instance, we partnered with HHMI Janelia Research Campus to develop the Model Hardware Standard (MHS), a shared specification that lets AI agents safely operate lab instruments. MHS is currently in research preview. We also support the Allen Institute , a leader in creating large open datasets and tools to advance research on the brain, cells, and the immune system. The institute is building teams of AI agents to analyze that data, turning months of manual analysis into hours. AI for Science began by supporting individual scientists. By partnering with institutions and foundations, we’re now able to reach more researchers across many fields.
